With concave conversion $\hat bq^{\xi_c}$, the required rate becomes $\xi_c\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$. The model implies that influence spending rises with share as $p^{\xi_v+(1+\eta)(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$, at least quadratically, which is testable (TESTS, Test B).

*Numerics (CS).* The size threshold at $f=0.05$ is 0.2236 per carrier and 0.0500 per decision, and 0.1717 and 0.0998 at $\xi_f=0.3$ and $0.7$. Two equal independent carriers give a quarter of the monopoly's cultivated conversion at $\xi_v=0$ and half at $\xi_v=1$; shares $(0.5,0.3,0.2)$ give 0.16 and 0.38 of the monopoly value. By log-log regression over $p\in[0.05,1]$, $q^\ast$, $pq^\ast$ and spending scale as $p^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$, $p^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ and $p^{\xi_v+(1+\eta)(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ at $\eta\in\{0.5,1,2\}$ and $\xi_v\in\{0,0.3,1\}$, to three decimals, and the exponents hold in 300 random settings; the splitting factor $k^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ is reproduced for $k\in\{2,5\}$ at $\eta\in\{0.5,2\}$. With $\alpha_{\rm ctl}=0.2$ the rate law's threshold is 0.1333 at $\eta=0.5$ and 0.0667 at $\eta=2$, and on either side the cultivated term grows or decays at the predicted $\pm0.0600$ and $\pm0.0300$. With $\eta=1$, $\alpha_{\rm ctl}=0.2$, no correction growth and $k=10$, simulation gives split delays of 23.00 and 11.50 against formula values 23.03 and 11.51. A dominant share of 0.6 with $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.09$ crosses one for all three elasticities, and with $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.11$ decays. With $f$ fixed at 0.3 the monopoly crosses at 10.75 and ten parts never do; with $f$ falling at 0.05, the parts begin to cultivate $2\ln10/0.05=92.1$ later than the monopoly.

*Numerics (FF).* A falling floor can end cultivation below the rate law: with $\eta=p=\chi\hat Y=c_{\rm ev}=a_{\rm ev}\hat B=1$, $A=0$, $\gamma=e^{-0.2t}$, $d=2e^{0.05t}$ and $f=0.49e^{-0.01t}$, $q^\ast=[0.5e^{-0.05t}-0.49e^{-0.01t}]_+e^{0.2t}$ reaches zero at $t=\ln(0.5/0.49)/0.04=0.505$ and stays there, so the certificate never fails although $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$ is below the rate law of 0.1: the first case of (3). In the CS setting with $\alpha_f=0.05$, $d_f$ is 1.200, 1.978 and 3.262 at $t=0$, 10 and 20. From $f_0=0.1$ ($d_f(0)/d_0=1.80$): at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.051$ cultivation ends at $t=588$, yet the certificate fails at $t=38.4$, with a peak row sum of $2\times10^{21}$, the transient of (3); at 0.06 it holds (peak 0.267), cultivation ending at $t=59$. At $\alpha_{\rm cor}=\alpha_f=0.05$ with cultivation at the start it fails, at $t=37.1$ from $f_0=0.1$ and at $t=58.0$ from $f_0=0.17$ ($d_f(0)/d_0=1.06$); from $f_0=0.3$ ($d_f(0)/d_0=0.60$), with nothing cultivated at the start, it never fails. An independent implementation predicts the eventual certificate by the three cases in 32 of 32 settings ($\alpha_f\in\{0.02,0.05\}$, eight values of $\alpha_{\rm cor}$, with and without cultivation at the start). The lump-sum threshold exponents computed by bisection on $p$, 0.2500, 0.5000, 0.3333 and 0.2500 at $(\xi_f,\xi_v,\eta)=(0,0,1),(1,1,1),(0,0,2),(0,1,0.5)$ in one implementation and 0.2500, 0.3333, 0.4000 and 0.2273 at $(0,0,1),(0,1,1),(0.5,0,2),(1,0.3,0.5)$ in another, equal $\eta/(2+2\eta-\xi_v-\eta\xi_f)$ in every setting. With $A=0.3$, $p=0.6$ and $\eta=1$, as $\gamma$ falls through $1$, $10^{-2}$, $10^{-4}$ and $10^{-6}$ the requirement $d^\ast$ is 0.529, 1.146, 1.199 and 1.200 with a per-unit floor $f=0.3$, levelling off at $\chi\hat Y/f=1.2$; 0.465, 4.65, 46.5 and 465 with a lump sum $F_q=0.3$; and 0.638, 4.80, 46.6 and 465 with no floor: the lump sum tracks the case without a floor.

### Capacity off its selected level

The rate law of Proposition 4.5(2) takes capacity at its selected level. Selection on capacity is itself a dynamic, and a joint model of capacity and the field shows where the quasi-static reading is conservative and where it is optimistic.

**Proposition S4.2 (The rate law off the selected level) [thm; num].** *Take the evaluation domain of (10) with row sum (12), $[H_\gamma]$, $f=0$ and $d=d_0e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$ with $\alpha_{\rm cor}>0$, and let capacity $q$ adjust toward its selected level $q^\ast(t)$, which grows at $g_q=(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$.*
1. *(Sufficiency under any adjustment.) If capacity starts at or below $q^\ast$ and does not rise while above it, the sufficient half of Proposition 4.5(2) holds whatever the speed of adjustment; while $q^\ast$ rises such capacity stays below it, so the quasi-static failure date is the earliest [thm].*
2. *(Necessity needs tracking.) Below the rate law the certificate fails beyond a finite time whenever $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$ [thm]. (a) Under adjustment at a constant rate $\kappa_q$, $\dot q=\kappa_q(q^\ast-q)$, $q/q^\ast\to\kappa_q/(\kappa_q+g_q)$ and failure is delayed, asymptotically, by $\eta\ln(1+g_q/\kappa_q)/(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})$ [thm]. (b) Under gradual selection, $\dot q=V_q(q)\,\partial_q\Delta\pi$ with a selection variance $V_q(q)>0$, $\int_{q(0)}^{q(t)}dq/V_q(q)\le\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})$ at every date. Where that integral diverges as $q\to\infty$, as for constant variance or variance proportional to $q$, capacity is bounded and the certificate holds from some date on for every $\alpha_{\rm cor}>0$, after a possible transient failure [thm]. (c) Where the variance rises faster than capacity, as for variance proportional to $q^2$, capacity can grow without bound only if $\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})$ exceeds $\int_{q(0)}^\infty dq/V_q(q)$; fast selection then tracks the selected level and fails as the quasi-static model does, while slow selection stays bounded below the rate law [num].*
3. *(Reversal.) In a captured domain, a reform that raises $d$ for a hold and is then withdrawn must be held longer when capacity lags its selected level than the quasi-static model says: 6 to 14 per cent longer at the parameters computed, 17 to 54 per cent where the domain's conversion comes from its own share, and more as adjustment slows relative to $\varepsilon d$ [num].*

*Proof.* (1) is the sufficiency argument of Proposition 4.5(2); while $q^\ast$ rises and $q(0)\le q^\ast(0)$, such capacity satisfies $q\le q^\ast$, so $G_{\rm ev}\le G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}$ at every date. (2) The first claim is the necessity argument. (a) The linear equation has the particular solution $q=\kappa_qq^\ast/(\kappa_q+g_q)$, to which every solution converges; it scales the cultivated term by $\kappa_q/(\kappa_q+g_q)$, which shifts its exponential by $\ln(1+g_q/\kappa_q)$ at rate $(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$. (b) With $f=0$, $\partial_q\Delta\pi=\chi\hat Y/d-\gamma p^{\xi_v}q^\eta\le\chi\hat Y/d(t)$, so $\frac{d}{dt}\int_{q(0)}^{q(t)}dq/V_q\le\chi\hat Ye^{-\alpha_{\rm cor}t}/d_0$, and integrating gives the bound. For constant variance it reads $q(t)\le q(0)+V_q\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})$; for variance $v_qq$, $q(t)\le q(0)\exp\big(v_q\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})\big)$. Bounded capacity gives $G_{\rm ev}\le(A+a_{\rm ev}\hat Bq_{\max})/(c_{\rm ev}d)\to0$. (c) The bound is the same; near its selected level capacity relaxes at rate $V_q(q^\ast)\,\eta\gamma p^{\xi_v}q^{\ast\,\eta-1}=V_q(q^\ast)\,\eta\chi\hat Y/(dq^\ast)$, which for $V_q\propto q^2$ is proportional to $q^\ast/d$ and grows at $(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$ below the rate law, so capacity that reaches its selected level stays with it; whether it reaches it is the numerical part. (3) is numerical. $\square$

*Reading.* Capacity that must keep leaping toward an optimum running away from it is closer to strategic choice than to gradual selection; the necessary half describes carriers that optimize, the case Section 4.3 designs a requirement against. TESTS Study 2's predicted failure signature changes accordingly: slow adaptation of capacity delays failure below the rate law and does not produce failure above it; a certificate that fails at a correction growth rate slightly above the rate law points to a slow variable that erodes correction itself, the case of Proposition 4.6.

*Numerics (QS).* In the CS setting ($\chi=\bar y^\top\hat A\bar y=\eta=1$, $\xi_v=0$, $p=0.6$, $A=0.3$, $a_{\rm ev}\hat B=c_{\rm ev}=1$, $d=2e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$, $\gamma=e^{-0.2t}$, rate law 0.1; evaluation domain with $\zeta=1$, $\varepsilon=0.5$, $x_{\rm ev}(0)=0.01$), in two implementations that agree to within 0.1, the dates of certificate failure and of capture ($x_{\rm ev}>\tfrac12$) at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$ are 28.8 and 44.9 with capacity at its selected level, 38.1 and 56.0 under adjustment at $\kappa_q=0.1$, 46.9 and 66.3 at $\kappa_q=0.03$, 28.9 and 45.0 under selection with variance proportional to $q^2$ (scaled to its starting level, speed 1), and never under constant-variance selection ($V_q=1$); failure at $\kappa_q=0.3$ comes at 32.9. At $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.09$ the pairs are 145.9/221.6, 183.0/267.7 at $\kappa_q=0.1$, 223.0 and no capture within 300 at $\kappa_q=0.03$, 146.2/222.0, and never; at 0.10 and above no dynamic fails or is captured. With correction not growing, the pair is 13.8/23.5 with capacity at its selected level, and capture follows failure by 8 to 60 periods across the dynamics. Simulated delays against the formula of (2)(a): 4.11/4.05, 9.29/9.16 and 18.11/17.92 at $\kappa_q=0.3,0.1,0.03$ for $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$; 8.40/8.41, 19.70/19.71 and 40.23/40.24 for 0.08. Along the path at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$, $q/q^\ast$ settles at 0.87, 0.40 and 0.17 for $\kappa_q=1,0.1,0.03$, as $\kappa_q/(\kappa_q+g_q)$ with $g_q=0.15$ predicts, and falls toward zero under constant-variance selection. The bound of (2)(b) at $V_q=1$: $q(600)=7.29\le9.18$ at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.02$ and $2.18\le3.78$ at $0.05$, with peak row sums 0.51 and 0.20. Within a horizon $H$ of 150, 300 and 600, the largest $\alpha_{\rm cor}$ at which evaluation is captured is 0.085, 0.093 and 0.096 with capacity at its selected level, approaching the rate law from below; 0.079, 0.090 and 0.095 at $\kappa_q=0.03$; 0.009 under constant-variance selection at $V_q=1$ and 0 at $V_q=0.1$; and 0.063 at every horizon under variance proportional to $q^2$ at speed 0.1, slow selection staying bounded below the rate law as (2)(c) says. A third implementation with its own parameters ($p=1$, $A=0$, $d_0=2$) agrees in every direction: at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$ failure and capture at 13.0/25.4 with capacity at its selected level, 22.5/36.9 and 31.3/47.5 at $\kappa_q=0.1$ and 0.03, never under constant variance, and 23.8/58.9 under variance proportional to $q$, whose capacity then stays bounded, as (2)(b) says, and the certificate holds again by the end (final row sum $9\times10^{-8}$); at 0.09, 69.3/118.8, 106.4/169.9, 146.3/221.7, never and never; nothing fails at 0.10 or 0.12. Its lag delays are 9.42/9.16, 18.23/17.92 and 6.33/6.44 (simulated/formula), and under constant variance at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.01$ the certificate fails transiently at $t=10.6$ and recovers to a row sum of $5\times10^{-8}$. A fourth, with variance proportional to $q^2$ at speeds 100, 1, 0.1 and 0.01, gives failure at 69.3 (the quasi-static date), 75.3, and none within 250 periods for the two slowest, at $\eta=1$ and $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.09$; at $\eta=2$ and $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.057$, at 69.1 (the quasi-static date), 71.6 and none. Across these runs capture follows the certificate's failure by 8 to 85 periods, longer as correction approaches the rate law. Five independent implementations agree in direction, and none fails earlier than capacity at its selected level. *Numerics for (3).* From a captured evaluation domain at $d_1=0.5$ (row sum 1.46), raising $d$ to $d_2$ for a hold and withdrawing it: the minimal hold is 94.8 with capacity at its selected level against 107.7 at $\kappa_q=0.03$ (14% longer) for $d_2=1.0$; 354 against 399 for $d_2=0.7$; 55.9 against 59.5 for $d_2=2.0$; adjustment at $\kappa_q\ge0.1$ changes the hold by under 4%. In a self-sustaining domain whose conversion comes from its own share, the hold is 38.05 with capacity at its selected level, 44.34 at $\kappa_q=0.1$ (17% longer) and 58.67 at $\kappa_q=0.03$ (54% longer).

### Proof of Proposition 4.6, and the hysteresis of release

*(1).* With $\mathcal M(t)=\mathcal M_0e^{-\delta_ht}$, $d_{\rm eff}(t)=d_0e^{(\alpha_{\rm cor}-w\delta_h)t}$. Carriers select capacity against the correction that acts, and the certificate is computed with it, so Proposition 4.5(2) applies to $d_{\rm eff}$ with growth rate $\alpha_{\rm cor}-w\delta_h$, and both halves hold with the threshold raised by $w\delta_h$; the start condition is unchanged because $\mathcal M(0)=\mathcal M_0$. Where delegation is partial, a share $D_h(t)\in[0,1]$ of corrective work, and capacity recovers at $\varrho_h$ where work is not delegated, $\dot{\mathcal M}=-\delta_hD_h\mathcal M+\varrho_h(1-D_h)(1-\mathcal M)$, $\mathcal M$ declines at a rate of at most $\delta_h$, so the shift is at most $w\delta_h$, approached as $D_h\to1$. If erosion stops at $\mathcal M_{\min}>0$, $d_{\rm eff}$ tends to $d(\mathcal M_{\min}/\mathcal M_0)^w$: the rate condition is unchanged, and the level $d$ must exceed the requirement by the factor $(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})^w$. $\square$

*(2), the mechanism.* Captured evaluation reports that delegation is safe; delegation rises; human capacity erodes; correction weakens; capture deepens. This is a second positive loop, through human capacity, beside the conversion loop of (10), and it makes capture bistable over a band of correction rates: a domain that is not captured stays uncaptured at rates at which a captured one stays captured.

*Numerics (QS).* With $w=1$ and pure erosion in the third implementation above, the eventual certificate holds at $\alpha_{\rm cor}\in\{0.15,0.2,0.22\}$ and fails at $\{0.1,0.12\}$ for $\delta_h=0.05$, and holds at $\{0.2,0.22\}$ and fails at $\{0.1,0.12,0.15\}$ for $\delta_h=0.1$: the predicted thresholds 0.15 and 0.20. With partial delegation rising as $D_h(t)=1-0.8e^{-0.1t}$, $w=1$ and the CS parameters, the largest $\alpha_{\rm cor}$ at which evaluation is still captured by $t=600$ rises from 0.096 without the coupling to 0.145, 0.190 and 0.231 at $(\delta_h,\varrho_h)=(0.05,0.05)$, $(0.1,0.05)$ and $(0.2,0.1)$: near $0.1+\delta_h$, and below it within a finite horizon and where capacity recovers while not delegated. For (2), in one domain with a constant cost of control and capacity at its selected level, so as to isolate the loop through $\mathcal M$, with $\delta_h=0.2$, $\varrho_h=0.05$, $w=\tfrac12$ and delegation $D_h=0.1+k_Dx_{\rm ev}$: the smallest permanent correction rate that undoes capture from the captured state is 0.634 at $k_D=0$, matching the 0.638 above which the certificate holds without the loop; 1.006 at $k_D=0.3$; and 1.675 at $k_D=0.6$, where captured human capacity is 0.40 and 0.14 of its uncaptured level. These are 1.6 and 2.6 times the certificate rate. The form $d_{\rm eff}=d(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$ is a modelling choice inside the open interface "Human capacity and correction" (S13).

### Mobilization (Remark 4.7)

Let those harmed mobilize in proportion to perceived harm, $d=d_0+k_B(1-\tilde V)$. Since $1-\tilde V=(1-m)(1-V)$, masking attenuates the feedback by $1-m$ exactly when it is needed, and even with accurate reports $d\le d_0+k_B$, which cannot keep a certificate against a requirement that rises without bound. After a recovery, mobilization falls back to $d_0$ and the certificate can fail again: a regulatory cycle. With a per-unit floor that AI does not lower, enough gain and speed can keep the certificate over a stated interval, since the requirement then levels off (Proposition 4.5(3)).

### Proof of Proposition 4.8, and the late alarm

$\tilde V$ increases in $V$ and equals $V_{\rm alarm}$ at $V=(V_{\rm alarm}-m)/(1-m)$. For $V_{\min}\le V\le V_{\rm alarm}$ and $m>m_{\rm fl}$, $\tilde V\ge V_{\min}+m(1-V_{\min})>V_{\rm alarm}$. At $V^\ast$, $\tilde V\ge V^\ast+\bar m(1-V^\ast)>V_{\rm alarm}$. $\square$ If $V\to0$ and eventually $m\le m_1<V_{\rm alarm}$, the alarm fires. If $V$ declines from $V_{\rm alarm}$ as $\dot V=-k_VV$ with constant $m<V_{\rm alarm}$, the alarm is delayed by $k_V^{-1}\ln[(1-m)V_{\rm alarm}/(V_{\rm alarm}-m)]$, which diverges as $m\uparrow V_{\rm alarm}$.

### Remark 4.9 in full

(1) With an upper bound $m_{\rm in}$ known, an alarm on reports at $V_{\rm target}+m_{\rm in}(1-V_{\rm target})$ fires no later than the true condition reaches $V_{\rm target}$, for every $m\le m_{\rm in}$. (2) A bound estimated inside the evaluated lineage is itself masked. (3) For masking by the evaluated model, a bound estimated by evaluators independent in funding and appointment is still a reading of the model's behaviour, which the model can shape (evaluation awareness, sandbagging); the bound must come from evidence that behaviour does not control: weights and activations, which linear probes read for strategic deception (Goldowsky-Dill et al. 2025); audits of training data; and elicitation by fine-tuning, which recovers capability that a password-locked model withholds under prompting (Greenblatt, Roger, Krasheninnikov and Krueger 2024). (4) Where models evaluate models, through AI feedback, model-written evaluations and models as judges or monitors, $x_{\rm ev}$ is the share of that evaluation done by the evaluated lineage and can be read from who built the evaluator (EV4, EV6); checks of the same lineage add a common cause of their own (Section 6.5). (5) A disjunctive alarm, firing on any credible report, fails silently only if $m_{\rm out}>m_{\rm fl}$. (6) For static, memoryless affine masking $\tilde V=m+(1-m)V$, the correlation coefficient of reports is that of $V$, exactly. *Numerics (AC):* on a stochastic logistic host with load 0.4 and true condition 0.36, masking at $m=0,0.25,0.5$ gives mean reports 0.358, 0.519 and 0.679 with the same lag-one autocorrelation, 0.9814, from which the condition recovered is 0.376; a report filter with memory (exponential smoothing at 0.8) drives the recovered value to 0.042, and the indicator fails. It also needs long series, and once used as an alarm it becomes a target; faking dynamics is nevertheless costlier than faking levels, and the indicator can be cross-checked against channels that observe actions directly.

### The field model

**Lemma S4.3 (The field is cooperative) [thm].** *With $N_D$ domains, $[0,1]^{N_D}\times[0,\infty)^{N_D}$ is forward invariant under (10), $\limsup_tz_i\le\sum_jb_{ij}/d_i$, and the system is cooperative in the sense of Smith (1995), so its flow preserves the componentwise order (Hirsch 1985).*

*Proof of Proposition 4.10.* (1) At $Z_{\mathcal C}$ the Jacobian is block lower-triangular, with diagonal entries $\mp\zeta_ic_i(G_i(\mathcal C)-1)$ and $-\varepsilon_id_i$. (2) By the lemma, eventually $a_iz_i-c_i\le c_i(\sum_jG_{ij}-1)/2<0$. (3) Take $G_{i1}>1$ for $i\ne1$, $G_{12}>1$, and other off-diagonal gains zero. (4) Pruning, by comparison. $\square$ Part (2) is sufficient and not necessary: with a row sum of two and a start at $(0.1,0.1)$ the state stays in $[0,0.1]\times[0,0.2]$ and converges to no capture (FA; S14).

*Proof of Proposition 4.11.* Comparison and pruning; the re-seeding case is the cascade from the domains that remain captured. **Cascades.** If $x_j\to1$ on a seed, $x_i\to1$ on its cascade hull, the closure of $\mathcal S_{k+1}=\mathcal S_k\cup\{i:x_i(0)>0,\ G_i(\mathcal S_k)>1\}$ (Kempe, Kleinberg and Tardos 2003; Morris 2000), by induction using a lower bound on rule support.

*The keystone example.* In the first version of this model, a liability reform at development unwound capture in every domain while development was a keystone; once the other domains' mutual conversion rose to 1.1, capture survived the complete decoupling of development.

*Proof of Proposition 4.12.* The frozen share drives $z\to b_px_p/d$. The equilibria of the released system are $(0,0)$, $(1,b/d)$ and the saddle $(1/G,c/a)$. The function $axz-cx-\frac{ad}{2b}z^2$ is nondecreasing along solutions, so every solution converges to an equilibrium and the stable manifold of the saddle separates the basins. With ordinary rule-making the release point $(x_p,bx_p/d)$ lies on the nullcline through the saddle, which decides by $x_p$ against $1/G$; with rule-making insulated the release point is $(x_p,0)$, and $x_\sigma>1/G$ by strong cooperativity. $\square$ *The length of an insulated pause.* By cooperativity the basin of absence is a down-set, bounded by the separatrix. With rule-making insulated, rule support decays from its captured level $b/d$ as $(b/d)e^{-\varepsilon dt}$ while the share stays at $x_p$; at release the state is in the basin of absence iff it lies below the separatrix, whose height at $x_p$ is $S(x_p)$ in units of $c/a$. In those units the captured level is $G$, so the pause must last $\ln\big(G/S(x_p)\big)/(\varepsilon d)$: zero where $S(x_p)\ge G$, $\ln G/(\varepsilon d)$ at $x_p=1/G$, where $S=1$, and no finite length where $x_p\ge x_\sigma$. A frozen share accumulates no lock-in; the hold of a reform that insulates rule-making while shares keep moving is S11's. *Numerics (DW):* by forward integration in logit coordinates, $x_\sigma$ is 0.676 when rule-making is as fast as adoption with $G=2$ and 0.984 when it is ten times slower; 0.321 and 0.775 at $G=5$; a second method, integrating the separatrix backward, agrees. In units of $1/(\varepsilon d)$, with $G=2$ and equal speeds, $S=3.92$, 2.15, 1.00 and 0.45 at $x_p=0.1$, 0.3, 0.5 and 0.6, so the pause is zero from $x_p=0.1$ and $0.3$, 0.69 from 0.5 and 1.50 from 0.6, and no finite length from 0.676; with rule-making ten times slower it is 0.24, 0.48, 0.69, 0.81, 1.17 and 2.08 at $x_p=0.1$, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6, 0.8 and 0.95; with $G=5$ and equal speeds, 0.82 from $x_p=0.1$, $1.61=\ln5$ from 0.2, 3.45 from 0.3, and no finite length from 0.321. This result also appears as Proposition 10 of the author's paper of 28 September 2026 (LEDGER records the overlap; attribution is the author's to settle).

*What the field model cannot show.* It is cooperative, so it has no attracting cycles and describes lock-in, not reform cycles; a delayed, masked negative loop from the host to correction could produce cycles and is not modelled. The mobilization of Remark 4.7 is one such loop, and a delayed balancing channel is treated in S2. *Recovering the parameters.* Integrating (10) gives $\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{logit}x_i=\zeta_ic_i[\sum_jG_{ij}(k_i*x_j)(t)-1]$ plus a decaying transient, with kernel $k_i(s)=\varepsilon_id_ie^{-\varepsilon_id_is}$; series for the shares identify the conversions. This is recoverability, not causal identification.

### Proof of Proposition 4.13, and the two clocks

By (a) and Proposition 3.4, on every alarm-free path the load tends to $\bar e$ and $\limsup_tV\le(1-h_V\bar e/\rho)^+<V_{\min}$ under (reach). By (e), $m(t)>m_{\rm fl}$ throughout the band $[V_{\min},V_{\rm alarm}]$, so by Proposition 4.8 no alarm fires there, and the host passes below $V_{\min}$ on an alarm-free path. If $m_{\rm in}\le m_{\rm fl}$, then $m\le m_{\rm fl}$ and the alarm fires at some $V\ge V_{\min}$, later than without masking. *The clock form.* By (b), (c) and Proposition 4.5(2), with its part (3) where the floor falls, the certificate fails and $x_{\rm ev}\to1$, so after a finite date $T_{\rm cap}$ the share $x_{\rm ev}$ stays above the threshold of (e). If $T_{\rm cap}<T_A$, every date at which $V$ lies in the band is later than $T_{\rm cap}$, so (e) holds and the first part applies. $\square$ The load is at most $\bar e$, so by comparison the host declines no faster than under full load; when $h_V\bar e<\rho$ it cannot reach the alarm before $(\rho-h_V\bar e)^{-1}\ln[(1-V^\ast/V_0)/(1-V^\ast/V_{\rm alarm})]$, with $V^\ast=1-h_V\bar e/\rho<V_{\rm alarm}$, a lower bound on $T_A$; in every case $T_A$ is finite under (reach). Conditions (b) and (c) say nothing about $T_A$, which is why they make (e) a condition to check rather than a consequence.

*Numerics (SR).* Two computations show the clocks deciding. With capture set from outside: a host $\dot V=V(\rho(1-V)-h_V)$ under full race load from $V_0=1$, with $\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$, floor 0.3, alarm 0.6, $m_{\rm in}=0.5$ and $m_{\rm out}=0$, so $m_{\rm fl}=0.43$ and (e) needs $x_{\rm ev}>0.857$, crosses the floor at $t=21.0$. If evaluation is captured along a logistic of width 2 centred at $t=0$, the threshold is passed at $t=3.6$, no alarm fires and the crossing is silent; centred at $t=20$ or $t=45$, the threshold is passed at $t=23.6$ or $48.6$ and the alarm fires at $t=7.4$, before the floor, although $x_{\rm ev}\to1$ in every case: (b) and (c) without (e). With capture endogenous in the joint model (the CS parameters, a race share rising from 0.05 at rate 0.12, the same host, floor and alarm): with correction not growing, evaluation is captured near $t=23.5$ when capacity is at its selected level; independent evaluation raises the alarm at $t=32.1$ with $V=0.58$, while capacity at its selected level or adjusting at $\kappa_q=1$ and 0.1 delays the alarm to $t=58.8$ with $V=0.20$, after the floor; capacity adjusting at $\kappa_q=0.03$, or under constant-variance selection, leaves evaluation uncaptured when the alarm is due, and it fires at $t=32.1$. With correction growing at 0.05, half the rate law, capture comes too late under every dynamic and the alarm fires at $t=32.1$. Whether capture precedes evidence thus depends on the time constant of capacity as well as on the rate law, and acceleration of the contest moves both clocks.

## S5. Acceleration and the three erosions

This chapter holds the full statements of the results that Section 5 carries in a sentence, the proofs of those it states, and the numbers behind every figure it quotes. Local symbols: $w_p=\delta(1-p_d)/(1-\delta)$ is the weight of the future that a deviation leaves open; $A$, $B$ and $K$ are the stage constants of the binary game of Proposition 6.1, defined at Remark S5.1; $\tau_{\rm det}$ is the mean time to detect and answer a deviation. $\Lambda^\ast$ keeps its meaning, the gap factor of Remark S2.1.

### Proposition 5.1 in full, and proof

*(1) In the objective an actor acts on, if $S_i<P_i$, restraint is never a best response to a racing rival; in the contest of Proposition 3.6 this holds for $r<n/(n-1)$, and in the $n$-player game it also needs restraint against one racer to pay less than racing alongside it. (2) A best response in the objective does not imply $S<P$ in material payoff or true welfare: with $n=2$, $r=1$, $\Omega=c=1$, $\ell=0.1$ and $\theta=-1$, racing is the strict best response in the objective, while staying out gives $S^\pi=-0.05$ against $P^\pi=-0.10$, and with $\kappa=0.2$, $S^W=+0.04$ against $P^W=-0.12$. (3) If falling behind costs $L_b\ge0$ beyond losing the prize, joining instead of staying out gains, at $\theta=0$, $\frac{\Omega+L_b}{n}\big(1-\frac{r(n-1)}{n}\big)$ for $r\le n/(n-1)$, zero in the fully dissipative limit; with asymmetric costs $c_1<c_2$ the strongest keeps a rent $(\Omega+L_b)(1-c_1/c_2)$ (Hillman and Riley 1989; Baye, Kovenock and de Vries 1996). (4) If the events $T_i>R_i$ are independent with probabilities $\xi_i$, the race begins with probability $1-\prod_i(1-\xi_i)$, which tends to one iff $\sum_i\xi_i$ diverges.*

*Proof.* (2): at $\theta=-1$ intensity is $1/2$ and $P^\pi=-0.1$; against $e=1/2$ zero intensity gives $-0.05$ and the other $0.45$. (3): with penalty $L_b$ the contest has prize $\Omega+L_b$; joining minus staying out is $(\Omega+L_b)/n-(c+\ell)e$ with $e=r(n-1)(\Omega+L_b)/(n^2(c+\ell))$; in the symmetric all-pay contest each player's expected payoff is that of the lowest bid in its support, $-L_b$ (Baye, Kovenock and de Vries 1996), as for two actors with $r\ge2$ (Ewerhart 2015). (4): $\prod(1-\xi_i)\to0$ iff $\sum\xi_i=\infty$. $\square$

### Proof of Proposition 5.2

At the symmetric equilibrium the win probabilities sum to one, so total material payoff is $\Omega-ne(c+n\ell)$; substituting $e(\theta)$ from Proposition 3.6(1) gives $s_{\rm dis}$. With stakes $s\Omega$ and loss $s\ell$ the share is $r(n-1-\theta)(c+ns\ell)/(n(c+(1+\theta)s\ell))$, whose derivative in $s$ has the sign of $c\ell(n-1-\theta)>0$, with limit $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$. Total payoff stays positive at every scale iff the limit is at most one, $r(n-1-\theta)\le1+\theta$, that is, $\theta\ge\theta_g$. At $\theta=-1$ the share is $r(1+ns\ell/c)$. $\square$

For $n=4$ and $r=1$ the ceiling $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$ is 3, 1.86, 1 and 0.33 at $\theta=0$, 0.4, 1 and 2, and $\theta_g=1$; with $\ell=0.3c$ the share at unit scale is 1.27 at $\theta=0$ and 1.007 at $\theta=0.4$, and it rises toward each ceiling as the scale grows [num: legacy v5]. At $\theta=0$ each actor's loss relative to joint restraint is $\frac{r(n-1)}n\frac\Omega n\frac{c+n\ell}{c+\ell}$, which exceeds $\Omega/n$ iff $r(n-1)(c+n\ell)>n(c+\ell)$: $1.27\,\Omega/n$ in that example. If the shared loss per unit did not grow with power, the share would stay at its unit-scale value.

### Remark 5.3 in full

A unit of capacity can do one of three things. It can expand into an unclaimed frontier at marginal value $\varphi(F)-c_x-(1+\theta)H/n$, where $\varphi$ falls as the remaining frontier $F$ shrinks, $c_x$ is the private cost and $H$ the cost to the host, borne equally (Proposition 3.5(5)). It can destroy rivals' capacity at value $-c_y-\theta\Delta_y/(n-1)$, where $c_y$ is the private cost and $\Delta_y$ the capacity destroyed. Or it can rest. The symbols are local to this remark.
1. *If $\theta\ge-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$, war among copies never pays.*
2. *At $\theta=-1$ expansion stops only where $\varphi=c_x$; at $\theta>-1$ it stops at $\varphi=c_x+(1+\theta)H/n$, the point of enough.*
3. *At $\theta=-1$ expansion gives way to destruction once $\varphi(F)<c_x+\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y$, before the frontier is exhausted.*

*Proof.* Each clause compares the three marginal values. For $\theta\ge-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$ the value of destruction is not positive, which gives (1). At $\theta=-1$ the host term vanishes from the value of expansion, which gives (2); destruction is then worth $\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y$, and it is preferred to expansion once $\varphi-c_x$ falls below that, which gives (3). $\square$

*Toy.* Four copies of one design with compounding expansion, a frontier of 1,000 and a logistic host. At $\theta=-1$ expansion peaks at a stock of 494 near $t=25$; the switch comes with 32% of the frontier left (predicted by (3) at 322, simulated at 320), and total stock then falls to about $4\times10^{-9}$. At $\theta=0$ the copies stop at the point of enough, keep a stock of 518 and never fight; at $\theta=3$ they stop earlier, at 343. With asymmetric starts one survivor keeps 17% of the peak. With destruction too costly to pay, no accounting fights, but $\theta=-1$ drives the host to 0.109. One result runs against the narrative: in the run with war, the host's minimum under $\theta=-1$ (0.245) is higher than under $\theta=0$ (0.194), because capacity spent on war is not spent on the host (S14) [num: FS]. The parameters were chosen by the author; the toy illustrates the static condition and estimates nothing (compare Hirshleifer 1991; Skaperdas 1992).

The corpus reaches the switch from the other side: a logic organised as outward struggle does not stop when outward space runs out but turns its means on whatever is left, and in one of two endgames fighting goes on until everything within reach is eaten and then continues inside, as a zero-sum game [corpus: meta-attractor, 2026-10-01 and 2026-10-04].

### Proof of Proposition 5.4

Cooperation is worth $C_i/(1-\delta)$. A one-period deviation $a$ is worth $C_i+g_i(a)+\delta\big[p_d(a)D_i(a)/(1-\delta)+(1-p_d(a))v_i/(1-\delta)\big]$, where $v_i/(1-\delta)$ is the continuation value of punishment computed in the same game, with breakouts possible during punishment. Multiplying the no-deviation condition by $1-\delta$ and rearranging gives (14) for that deviation. In this stochastic game with an absorbing state, discounting and bounded payoffs, the one-shot deviation principle holds, and it holds deviation by deviation: (1) is the no-gain condition for each $a$ with its own $(g_i(a),p_d(a),D_i(a))$, which is why the deviation with the largest gain need not bind; when $p_d$ and $D_i$ are common to all deviations, the one with the largest gain binds. That $a^\ast$ is the path of some subgame-perfect equilibrium needs $v_i$ to be the least subgame-perfect continuation value, the extension of optimal penal codes (Abreu 1988) to stochastic games with an absorbing state; this is asserted for finite stage games with discounting and flagged for outside review.

(2) Solve (1) for $p_d$. With $\alpha=(1-\delta)g_i/\delta$, $b=D_i-C_i$ and $c=C_i-v_i$, $\bar p_{d,i}=(c-\alpha)/(b+c)$, where $b+c=D_i-v_i>0$. Its derivatives are $-1/(b+c)$ in $\alpha$, $-\bar p_{d,i}/(b+c)$ in $b$ and $(1-\bar p_{d,i})/(b+c)$ in $c$, so on $[0,1)$ it falls with $g_i$ and with $v_i$, falls with $D_i$ except at $\bar p_{d,i}=0$, where the derivative is zero, and rises with $C_i$, whose derivative is $1/(b+c)$. Scaling $(g_i,C_i,D_i,v_i)$ alike scales $\alpha$, $b$ and $c$ alike and leaves the ratio unchanged [num: NB; ES]. (3) The two bounds differ by $\delta p_d(D_i-C_i)$. (4) Set $p_d=1$; with $D_i\ge C_i$ the condition forces $g_i\le-\delta(D_i-C_i)/(1-\delta)\le0$. (5) With at most one breakout a period, the three events of a punishment period, $i$ breaks out, the other breaks out, neither, have probabilities $q_b$, $q_b$ and $1-2q_b$, which needs $q_b\le\frac12$; the punishment value satisfies $V_P=P_i+\delta\big[q_bD_i/(1-\delta)+q_bV^{\rm sub}_i/(1-\delta)+(1-2q_b)V_P\big]$, and $v_i=(1-\delta)V_P$ is the closed form. With independent breakouts each racer breaks out alone with probability $q_b(1-q_b)$, and the race continues with probability $q_b^2+(1-q_b)^2$, since a simultaneous breakout leaves it as it was; this gives the same closed form in $q_b(1-q_b)$, valid for every $q_b\in[0,1]$. And $v_i>P_i$ iff $D_i+V^{\rm sub}_i>2P_i$. In the example, restraint is enforceable at $p_d=0$ iff $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, that is $v_i\le0.7778$; with $v_i(q_b)=3.6q_b/(0.1+1.8q_b)$ this holds iff $q_b\le0.0354$, and $v_i<C_i$ iff $q_b<0.0556$. $\square$

*Numerics (ES; EC).*
- *Per deviation.* With $C=3$, $D=6$, $v=1$ and $\delta=0.9$, deviation X ($g=1$, $p_d=0.1$) is deterred, its value 17.5 against 30 for cooperation (temptation 0.37 against a remaining sanction of 1.62), while deviation Y ($g=0.2$, $p_d=0.6$) is not, 39.2 against 30 (temptation 1.64 against 0.72) [num: ES].
- *Breakouts during punishment.* The closed forms equal value iteration on the explicit chain, exclusive ($v=0.8950$, 1.2857 and 1.6875 at $q_b=0.045$, 0.1 and 0.3) and independent (0.8723, 1.2366 and 1.5816). At $q_b=0.6$ the exclusive form is invalid ($1-2q_b<0$) and the independent one gives 1.6241. The window of the example is not a knife edge: deterrence fails while $v<C$ on $[0.0498,0.0555]$, $[0.0354,0.0555]$ and $[0.0159,0.0555]$ for $g=0.5$, 2 and 5 on a grid of $q_b$, and racing in the punishment phase is a best response in the cases checked, so the punishment is credible [num: ES].
- *Best response over plans.* In 20,000 random settings of an explicit-action stochastic game in which one breakout technology sets both the deviation's $p_d$ and the punishment's $q_b$, player 1's optimum over all deviation plans is computed by value iteration on the decision process. Cooperation is sustained in 6,821. In 35 settings the punisher prefers to quit the race, so part (5) does not give the credible punishment value and the proposition's premise fails; in the other 19,965, (14), evaluated with $v$ from part (5), agrees with the best response in every case [num: ES].
- *Valuation check.* Comparing the discounted value of the fixed deviation path with cooperation in 200,000 random settings, a third each with $D<C$, $D=C$ and $D>C$, reproduces part (1) in every case. Since that comparison is (14) rearranged, as exact rational arithmetic confirms (no disagreement in 20,000 draws), it checks the algebra and not the incentives [identity: EC; ES]. The bound without the breakout term says "sustainable" when deviation pays in 10,723 of 66,538 cases with $D>C$ and in none with $D\le C$, and says "not sustainable" when restraint is sustainable in 10,032 of 66,842 cases with $D<C$. Both of Codex's V6.0 counterexamples are reproduced (cooperation worth 30, deviation 22) [identity: EC].
- *A different point.* With $C=1$, $D=4$, $V^{\rm sub}=0$, $g=0.5$ and $q_b=0.1$, the punishment value rises to 1.29, above $C$, and 1.78 at $q_b=0.45$; restraint is not sustainable even at $p_d=0$. There both actors prefer the race with breakouts to cooperation itself: what fails is the preference for cooperation, not deterrence, which is why the example of (5) replaces this one. Where $D+V^{\rm sub}<2P$ breakouts harden the punishment instead: with $D=3$ and $V^{\rm sub}=-5$, $v=-0.64$ at $q_b=0.1$ [num: EC; ES].
- *Homogeneity.* $\bar p_d=0.2963$ is unchanged when all of $(g,C,D,v)$ are scaled by 2 or 10; scaling only $g$ and $D$ moves it to 0.1296 and $-0.0037$ [num: ES].

### Derivation of Remark 5.5

Let detection and response arrive at a random time $\mathcal T$ after the deviation, with $\upsilon(T)=\Pr(\mathcal T\le T)$, and let the deviation become decisive at $T_{\rm dec}$ unless it has been answered. It escapes punishment iff $\mathcal T>T_{\rm dec}$, so $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$; substituting in (14) and solving as in Proposition 5.4(2) gives $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}$. A memoryless clock of mean $\tau_{\rm det}$ has $\upsilon(T)=1-e^{-T/\tau_{\rm det}}$. Along the compounding route $T_{\rm dec}$ is the time of Remark S2.1, which a structural loop gain above one shortens, so detection that is timely today stops being timely as leads open faster.

*The correspondence with Proposition 8.6.* Take its one-shot payoffs as continuation values normalized by $1-\delta$: $R=C_i$; $T=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta D_i$, the deviation that becomes decisive; $P-F=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta v_i$, the deviation detected and answered. Then $T-R=(1-\delta)g_i+\delta(D_i-C_i)$ and $T-(P-F)=\delta(D_i-v_i)$, so $(T-R)/(T-P+F)=1-\bar p_{d,i}$, and $T<R$ iff $\bar p_{d,i}>1$, the exemption of Corollary 5.7. Over 100,000 random settings with $D>v$ the two thresholds differ by at most $4.4\times10^{-11}$ [identity: JX].

*Where they differ.* Proposition 8.6 states when cooperating beats defecting against a cooperator, given the sanction $F$ and the one-shot payoffs; trust in the other's cooperation then decides whether cooperating is a best response, and the credibility of $F$ is not examined. (14) is the condition on the equilibrium path: the other restrains, the sanction is the credible continuation $v_i$ of a game in which breakouts remain possible, and the decisive position carries its own stakes through $\delta D_i$. Where the stage games coincide and trust is one, the two thresholds are one number; the difference matters where the sanction has to be enforced by the same actors it is meant to protect.

*Check of the clock.* Simulated escape probabilities under a memoryless clock equal $e^{-T_{\rm dec}/\tau_{\rm det}}$ within sampling error (0.4485 against 0.4493 at $T_{\rm dec}=8$, $\tau_{\rm det}=10$; 0.1349 against 0.1353 at $T_{\rm dec}=20$; 0.0818 against 0.0821 at $T_{\rm dec}=5$, $\tau_{\rm det}=2$), and simulated deviation values agree with (14): with $C=1$, $D=1.5$, $v=0$, $g=0.3$ and $\tau_{\rm det}=10$, deviating is worth 0.738 against 1 at $T_{\rm dec}=8$ and is deterred, and 1.235 at $T_{\rm dec}=2$ and is not [num: JX].

### Proof of Corollary 5.6

At $\theta=-1$ every symmetric profile gives each objective 0. *Two actors.* Let the punisher use any strategy $\sigma_2$, pure or mixed, conditioning on the history as it likes, and let the punished play its mirror, $\sigma_1(h)=\sigma_2(\mathrm{swap}(h))$: at every history, what the punisher would play with the roles swapped. The punishment phase is exchangeable and starts from a state that treats both alike, so the joint distribution of the two payoff streams is invariant under swapping the players and their expected objectives are equal; since the objectives sum to zero at $\theta=-1$, both are 0, and $v_1\ge0$. The one-period case is the antisymmetric matrix identity: the relative objective of a symmetric two-player game has $A=-A^\top$, and mirroring a mixed strategy $y$ gives $y^\top Ay=0$. *$n\ge3$.* In an anonymous game in which all punishers use the same, possibly mixed and independent, strategy, mirroring yields an exchangeable profile with expected relative payoff 0. In either case $v_i\ge0=C_i$, and (14) requires $(1-\delta)g_i+\delta p_dD_i\le\delta(1-p_d)(0-v_i)\le0$. *Where the premise fails.* A continuation that carries a label, such as an absorbing loss that can strike only the target, is not exchangeable: the mirror does not copy the punisher's continuation, and the bound fails. $\square$

*Numerics (CR; ES).* Over 20,000 random two-player games, mixed mirroring gives at most $2\times10^{-16}$ in absolute value; in 3,000 anonymous games with $n\ge3$, no violation [num: CR]. *The continuation premise.* An exchangeable continuation, in which a lottery awards an absorbing $\pm10$ with probability 0.3 a period, leaves the mirrored punished at a normalized relative value of $0.000000$. A label-dependent one, in which the target takes $-10$ with probability one half whatever it does, holds it at $-8.18$, so a deviation is worth $-69.6$ and relative actors sustain cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium (S14); the symmetric variant of the same lottery gives a punishment value of 0 and a deviation worth $+4$, so it does not [num: ES].

*The role-differentiated counterexample.* Three players choose (restrain or race) × (X or Y); a player whose second component is unique gains one, and racing gains 0.1 and costs each other 0.1. All restraining with X gives each 0, and the best deviation gains 1.2. The punishment in which the deviator and one punisher take X and the other punisher Y holds the deviator at $-0.5$ and is a stage equilibrium in objectives, so grim reversion to it is credible, and cooperation is sustained for $\delta\ge1.2/1.7=0.706$; a symmetric punishment could be mirrored for 0 [num: CR]. *Strictness.* As printed the punishment is weak: the best unilateral deviations from it gain 0, 0 and $-0.2$, so the two players in the X role are indifferent. Adding 0.02 to the prize for a unique X makes it strict; subtracting 0.02 destroys it, and the roles must swap, after which a perturbation of $-0.05$ gives a strict punishment with $\delta^\ast=0.716$. Under any small asymmetry between the roles' prizes the matching assignment is strict, and under random anonymous perturbations of all payoffs a strict punishment holding the deviator below cooperation exists in 300 of 300, 300 of 300 and 282 of 300 games at perturbation sizes 0.02, 0.05 and 0.1 (median $\delta^\ast$ 0.708, 0.711 and 0.713); a second scheme gives 400 of 400, 400 of 400 and 372 of 400 at 0.01, 0.05 and 0.1 (median about 0.71). With other stage parameters the deviator is still held at $-0.5$ ($\delta^\ast=0.730$) [num: CR]. *Indifference.* A purely relative punisher gains $+0.95$ from attacking the deviator and $+0.95$ from attacking its fellow punisher, against $+0.55$ from abstaining, and both punishers prefer the Y role ($+1.0$ against $-0.5$): the punishment needs coordination on roles, and which punisher takes which is equilibrium selection [num: CR]. Duersch, Oechssler and Schipper (2012) concern a dynamic imitation rule; the corollary uses only that a symmetric two-player zero-sum game has value 0, so rock–paper–scissors games are covered once mixed mirroring is allowed.

### Proof of Corollary 5.7

With $p_d=0$, (14) reads $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, satisfiable for $C_i>v_i$ and $\delta$ large, given a punishment that does not raise the chance of breakout (else $v_i$ rises, Proposition 5.4(5)); at $\theta=-1$ apply Corollary 5.6. The condition is linear in $p_d$: the temptation less the sanction is $(1-\delta)g_i-\delta(C_i-v_i)+\delta p_d(D_i-v_i)$. So it holds for every $p_d\in[0,1]$ iff it holds at both ends; where $D_i\ge v_i$ the end $p_d=1$, $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$, implies the other, and where $D_i>v_i$ it is $\bar p_{d,i}\ge1$. Otherwise the condition is $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$, and (a) and (b) classify the terms of its expression: (a) lowers $p_d$ directly, by removing the decisive class, by custody that no party can use alone, or by detection and response faster than breakout ($p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, Remark 5.5); (b) raises $\bar p_{d,i}$ through $D_i$ relative to $C_i$, through $v_i$, or through $g_i$. $\square$ With $C=1$, $D=0.9$, $v=0$, $g=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, $\bar p_d=0.8642$, and (14) holds at $p_d=0.86$ and fails at 0.87 and 0.9, so $D<C$ alone exempts nothing; with $g=0.5$, $(1-\delta)g=0.05\le\delta(C-D)=0.09$, and restraint holds at every $p_d$ [num: ES].

**Remark S5.1 (An illustration: the faithful-AI game at the boundary case) [thm; num].** In the binary game of Proposition 6.1 with a common $\theta$, write $A=\epsilon(b-h_d)-c$ for the material gain from racing alone, $B=\epsilon(b/(n-1)+h_d)$ for the average loss that racing imposes on each other actor, $K=c+n\epsilon h_d$ for each actor's loss when all race, against all restraining, and $w_p=\delta(1-p_d)/(1-\delta)$. Restraint is a stage equilibrium when $\theta\ge A/B$, and, at the boundary case $D=C$ with punishment by reversion to the race without breakouts, can be sustained when $\theta\ge\theta_{\rm rep}(p_d)=(A-w_pK)/(B+w_pK)$; equivalently, for a given working objective, restraint is enforceable iff $p_d\le\bar p_d(\theta)=1-(1-\delta)(A-\theta B)/\big(\delta K(1+\theta)\big)$, which rises with $\theta$ wherever $A>\theta B$. With $n=4$, $b=1$, $c=0.3$, $h_d=0.2$, $\epsilon=1$ and $\delta=0.9$: $A/B=0.94$, and $\theta_{\rm rep}$ is $-0.901$, $-0.812$, $-0.322$, $+0.005$, $+0.634$ and $+0.937$ at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99 and 1, from the closed form and from direct discounted streams, with racing credible at each [identity: BI; num: JX]. An isolated self ($\theta=0$) loses enforced restraint once $p_d>0.9495$, and a self at $\theta=0.4$ once $p_d>0.9793$ [num: JX]. These are the thresholds of the boundary case, not bounds: a decisive position worth more than cooperation raises them (with $D-C=0.5$ in objective units, $\theta_{\rm rep}$ at $p_d=0.9$ rises from $-0.32$ to $+2.34$, and at $p_d=1$ no $\theta\le n-1$ sustains restraint, Proposition 5.4(4)) [num: NB; JX]. In the continuous contest intensity is positive for every $\theta<n-1$, so self-enforcing restraint there needs full inclusion of the contestants.

*Test G.* Let $\theta$ be drawn uniformly on $[-1,3]$, and count the share of draws above 1 (wider) and below 1 (narrower) that sustain restraint; the restraint gap is the difference. At $D=C$ it rises with $p_d$: $+0.05$, $+0.09$, $+0.21$, $+0.34$, $+0.50$, $+0.82$ and $+0.97$ at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.8, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99 and 1. With $D-C=0.5$ it is hump-shaped: $+0.05$, $+0.30$, $+0.92$, $+0.33$, 0, 0 and 0 at the same values, because at $p_d=1$ the condition is $(1-\delta)g\le\delta(C-D)<0$, impossible for $g\ge0$, so the enforceable set is empty for every $\theta\le n-1$ and this theory and structural realism agree there. The registered wording: the gap grows with $p_d$ over the range in which the wider objective still sustains restraint; where a decisive position is worth more than cooperation it closes as $p_d\to1$; the test discriminates at intermediate $p_d$ [num: JX].

### Proof of Proposition 5.8

With linear payoffs the objective $(1-s)W+\theta sW/(n-1)$ has slope $W(\theta/(n-1)-1)\le0$ in the share $s$ given away, zero only at $\theta=n-1$; with logarithmic utility the first-order condition of $\ln((1-s)W)+\theta\ln(sW/(n-1))$ gives $s=\theta/(1+\theta)$, which a grid search confirms at $\theta=0.4$, 1, 2 and 3 (0.286, 0.500, 0.667, 0.750) [num: WG]. A winner acting on its true welfare toward what can be divided has $\theta=\kappa_W$ by Proposition 3.6(2), so it gives $\kappa_W/(1+\kappa_W)$; toward a positional prize its accurate weight is $\kappa_s\le0$, and it gives nothing. The promise is Proposition 3.5(4): a share promised to the $n-1$ others is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, and one split among all $n$ by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$ [num: SM]. $\square$

### Proposition 5.9 in full

*Two actors bargain over a flow of value 1 per period with discount $\delta$; fighting ends bargaining, A then obtaining the flow with its probability of prevailing less a cost $c_A$ per period; A prevails with probability $w_1$ today and $w_2<w_1$ after B's advantage arrives one period later. (1) Let $x$ be A's per-period share after the shift; fighting now, worth $(w_1-c_A)/(1-\delta)$, is rational if $w_1-c_A>(1-\delta)+\delta x$, and only if, when peace gives A the whole flow today. (2) Under (A8), the advantaged side setting the terms, B offers A its reservation value $x=\max(w_2-c_A,0)$. (3) Under other protocols $x$ can reach $\min(w_2+c_B,1)$, and attack can cease to be rational. (4) If $c_A\ge w_1$, war is worth at most zero. A shift spread over enough periods removes the motive.*

*Proof and numbers.* (1) and (2) compare the two discounted streams. (3): with $w_1=0.5$, $w_2=0$, $c_A=c_B=0.2$ and $\delta=0.8$, A attacks under (A8), while if A sets the future terms peace gives A 1.8 and B 3.2, against 1.5 each from war [num: legacy v4.2]. For the gradual shift, with $w$ falling from 0.5 to 0 over $N$ periods, $c_A=0.05$ and $\delta=0.8$, attack is rational at the start for $N\le3$ and never for $N\ge4$, by backward induction on $V_t=\max(W_t,\delta V_{t+1})$ [num: legacy v4.4]. $\square$ The premise is the protocol (A8); the mechanism is the incredibility of a promise the advantaged side has no reason to keep, the same incredibility that Proposition 5.8 states for a winner. In the loop of Section 2 the shift is the opening of a lead, at the speed of Remark S2.1.

### Proof of Proposition 5.10

By Proposition 3.6(1), total intensity is $ne(\theta_t)$, which gives $h_t$; survival is $\prod_te^{-h_t}$ by the chain rule. Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the divisible prize gives the contest with prize $(1-\beta)\Omega_t$ (Proposition 10.2), linear in $h_t$; a rebated levy enters at $\tau(n-1-\theta_t)/n$. Since $\ell/(c+(1+\theta)\ell)<1/(1+\theta)$, $\sum_tr_t\Omega_t(n-1-\theta_t)/(1+\theta_t)<\infty$ suffices for $\theta_t>-1$ whatever $\ell_t$. A background hazard from capability growth adds to $h_t$, and no lever of the contest removes it: that is the growth channel. $\square$ The closed form agrees with direct evaluation by construction, and the symmetric intensity with a best response on a grid (0.11444) [identity: RH, with an independent grid best response].

### Lemma 5.11 in full

*Fix one irreversible event. If on every surviving history the residual probability in round $k$ is at least $h_k$ with $\sum_kh_k=\infty$, then $\Pr(\Phi_N)\le\prod_{k\le N}(1-h_k)\le\exp(-\sum_{k\le N}h_k)\to0$, by the chain rule, without independence between rounds; if it is at most $\bar h_k<1$ with $\sum\bar h_k<\infty$, survival is at least $\prod(1-\bar h_k)>0$.* With $h_k=0.2/k$ survival is 0.34, 0.22 and 0.14 by rounds 100, 1,000 and 10,000; with $0.2/k^2$ it settles at 0.70 [num: legacy v5].

### Proof of Proposition 5.12

Proportional correction: Hayes (1950). Derivative action extends the range: for $a=1$ and $\tau_c=1.5$, gains $k_p=1.02$, $k_d=0.644$ stabilize the mode, where proportional correction alone diverges [num: legacy v4.2]. For the sensitivity bound, with plant $e^{-s\tau_c}/(s-a)$ and any stabilizing linear time-invariant controller, the complementary sensitivity is stable with value 1 at $s=a$; writing it as $e^{-s\tau_c}$ times a stable function and applying the maximum modulus principle gives $\|\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge e^{a\tau_c}$, and robust stability $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty<1$ gives $|w(a)|e^{a\tau_c}<1$, necessary only: $w(s)=2(s-a)/(s+a)$ has $w(a)=0$ but $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge2e^{a\tau_c}$. Evidence cannot be acted on before it exists. $\square$

### What would make coverage lag

Consequences take their time, and tools raise the throughput of observation without making consequences arrive sooner; institutions deliberate at rates set by attention and procedure; learning theoretical models and forming new mechanisms are human processes; and understanding produced with human time, $\dot U=A_UL_U^{\lambda_U}U^{\phi_U}$ with $\phi_U<1$ and bounded labour, grows polynomially (Jones 1995; Bloom et al. 2020). None settles the premise: if AI raises $A_U$ exponentially at rate $g_A$, $U$ grows exponentially at $g_A/(1-\phi_U)$. Where correction leans on AI, the human capacity it rests on can erode, which raises the rate correction needs (Proposition 4.6). Measures: the safety of control protocols at a fixed audit budget across generations, and catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours, controlled for evaluation awareness. Under strict legibility, (coverage lags) is close to definitional for systems beyond human capability, and the empirical content lies in (residual floor), which a safety case with a numerical bound on the uncovered part would state (Clymer et al. 2024). (variety grows) is what makes (coverage lags) expected on a continuation branch; the theorem does not use it, and R5's list of conditions under which the requirement is not needed keeps it.

### Proof of Theorem 5.13

For $t\ge T_\varepsilon$, (coverage lags) gives $U_t/Y_t\le\varepsilon$ on every surviving history, so by (residual floor) the conditional probability of the event is at least $h(1-\varepsilon)$; the chain rule gives the bound. The growth of $Y_t$ is never used, which is why (variety grows) is a motivation and not a premise. Under (shared failure) the event is one for all, so the bound is every actor's; without it the chain runs over the racer's own exposure. The converse is the upper part of Lemma 5.11 with $\bar h_k=\bar hu_k$. (residual floor) gives a liminf of the hazard of at least $h$, not that the hazard tends to $h$. With $u_t=1/(t+2)^2$ coverage stays behind forever and survival with $h=0.05$ stays above 0.98; with $h=0.05$ and $\varepsilon=0.1$, survival eighty periods after $T_\varepsilon$ is at most 0.025 [num: legacy v5]. $\square$

### Proposition 5.14 in full

*Let hazardous variety be frozen at $Y_\tau$ from $\tau$ while coverage grows. (1) The exposure after $\tau$ is $\int_\tau^\infty(1-U(t)/Y_\tau)^+dt$: $Y_\tau(1-U(\tau)/Y_\tau)^2/(2\dot U)$ for linear coverage, and $(\ln\varrho-1+1/\varrho)/g_U$ for coverage growing at rate $g_U$, with $\varrho=Y_\tau/U(\tau)>1$. (2) Rolled back to $Y\le U$, exposure stops. (3) If a sprint builds capability $\varrho_X$ times the rivals' level and hazardous variety is a fraction $\varphi$ of capability's, frozen exposure is zero when $\varphi\varrho_X\le1$. (4) For linear coverage the same frozen ratio costs more the later the sprint; this fails for some sub-exponential coverage.*

*Proof.* Integrate. For (4), let $\log U$ rise by 0.5 on each interval $[m^2,m^2+1]$ and stay constant otherwise: $\log U\approx0.5\sqrt t=o(t)$, while $\dot U/U=0.5$ inside each interval, so a sprint timed to end inside one costs less than an earlier one (S14). $\square$ With hazardous variety equal to capability's, growth 0.3, linear coverage $1+0.5t$ and $h=0.05$, a sprint to $\tau=10$ leaves survival 0.86; freezing then gives 0.53, and rolling back keeps 0.86 [num: legacy v4.5]. What the freezing winner gives up is permanent superiority: a frozen stock is overtaken by anyone who keeps growing, which is why Section 8.3 treats the sprint-and-freeze path by its incidence and not by this proposition alone.

### Proof of Proposition 5.15

After $t_N$, $U_t\ge U_{t_N}+u(t-t_N)$, so the uncovered share is zero from $t_N+Y_N/u$ on, and Lemma 5.11's upper part bounds survival below. In the growth-channel example, capability frozen at need leaves survival on the channel at 0.717, with coverage catching up at period 14, while capability compounding at 10% a period leaves $1.8\times10^{-9}$ by period 400 [num: PE]. The success function is homogeneous of degree zero, so symmetric intensity is proportional to the stakes for every $\theta<n-1$; an objective bounded in the stakes, such as $1-e^{-\Omega}$, has intensity bounded as they grow. With $n=4$, $r=1$ and $c=1$, intensities are 0.19, 1.88 and 18.75 for linear payoffs at stakes 1, 10 and 100, against 0.12, 0.19 and 0.19 for the saturating objective [num: SM]. $\square$ An absolute objective linear in the stakes is as unsatiated as a relative one, which is why the core states the rule as satiation in the stakes and not as relative against absolute.

### Proof of Proposition 5.16

(1) Let all $n$ actors hold $\theta$, so $J_S=\pi_S+\frac\theta{n-1}\big(\pi_R+\sum_k\pi_k\big)$ and $J_R=\pi_R+\frac\theta{n-1}\big(\pi_S+\sum_k\pi_k\big)$, the sums over the third parties $k$. With $\pi_S=-(y-u-b_0)^2$, $\pi_R=-(y-u)^2$ and $\sum_k\pi_k$ linear in $y$ with total slope $L$, both objectives are quadratic in $y$ with the same curvature, $-2(n-1+\theta)/(n-1)$, negative iff $n-1+\theta>0$. Their ideal points are
$$y_S-u=\frac{2b_0(n-1)+\theta L}{2(n-1+\theta)},\qquad y_R-u=\frac{2\theta b_0+\theta L}{2(n-1+\theta)},$$
which differ by $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$, independent of $L$: what the receiver's action does to third parties enters both objectives alike. Each objective is then, up to a positive factor and terms that do not depend on $y$, the uniform-quadratic payoff of Crawford and Sobel with that difference as the bias, and their condition for an informative equilibrium applies to it. Numerically maximized ideal points match the formula in 19 combinations of $n\in\{2,3,4,6\}$ and $\theta$, to $1.5\times10^{-8}$ [num: TK].

(2) At $\theta=-1$ with $n$ actors, $J_k=\pi_k-\frac1{n-1}\sum_{j\ne k}\pi_j$, and direct expansion gives $J_S-J_R=\frac n{n-1}(\pi_S-\pi_R)$ and $J_S+J_R=\frac{n-2}{n-1}(\pi_S+\pi_R)-\frac2{n-1}\sum_{k\ne S,R}\pi_k$; the identity holds to $3.6\times10^{-15}$ [num: T3]. With $n\ge3$ the effective bias of (1) is $b_0n/(n-2)$: at $b_0=0.05$ it is 0.150, 0.100, 0.075 and 0.0625 for $n=3$, 4, 6 and 10, so informative talk needs a material bias below 0.083, 0.125, 0.167 and 0.200 [num: TK]. A two-message partition equilibrium, built directly with indifferent boundary types and numerical best responses, exists iff the effective bias is below $\frac14$ (for $n=3$: at $b_0=0.08$, bias 0.24, boundary type 0.02; at $b_0=0.1$, bias 0.30, none; at exactly $\frac14$, none); with $b_0=0$ and a common cost to third parties, full revelation is an equilibrium [num: T3]. With third-party payoff $-ky^2$ instead of linear, the gap in units of the state is still $b_0n/(n-2)$ to the grid's resolution [num: TK]. With $n=2$ at $\theta=-1$ the curvature vanishes and the objectives sum to zero. If a sender's and a receiver's payoffs sum to zero, each sender type induces, among the equilibrium actions, one that minimizes the receiver's payoff in its state, so the receiver's payoff is at most that of ignoring messages, which it can always do; equilibrium payoffs equal the no-talk values (300 random games, 1,096 pure equilibria, none whose receiver payoff differs from the no-talk value) [num: TK].

(3) A sender that intends to race prefers the receiver to cooperate: with $R=1$, $T=2\varepsilon$, $P=\varepsilon$ and $S=0$, it gains $\varepsilon$ from the receiver's cooperation however small $\varepsilon$ is (compare Aumann 1990). $\square$

That pairs who still talk align against third parties is a reading of (2), not a result; the amplification falls with $n$, as Snidal (1991) found for relative gains generally.

### Proof of Proposition 5.17

*Arm 1.* By Remark S2.1, one channel's gap factor is $\Lambda^\ast=1+(d_K+\delta_f)(\Gamma-1)$, and $(d_K+\delta_f)\Gamma=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K$ by (2), so $\Lambda^\ast-1=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K-d_K-\delta_f$, increasing in $\omega$. Where $\Lambda^\ast>1$, $T_{\rm dec}=\ln(\bar\Delta/\varepsilon_0)/\ln\Lambda^\ast$ falls with $\omega$, and since $\upsilon$ is non-decreasing, $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ rises, strictly where $\upsilon$ is increasing. With several channels the Perron root of (S1) rises with each channel's term $(1-\beta)r_j\omega_jd_j$, since above its poles the right side of the secular equation increases in each term and decreases in $\Lambda$. *Arm 2.* By (7) the deviation's gain in objectives is $g_i(\theta)=(1+\theta)\bar\Delta+\frac{n-1-\theta}{n-1}(\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta)$, which falls in $\theta$ when $\bar\Delta\le0<\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta$. At a symmetric profile $J_i=(1+\theta)\bar\pi$, so $C_i-v_i=(1+\theta)(\bar\pi_C-\bar\pi_P)$, which rises in $\theta$. Write $\bar p_{d,i}=(c-\alpha)/(b+c)$ as in the proof of Proposition 5.4: a narrower $\theta$ raises $\alpha$, lowers $c$ and does not lower $b$, and each lowers $\bar p_{d,i}$ where $b+c>0$ and $0\le\bar p_{d,i}\le1$. With a fixed prize, $\partial\theta_\Gamma/\partial\Gamma_\delta$ has the sign of $\theta_0-(n-1)<0$, and in one channel $\Gamma_\delta=(1-\beta)r\omega\,\delta d_K/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K-\delta_f)\big)$ is proportional to $\omega$; with a growing prize, Proposition 3.9 gives the sign. The binary game of Remark S5.1 meets the premises, and there $\bar p_d(\theta)=1-(1-\delta)(A-\theta B)/\big(\delta K(1+\theta)\big)$ rises with $\theta$ wherever $A>\theta B$, since $A-\theta B$ falls and $1+\theta$ rises: the instance the reduced form below computes. *Arm 3.* $\partial\bar p_{d,i}/\partial v_i=-\big[(1-\delta)g_i+\delta(D_i-C_i)\big]/\big[\delta(D_i-v_i)^2\big]$, negative wherever $\bar p_{d,i}<1$; Section 4 gives the mechanism by which captured correctors raise the value of the punishment phase. *Arm 4.* $\bar p_{d,i}$ is unchanged when $(g_i,C_i,D_i,v_i)$ are scaled alike. If $\alpha$ and $b$ are multiplied by $k$ relative to $c$, $\bar p_{d,i}=(c-k\alpha)/(kb+c)$, whose derivative in $k$ is $-c(\alpha+b)/(kb+c)^2<0$ where $c>0$ and $\alpha+b>0$, that is, $\bar p_{d,i}<1$ [num: ES]. *The pivot.* In one channel $\Gamma=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K/(d_K+\delta_f)$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ are both proportional to $\omega=\kappa_c\Omega/(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)$, which rises with the stakes and falls with provision, so arms 1 and 2 are comparative statics of $\omega$. $\square$

*The reduced form (JX).* In the binary game of Remark S5.1 at $D=C$, let actors act on $\theta_\Gamma(\theta_0,\Gamma_\delta)$ with zero common return, so that the compounding contest running alongside enters to first order through the price of standing; rivals do not respond, one $p_d$ applies to the deviation, and the punishment is the stage equilibrium of the binary game, with no breakout during punishment ($q_b=0$). The largest discounted loop gain at which restraint remains enforceable is found by bisection on direct discounted streams, with no threshold formula. For $\theta_0=0$ it is 0.924, 0.852, 0.387 and 0.108 at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.9 and 0.94, and none exists above $p_d=0.9495$; for $\theta_0=0.4$ it is 0.953, 0.908, 0.621, 0.448, 0.377 and 0.161 at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.9, 0.94, 0.95 and 0.97, and none above 0.9793 (V6.1 printed 0.99 for both). With a common loop gain, the prize growing with what all reinvest, the objective blade opens again: at $p_d=0.9$ the value rises from 0.387 to 0.571 at $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=0.3$ and to 0.755 at 0.6 [num: JX]. In this game $D+V^{\rm sub}>2P$, so a breakout technology that also acted during punishment would soften it (Proposition 5.4(5)) and lower every figure; the joint model below computes that case.

**Remark S5.2 (Both blades in one reduced model) [num].** The arms of Proposition 5.17 are proved one at a time; this remark runs them together along paths of the pivot, so that the conjunction can be seen in one place. It estimates nothing. *Model.* Four actors and one ratio channel, capacity, in the model of Proposition 2.3: $p_i=K_i^r/\sum_jK_j^r$ with $r=3$, $d_K=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.05$ and nothing shared; the won share $\omega$ fixes provision against the stakes, and $\Gamma$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ follow from it at $\delta=0.9$. $T_{\rm dec}$ is the number of periods in which the nonlinear stock dynamics take a lead from 5% to 50%, with stakes and provision held at their current values while it opens (the linearized time is computed beside it). Detection and response run on a memoryless clock of mean 10 periods, so $p_d=e^{-T_{\rm dec}/10}$ (Remark 5.5). Actors act on the working objective $\theta_\Gamma(0,\Gamma_\delta)$ of Proposition 3.9, and on $-1$ at $\Gamma_\delta\ge1$, the limit from below; rivals do not respond. The stage game is that of Remark S5.1 ($b=1$, $c=0.3$, $h_d=0.2$, $\epsilon=1$) with every payoff, the decisive value included, proportional to the stakes, so that $\bar p_d$ does not depend on their scale and the stakes arm is off. The decisive position is worth $D=C$ or $D-C=0.5$ in objective units per unit of stakes. Punishment is reversion to the race, (i) without breakouts, or (ii) with one breakout technology throughout: during the punishment race each racer breaks out with the same $p_d$, independently, a simultaneous breakout leaving the race as it was and the subordinated being worth the race.

*Results.* At $D=C$ restraint is enforceable iff $\omega<0.551$ under (i) and $\omega<0.542$ under (ii). With $p_d$ held at zero, the objective blade alone closes at 0.556; with the objective held at $\theta_0$, the breakout blade alone never closes, since $p_d$ reaches only 0.45 at $\omega=0.9$. At the crossing $\Gamma=1.32$, $\Gamma_\delta=0.92$ and $p_d=0.10$. At $D-C=0.5$ the thresholds are 0.536 and 0.504. Along paths on which $\omega/(1-\omega)$ grows at the rate of the stakes less that of provision: with stakes growing 5% a period and provision fixed, from $\omega=0.20$, $p_d$ rises from 0 to 0.07 and $\bar p_d$ falls from 0.911 to 0.386 by period 30, and the blades cross at period 31.8; with provision growing as fast as the stakes, neither moves ($\omega=0.20$ and $\bar p_d=0.911$ throughout); with provision outgrowing the stakes from $\omega=0.60$, enforceability returns within ten periods. Over 96 settings of the detection clock (5 to 40 periods), diffusion (0 to 0.1), held self-model (0 and 0.4), decisiveness (2 and 3) and $D-C$ (0 and 0.5), the joint threshold lies at or below the lower of the two blades' own in all 96, strictly below in 84, and the breakout blade alone closes in 12 [num: JX, one reduced model, rivals not responding]. In this model the objective blade does most of the closing, and the breakout blade moves the crossing earlier.

*A second specification.* A separately written model, with different choices throughout, gives the same picture; its meeting times are its own and are not comparable with those above. Four actors; one channel with $r=1.5$, $d_K=0.5$, $\delta_f=0$, $\beta=0$ and $\kappa_c=1$; $\delta=0.8$; stakes $\Omega_t=e^{0.05t}$ against provision $k_0=1$, so that $\omega$ starts at 0.20; $T_{\rm dec}$ from the linearized gap factor, from a 1% to a 50% gap; a detection clock of mean 12 periods; $\theta_0=0$; the stage game of Remark S5.1 held fixed while the stakes grow; punishment without breakouts. $\Gamma$ passes 1 near period 42, after which $p_d$ rises from 0, while $\theta$ narrows from $-0.16$ and $\bar p_d$ falls from 0.842; the blades meet at period 59, where $\omega=0.83$, $\Gamma=1.24$, $T_{\rm dec}=34$ periods, $p_d=0.057$, $\theta=-0.78$ and $\bar p_d=0.045$. If $b$ and $d$ scale with the stakes while $c$ does not, which turns part of the stakes arm on, they meet at period 47. With provision growing as fast as the stakes neither blade moves. Detection twice as fast moves the meeting only from period 59 to 60, and a held self-model of 0.4 moves it to period 69: here too the objective arm, not the breakout arm, does the closing, since $p_d$ is still small when $\bar p_d$ reaches it [num: JX, one reduced model, rivals not responding].

*Label.* Two reduced models, with rivals not responding and, in their base runs, the stakes arm switched off; in the world the co-movement of the arms is conjecture C0, tested by Test E (Section 7.4). What the models show is narrower and exact: along a path on which the stakes outgrow provision beneath the game, both blades of (14) move toward each other through the one quantity $\omega$, and along a path on which provision keeps pace, neither moves.

## S6. AI: instrument and incarnation

This chapter gives the proofs and numerics of Section 6; the full statements of three results the core states in a sentence (Proposition S6.2, Proposition S6.4, Remark S6.5); and three supplementary results: how certain a lock must be for narrow E1 to need no trust (Remark S6.1), the formal part of the masked class, and the bounded optimizer (Remark S6.3). Numbers cite the block of `checks_v62.py` that reproduces them; AL is the alignment review's independent recomputation of Section 6 (`../v6.1/reviews/scripts-v6.1-alignment/al_checks.py`), ported whole, with its parts named as the reviewer named them.

### Proof of Proposition 6.1

The harm coefficient, $d$ in V6.1, is written $h_d$, since $d$ is the correction rate; its exponent in (3) is $\beta_h$ (the log of SM(5) writes $\beta_d$).

Changing $a_i$ from 0 to 1 changes $u_i$ by $\epsilon b-c-\epsilon h_d$, independently of the others' choices, and each other $u_j$ by $-\epsilon b/(n-1)-\epsilon h_d$. Summed over all principals the relative terms cancel: at all-racing each principal has $-c-n\epsilon h_d$, which gives (1). In objectives, racing changes $J_i$ by $\epsilon b\big(1-\frac{\theta}{n-1}\big)-c-\epsilon h_d(1+\theta)$, which is positive iff $\theta<\theta^\ast$; this gives (2). For (3), write $\theta^\ast=(T-H-c)/(T/(n-1)+H)$ with $T=\epsilon b$ and $H=\epsilon h_d$ growing with exponents $1+\beta_b$ and $1+\beta_h$, and $c$ with $\beta_c$. If $\beta_c<1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$, $c$ is negligible against the larger of $T$ and $H$, and the limit is set by $T/H$, which is constant, unbounded or vanishing as $\beta_b$ equals, exceeds or falls short of $\beta_h$. If $\beta_c>1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$, the numerator tends to $-\infty$ faster than the denominator grows. $\square$

*Numerics* ($n=4$, $b=1$, $h_d=0.2$, $c=0.3$). Exponents $(\beta_b,\beta_h,\beta_c)=(0,0,0)$ and $(1,1,0)$ give the limit 1.50; $(0,0.5,0)$ gives $-0.99$ at $\epsilon=10^6$; $(0.5,0,0)$ gives 2.998; $(1,0,3)$ and $(0,0,1.5)$ diverge to $-\infty$; at the boundary $\beta_c=1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$, $(0,0,1)$ gives 0.9375 and $(1,0,2)$ gives 2.1 [num: SM(5)]. With $b$, $h_d$ and $c$ fixed, $\theta^\ast$ is 0.9375 at $\epsilon=1$ and 1.44 at $\epsilon=10$; with $c$ also scaling with $\epsilon$ it stays 0.94 [num: AL(6.1)]. The double edge of Section 6.2: at $\epsilon=1$, all-racing gives each principal $-1.1$; lowering $h_d$ to 0.1 and 0.05 cuts the loss to 0.70 and 0.50 while raising $\theta^\ast$ to 1.39 and 1.70, and $\Delta J_i$ changes sign at $\theta^\ast$ [num: AL(6.1)]. This is the second fate of safety progress in Section 6.4. The condition once written as $\beta_c<\beta_b=\beta_d$ was neither necessary nor sufficient; the correct one is $\beta_c<1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$.

### Proof of Proposition 6.2

Actor $i$'s payoff is $-L_e(1-w_i)-L_c\big[1-\prod_j(1-\psi_j)\big]$, with $w_i=(1+s\iota_i)/\sum_j(1+s\iota_j)$. (1) With $k$ others delegating, $w_i(1,k)=(1+s)/(n+(k+1)s)$ and $w_i(0,k)=1/(n+ks)$. The first is non-decreasing in $s$ and the second decreasing, so the gain $L_e[w_i(1,k)-w_i(0,k)]$ increases in $s$, toward $L_e(1-1/n)$ for $k=0$ and $L_e/(k+1)$ for $k\ge1$. The cost is the change in shared survival, $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$, independent of $s$. If every limit exceeds its cost, there is a speed beyond which delegating is strictly better at every $k$; if one limit does not, delegating is worse at that $k$ for every $s$, since the gain stays below its limit. The smallest limit is $L_e/n$ and the largest cost $L_c\Delta\psi$, so $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ suffices. (2) With all delegating $w_i=1/n$, as with none, and shared survival is $(1-\psi_1)^n<(1-\psi_0)^n$. (3) Under relative objectives the shared term is common to all and cancels; the gain in $w_i$ remains, and is positive for every $s>0$. $\square$

*Numerics.* With $n=3$, $L_e=L_c$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\psi_1=0.2$, delegation is dominant from $s^\ast=1.1628$, where a lone delegator wins with probability 0.5196 against $1/3$ without delegation [num: AL(6.2)]. A speed advantage of this size is far from decisive, which is the sense of "well below decisive capability" in Section 6.3.

### Proof of Lemma 6.3, and where $q_L$ comes from

With $\dot{\mathcal M}=-\delta_h\mathcal M$ under delegation and $\dot{\mathcal M}=\varrho_h(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)$ otherwise, capacity reaches $\mathcal M_{\min}$ after $T_{\rm lock}=\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ of delegation, and from $\mathcal M<\mathcal M_{\min}$ it regains $\mathcal M_{\min}$ after $T_R(\mathcal M)=\varrho_h^{-1}\ln[(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)/(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M_{\min})]$. Capacity maintained in parallel does not decay; the resources it uses are withdrawn from strength. $\square$

The lock probability $q_L$ of Proposition 6.4 is therefore not free. Let a delegation last $T_{\rm del}$, and let the contest allow an actor that reverts a window $T_W$ before its next consequential decision. The lock counts if the delegated decision is irreversible within the window for deliberation, or if $T_{\rm del}>T_{\rm lock}$ and $T_W<T_R\big(\mathcal M(T_{\rm del})\big)$; $q_L$ is the probability of that event, set by the kind of decision, the contest's tempo and $\delta_h$. Drilled parallel capacity keeps the second part of the event rare at a cost in strength, which is why it has to be held in common. The same capacity carries correction: where the effective correction rate is $d(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$ and $\mathcal M$ decays at $\delta_h$, the rate at which independent correction must grow rises by $w\delta_h$ (Proposition 4.6; S4, block QS).

### Proposition 6.4 in full, and proof

*Let locked delegation end the delegator's ability to overrule with probability $q_L$, and losing the contest mean subordination with probability $p$. With $Q=\prod_j(1-\psi_j)$ and win probabilities as in Proposition 6.2, actor $i$'s survival is $\Phi_i=(1-q_L\iota_i)\,Q\,[w_i+(1-w_i)(1-p)]$. Write $a=(1-\psi_1)/(1-\psi_0)$ and, with $k$ others delegating, $w_D=(1+s)/(n+(k+1)s)$ and $w_N=1/(n+ks)$, so that*
$$\frac{\Phi_D}{\Phi_N}=(1-q_L)\,a\,\frac{1-p+p\,w_D}{1-p+p\,w_N}.$$
*(1) If $q_L=1$ and $Q>0$, not delegating gives strictly higher survival for every finite $s$, every $n$, every $p$ and every profile of the others. (2) If $p=1$ and $A=(1-q_L)a$, the ratio is $A(1+s)(n+ks)/(n+(k+1)s)$, which rises with $k$; all delegating is an equilibrium iff $A(n+(n-1)s)/n\ge1$, and no one delegating iff $An(1+s)/(n+s)\le1$. At least one always holds, since $A(n+(n-1)s)/n-An(1+s)/(n+s)=A(n-1)s^2/[n(n+s)]\ge0$, and both can. (3) If $p<1$, the subordination term $1-p$ dilutes the win probabilities and the ratio can fall as $k$ rises. If it decreases in $k$ and crosses one between $k=m-1$ and $k=m$ for some $1\le m\le n-1$, the profiles with exactly $m$ delegators are the only pure equilibria, and neither corner is one. (4) At bounded speed one delegator does not drive another's survival to zero: a non-delegator facing $k$ delegators survives with probability at least $Q_N/(n+ks)>0$.*

*Proof.* (1) $\Phi_D=0$ while $\Phi_N\ge Q_Nw_N>0$. (2) Take $k=n-1$ for all delegating and $k=0$ for none; the identity gives "at least one". With $p=1$ delegations are complements. (3) A profile with $m$ delegators is an equilibrium iff the ratio is at least one at $k=m-1$, so that no delegator gains by stopping, and at most one at $k=m$, so that no other actor gains by starting; a decreasing ratio meets both conditions at one $m$ only. With $p<1$ delegations can be substitutes. (4) $\Phi_N=Q_N[1-p+p\,w_N]\ge Q_Nw_N$. $\square$

*Numerics.* The outside reviewer's case, $n=3$, $s=1$, $p=0.1$, $q_L=0$, $\psi_0=0$, $\psi_1=0.015$: the ratios for $k=0,1,2$ are 1.00259, 1.00097 and 0.99928, so the only pure equilibrium has two delegators; with $p=1$ the ratios rise with $k$, 1.48, 1.58 and 1.64, and all delegate [num: DL(a), (b)]. Over 20,000 random draws with $q_L<1$, neither corner is an equilibrium in 0 draws at $p=1$, 1,586 at $p=0.5$ and 60 at $p=0.1$ [num: DL(c)]. Part (1) holds in 20,000 of 20,000 random settings [num: DL(d)]. At $p=1$, over 100,000 draws "neither" never occurs and "both" occurs 4,068 times [num: SM(6)]; an independent recomputation with another sampling distribution finds 0 and 21,922, with the closed forms equal to the direct survival comparison in every draw [num: AL(6.4)]. With $n=2$, $q_L=0.99$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\psi_1=0.05$, all delegating is an equilibrium only for $s\ge206.4$, with survival 0.0045 there [num: AL(6.4)]. V6.1's core statement of part (2) omitted $p=1$, without which it is false.

**Remark S6.1 (How certain the lock must be) [thm].** *In the model of Proposition 6.4, not delegating gives strictly higher survival for every $n$, every $p$ and every profile of the others whenever $(1-q_L)(1+s)(1-\psi_1)<1-\psi_0$.* Since $w_N\le w_D\le(1+s)w_N$, the ratio $(1-p+pw_D)/(1-p+pw_N)$ lies between 1 and $1+s$, so $\Phi_D/\Phi_N\le(1-q_L)a(1+s)<1$. The bound is approached as $n\to\infty$ with $k=0$ and $p=1$, where the ratio is $(1-q_L)a(1+s)\,n/(n+s)$. A lock is near certain in the sense of narrow E1 when its probability of failing is below $(1-\psi_0)/\big((1-\psi_1)(1+s)\big)$: the larger the speed advantage, the nearer one the lock must be for keeping AI out to need no trust.

### Proofs of Lemma 6.5 and Remark 6.6

*Lemma 6.5.* Variants acting on common terms exist and have relative performance lower by at least a positive margin, so under cumulative retention their share tends to zero, as in Proposition 3.4; the retained systems' decisions are the actor's. If constraints are an enforced admission condition, no admissible variant violates them. That an actor which cannot overrule decisions taken in its name has lost O2 in that domain is a definition. For a reader who counts survival only as preservation of the unit, delegation acts through the shared hazard instead: delegated systems interacting at machine speed without a human veto raise the probability of shared loss by reciprocal fear of surprise attack (Schelling 1960), inadvertent escalation (Posen 1991) and flash war (Scharre 2018), an argument with cited mechanisms, not a derivation. $\square$

*Remark 6.6.* A faithful system maximizes its operator's objective. Under a relative payoff that objective puts a weight on winning, equivalent to a self-model $\theta<0$ (Proposition 3.5(3)), and where reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return the working objective narrows further (Proposition 3.9). Retention by contest performance selects among systems on the same relative payoff. A more effective system raises the value the objective attains, not its content, as $\epsilon$ does in Proposition 6.1. $\square$

### Risk homeostasis in delegation (Section 6.3)
