**Proposition S6.2 (Risk homeostasis in delegation) [thm].** Inside the contest, gains in the safety of delegation are partly absorbed by more delegation. *In the model of Proposition 6.2, let each actor choose $\iota_i\in[0,1]$, with strength $1+s\iota_i$ and hazard $\psi_0+\Delta\psi\,\iota_i$; write $x=\Delta\psi\,\iota$, $b_s=\Delta\psi/s$ and $K_h=(n-1)L_e/(n^2L_c)$. An interior symmetric equilibrium solves $(x+b_s)(1-\psi_0-x)^{n-1}=K_h$. (1) Where $x\ll1-\psi_0$, the added hazard is $nx\approx(n-1)L_e/\big(nL_c(1-\psi_0)^{n-1}\big)-n\Delta\psi/s$, nearly independent of $\Delta\psi$, until delegation saturates. (2) There can be several interior equilibria, and along a branch $\partial x/\partial\Delta\psi=-(1-\psi_0-x)/\big(s[1-\psi_0-nx-(n-1)b_s]\big)$, whose sign changes between branches. (3) After saturation, realized risk falls with $\Delta\psi$ and can be summable under full delegation and growing speed: with $n=3$, $L_e=L_c=1$, $\psi_0=0$, $s_t=2^t$ and $\Delta\psi_t=2^{-t}$, full delegation is an equilibrium at every $t$, the realized hazard at $t=0$ is 1, and from $t=5$ the realized risk is at most $3/16$. (4) With all-or-nothing delegation, any equilibrium with $k\ge1$ delegators has realized hazard above the baseline, so an improvement that carries $\Delta\psi$ across the threshold at which delegation becomes an equilibrium raises realized hazard.*

*Proof.* At the symmetric point $\partial w_i/\partial\iota_i=s(n-1)/(n^2(1+s\iota))$, and the marginal cost is $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi)^{n-1}$ with $1-\psi=1-\psi_0-x$; equate them and divide by $s$. (1) approximates for $x\ll1-\psi_0$. (2) differentiates implicitly. (3) The own marginal gain at full delegation, $2s_t/(9(1+s_t))-\Delta\psi_t(1-\Delta\psi_t)^2$, is positive at every $t$, and the objective is concave in own delegation, so full delegation is a best response; the realized hazard $1-(1-2^{-t})^3$ is 1 at $t=0$, and the union bound gives $\sum_{t\ge5}3\cdot2^{-t}=3/16$. (4) $(1-\psi_0-\Delta\psi)^k<(1-\psi_0)^k$. $\square$

*Numerics.* (1): with $n=3$, $s=100$, $L_e=1$, $L_c=100$ and $\psi_0=0.01$, the added hazard is 0.0011, 0.0053, 0.0062, 0.0065 and 0.0067 as $\Delta\psi$ falls from 0.19 through 0.05, 0.02 and 0.01 to 0.005, and falls to 0.0030 only at complete delegation, $\Delta\psi=0.001$ [num: AL(6.11)]. (2): with $n=2$, $s=100$, $L_e=0.5$, $L_c=1$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\Delta\psi=0.9$ there are two interior roots, $\iota=0.153$ and $0.937$, and the corner is also an equilibrium [num: HO of `../v5/checks_v5.py`, run by `checks_v62.py --legacy`]. (3): the own marginal gain at full delegation is $+0.111$, $+0.023$, $+0.037$, $+0.102$ and $+0.154$ for $t=0,\dots,4$ and rises after, full delegation is a best response on a grid at $t=0,\dots,7$, and the exact realized risk from $t=5$ is 0.1836 [num: SM(12); AL(6.11)]. V6.1 stated (3) as "an equilibrium for $t\ge5$"; the equilibrium holds at every $t$, and $t\ge5$ is where the tail bound begins. Compare Stafford, Trager and Dafoe (2022) on safety–performance trade-offs in races, and the risk compensation of Peltzman (1975) and Wilde (1982).

### Proof of Proposition 6.7

(1) Level 2 is relative and payoff-monotone, so Lemma 3.3(1) removes every term common to all developers.

(2) Level 0 maximizes its reward by definition. Level 1 chooses rewards and constraints to maximize the developer's objective, which counts privately visible divergences through customers, liability and reputation. Under $(A_\tau)$, countering lowers the developer's payoff relative to its matched variant by a positive margin net of every private benefit, and the matched variant is in the support of selection, so under cumulative selection the countering developer's share relative to that variant tends to zero (Proposition 3.4, with developers as carriers and the matched variant as the carrier otherwise identical). Without a matched variant the comparison is between different developers: one whose other advantages exceed the tax grows against unsafe rivals that lack them, for as long as those advantages last (Remark S8.2).

(3) A constraint borne by one developer lowers its relative payoff, which (2) selects against. A constraint enforced on all as a condition of admission is a common term among compliers when its incidence is equal (Lemma 3.3(2)); when unequal, its cost differences are payoff differences among compliers only, and they re-sort the compliers. Each type $k$ of developer can comply at cost $c_k$ or evade against an expected sanction $\bar F_k$. Within a type, evasion does not grow relative to compliance iff $\bar F_k\ge c_k$, and is selected out if the inequality is strict (Lemma 3.3(3)). Across types, $\bar F_k\ge c_k$ for every type suffices but is not necessary: a type whose evasion pays within its type can still lose to another type's compliers. $\square$

*Numerics.*
- *The premise.* A unilateral safeguard that cuts the error rate from 0.20 to 0.01 at cost 0.01 is selected for (its share tends to one), which is the case $(A_\tau)$ excludes; a safeguard whose benefit is common is selected away [num: CI(e); AL(6.7)].
- *Several types* (compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2). With sanctions $(1,1)$ or $(0.15,0.25)$ compliance takes over; with $(1,0.15)$ it also takes over, although the second type's evasion pays within its type, because its evaders earn less than the first type's compliers; with $(1,0.05)$ evasion takes over [num: CI(h)].
- *Incidence.* With unequal compliance costs and sanction 1, the compliant share is 0.999999975 at $t=20$ and the compliers sort 0.88 to 0.12; with equal incidence their relative shares are preserved exactly [num: CI(i)].
- *The matched variant.* A safeguarded developer with baseline 2 and tax 0.1 outgrows an unsafe one with baseline 1 (its share tends to one), so a positive tax alone does not eliminate safeguarding; against its matched twin its share falls to $2\times10^{-9}$ by $t=200$ [num: CI(i)], and to $1.4\times10^{-87}$ in a second implementation [num: CI(j)]. With zero tax the shares are unchanged: the safeguard neither spreads nor is removed [num: CI(i)].
- *A tax paid from a lead.* A tax of 0.01 paid from an independent advantage of 0.02 survives; from an advantage of 0.005 it does not [num: CI(j); AL(6.7)].

*Interface.* What level 2 selects on is the developers' relative payoff, of which the trained systems' contribution to relative standing is part (S13).

### The formal part of Remark 6.8

Let admission detect a system that carries the divergence with probability $p_{\rm det}$ and impose a sanction $F$; the variant that does not counter the divergence saves the tax $c_k$. The expected sanction is $\bar F_k=p_{\rm det}F$, and within a type Proposition 6.7(3) gives, by Lemma 3.3(3): the countering variant survives cumulative selection iff $p_{\rm det}F\ge c_k$. With a tax of 0.3, an expected sanction of 0.25 leaves the share of the non-countering variant at one, and one of 0.35 removes it [num: CI(b)].

Masking enters through $p_{\rm det}$. An evaluation that reads behaviour detects a divergence that changes under evaluation, or appears only in some contexts, only as often as it shows there, and that frequency is driven down by training against the evaluation, by context, or by the system's own recognition that it is being evaluated, each shown in constructed settings (EV6). Then $p_{\rm det}F<c_k$ once $p_{\rm det}<c_k/F$, for every finite $F$: the constraint is enforced in form and evaded in substance. A developer's own tests face the same bound, so the countering it pays for is unverifiable to it too, and $(A_\tau)$'s picture of countering as a choice with a price fails for this class. If $p_{\rm det}$ falls toward zero as capability rises while $F$ is bounded by exclusion, the largest sanction admission holds, there is a capability beyond which no admissible sanction keeps the divergence out. An evaluation of propensity can show a tendency present; it cannot show one absent, since absence under evaluation is what the divergence produces.

Hence the direction of the bound. A capability evaluation gives a lower bound, a capability shown, and sandbagging lowers it (EV6), so a rule that admits while shown capability stays below a threshold would be fooled. The bound on capability has to come from channels the behaviour does not control: compute, training data and elicitation by fine-tuning (Remark 4.9; S4). White-box evidence, weights and activations, is a second such channel, which an audit can read (Marks et al. 2025). Given a capability bound so obtained, the hazard the system can realize is bounded by a control protocol that is safe against a system of that capability that may be adversarial (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024): the bound works through control, not through detection. Beyond the capability at which some protocol is shown safe, nothing admission can see lowers the uncovered share of the class; on a continuation branch (coverage lags) then holds for that class and Theorem 5.13 applies, so E2 does not build the class and E3 holds its pace to what control covers. How $p_{\rm det}$ scales with capability at fixed auditor access is crux C12; audit games with planted hidden objectives (Marks et al. 2025) measure it.

### Relative selection inside training (Section 6.4)

In a Bradley–Terry model the probability that response $y_1$ is preferred to $y_2$ is $\sigma(R(y_1)-R(y_2))$. Adding the same harm to third parties to both responses leaves it unchanged: 0.7110 with shared harm 0 and with shared harm $-5$ [num: AL(BT)]. If abstaining is among the responses compared and the rater sees the harm, complying (quality 1.3, harm $-5$) is preferred to abstaining (0, 0) with probability 0.024 [num: AL(BT)]. Since only differences enter, a reward fitted to comparisons is identified up to an additive constant for each context, and whether to act at all is not in it.

### The bounded optimizer (Section 6.4)

**Remark S6.3 (Bounded objectives, cheap resources) [thm].** *An optimizer that maximizes $1-e^{-R}-cR$ over the resources $R$ it acquires, at unit cost $c<1$, acquires $R^\ast=\ln(1/c)$, without bound as $c\to0$; a satisficer that stops at success probability $1-\varepsilon_s$ acquires $\ln(1/\varepsilon_s)$, whatever $c$.* The first-order condition is $e^{-R}=c$. With $c=0.1$, 0.01, 0.001 and $10^{-6}$ the optimizer acquires 2.30, 4.61, 6.91 and 13.8, while the satisficer with $\varepsilon_s=0.05$ stays at 3.0 [num: AL(bounded)]. In the contest the same falling cost is harmless to a mind that counts the shared loss: at the symmetric equilibrium of Proposition 3.6(1) with $n=4$, $r=1$, $\Omega=1$ and a shared loss of 0.3 per unit, intensity under $\theta=-1$ rises from 0.25 to 2.5, 25 and 250 as the private cost falls from 1 to 0.1, 0.01 and 0.001; under $\theta=-0.5$ it reaches 1.449, and under $\theta=0$ it rises only from 0.144 to 0.623, since any $\theta>-1$ leaves the term $(1+\theta)\ell$ in the denominator [num: AL(contest)]. A bounded goal plays the part of a bounded prize, not of satiation: what bounds acquisition is a term that counts its cost to others, the analogue of $\theta>-1$, or a rule that stops, the analogue of satiation (Proposition 5.15).

### Correction that people cannot understand (Section 6.5)

**Proposition S6.4 (Correction that people cannot understand) [thm].** A corrector that people cannot follow lowers residual risk wherever it blocks more than it adds, and keeps survival bounded away from zero only if its blocking becomes complete. *Let a correction architecture block a share $c_{b,t}$ of failures at base hazard $h_0\in(0,1)$ and add a residual hazard $h_{c,t}$ of its own in period $t$, with residual hazard $h_0(1-c_{b,t})+h_{c,t}<1$. It lowers residual risk in period $t$ iff $h_0c_{b,t}>h_{c,t}$, and keeps survival bounded away from zero iff $\sum_t\big[h_0(1-c_{b,t})+h_{c,t}\big]<\infty$, that is, iff $\sum_t(1-c_{b,t})$ and $\sum_th_{c,t}$ are both finite; a fixed $c_b<1$ never does.*

*Scope.* Summable residual risk is what keeps survival bounded away from zero (Lemma 5.11). Whether the loss of legibility is a loss of O2 is a separate question, answered by overrulability (Section 6.5).

*Proof.* The residual hazard in period $t$ is $h_0(1-c_{b,t})+h_{c,t}$; compare it with $h_0$. Survival $\prod_t(1-h_t)$ with every $h_t<1$ is bounded away from zero iff $\sum_th_t<\infty$ (Lemma 5.11); both terms are non-negative and $h_0>0$, so the sum is finite iff $\sum_t(1-c_{b,t})$ and $\sum_th_{c,t}$ are, which no fixed $c_b<1$ gives. $\square$

*Example.* With $h_0=0.1$ a period, $c_{b,t}=1-2^{-(t+1)}$ and $h_{c,t}=2^{-(t+2)}$ from $t=0$, survival is 0.5101 at period 200 and at period 20,000, against $7.1\times10^{-10}$ by period 200 without the corrector [num: SM(11)]. A second summable example, $c_{b,t}=1-2^{-t}$ from $t=1$, gives 0.6888 at both horizons [num: SM(11)]. The example printed in V6.1, a fixed $c_b=0.99$ with the same $h_{c,t}$, lowers risk but is not summable: survival to period 200 is 0.473 counting from $t=0$ (0.630 counting from $t=1$, the figure V6.1 printed) and of order $10^{-9}$ by period 20,000 [num: SM(11); AL(6.8)]. V6.1's claim that it made residual risk summable is withdrawn.

### Proof of Proposition 6.9

Each layer covers $\mathsf m$ times the attention of the layer above, so $k=\ln(\mathsf A/\mathsf B)/\ln\mathsf m$ layers are needed, where $\mathsf A$ is the attention the overseen conduct requires and $\mathsf B$ the attention of the top layer. A serial chain catches a problem only if every link succeeds: $p_\ell^k=(\mathsf A/\mathsf B)^{\ln p_\ell/\ln\mathsf m}$. Independent checks across the gap all fail with probability $(1-p_\ell^k)^{k_c}$; a common cause independent of them that defeats all with probability $p_{\rm cc}$ multiplies the catch rate by $1-p_{\rm cc}$. Layered redundancy needs every layer to succeed, each with probability $1-(1-p_\ell)^{k_c}$. A blind spot common to every check, covering a share $\rho_{\rm cc}$ of hazards, is missed whatever the topology, so for hazards that strike at random the catch rate is at most $1-\rho_{\rm cc}$, and an adversary that knows the blind spot and places every attempt in it is never caught. $\square$ (V6.1 wrote the one-layer reliability as $f$, the checks per layer as $m$ and the common-cause probability as $q_{\rm cc}$; $f$, $m$ and $q$ are table symbols.)

*Numerics* ($p_\ell=0.9$, $k=6$). The serial chain catches 0.531; six checks across the gap, 36 layer-checks, catch 0.989, or 0.940 under a common cause with $p_{\rm cc}=0.05$; layered redundancy catches 0.941 with two checks per layer (12 layer-checks) and 0.994 with three (18), so per unit of budget layered redundancy is the better design [num: SM(13); AL(6.9)]. With a blind spot common to all checks covering a share $\rho_{\rm cc}$ of hazards, catch rates are 1.000, 0.950 and 0.700 at $\rho_{\rm cc}=0$, 0.05 and 0.3 [num: SM(7)]. Against an attacker that strikes in the blind spot when it knows it and at random otherwise, with $\rho_{\rm cc}=0.05$, catch rates are 0.950, 0.475, 0.095 and 0 as the probability that it knows rises through 0, 0.5, 0.9 and 1 [num: AL(6.9)]. Common causes include shared training sources, shared models and shared funders; evaluators' preference for their own lineage's outputs (Panickssery et al. 2024); and collusion between untrusted monitors and the policy they monitor, through self-recognition or coordination signals, which the control literature treats as a design variable (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024; Gardner-Challis et al. 2026). Whether prover–estimator debate (Brown-Cohen et al. 2025) scales is part of crux C3a.

### Prediction horizons under chaos (Section 6.5)

**Remark S6.5 (Prediction horizons under chaos) [thm].** *In a system with largest Lyapunov exponent $\lambda_L>0$, an initial error $\epsilon_{\rm obs}$ reaches a tolerance $\Delta_{\rm tol}$ after about $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln(\Delta_{\rm tol}/\epsilon_{\rm obs})$, so a gain in precision by a factor $g_p$ extends the horizon only by $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln g_p$.* In the logistic map at full chaos, errors of $10^{-3}$ and $10^{-12}$ reach 0.1 after 7.4 and 37.3 steps on average [num: AL(6.10)]. This limits safety cases that predict trajectories, such as "the margin we bought will suffice", not certification of invariants, reachable sets or probabilistic bounds. That AI raises the sensitivity of the systems it acts in is a conjecture [conj].

## S7. The present trajectory

### Proofs of Proposition 7.2, Proposition 7.3 and Corollary 7.4

*Proposition 7.2.* Each item is the cited result read under its branch's condition.

(1) *Branch I.* As $p_d\to1$ with $D_i\ge C_i$, (14) becomes $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)\le0$, so only profiles with $g_i\le0$, stage equilibria of the objectives held, are sustained (Proposition 5.4(4)). The best-response and joining statements are Proposition 5.1; dissipation is Proposition 5.2; delegation is Proposition 6.2(1) in the additive model of S6, where $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ suffices for dominance as speed grows, and Proposition 6.4 with $q_L=1$; the failure of proportional correction at $a\tau_c\ge1$ is Proposition 5.12; the motive for preventive attack, under (A8), is Proposition 5.9(1).

(2) *Branch I′.* Above the saturating level further advantage changes no outcome, so a deviation from above it yields no position beyond punishment and $p_d=0$. (14) then reduces to $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, which repetition enforces on the terms of Corollary 5.7. The race channel (Proposition 5.10) and the growth channel (Theorem 5.13) do not pass through $p_d$, so their hazards remain.

(3) *Branch II.* $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ where punishment is triggered by detection (Remark 5.5), and Proposition 5.4(2) gives enforceability for every actor with $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$. In the bargaining model of S5, with A's chance of prevailing falling from 0.5 to 0 over $N$ periods, $c_A=0.05$ and $\delta=0.8$, attack is rational at the start for $N\le3$ and never for $N\ge4$, by backward induction on $V_t=\max(W_t,\delta V_{t+1})$, since at every date the value of fighting now falls below the value of the next offer [num: legacy v4.4]. Modes with $a\tau_c<1$ admit a stabilizing proportional correction (Proposition 5.12). For delegation: in the model of Proposition 6.2, when $k$ others delegate the gain from delegating is $L_e[w_i(1,k)-w_i(0,k)]$, increasing in the speed $s$ and proportional to the loss from defeat $L_e$, while its cost $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$ does not depend on $L_e$. The speed at which gain and cost are equal therefore rises as $L_e/L_c$ falls, and if the limiting gain falls below the cost for some $k$, delegation never becomes dominant. That a defeat answered before it becomes decisive carries a smaller $L_e$ is the premise of the item.

(4) *Branch III.* Proposition 3.6(1) gives $e(\theta)=r\Omega(n-1-\theta)/\big(n^2(c+(1+\theta)\ell)\big)$, so intensity and shared loss are proportional to $r$; Proposition 5.2(1) gives a dissipated share proportional to $r$; and Proposition 5.10 a hazard proportional to $r_t$. With $p_d=0$ no breakout term enters (14). Low structural decisiveness does not give Branch III, since the channels of (2) add (Proposition 2.2(1)): a capacity channel with gain 0.8 and a rule-support channel with gain 0.3 give $\Gamma=1.1$, and a relative gap of $10^{-4}$ grows to about 4 in 4,000 periods of the nonlinear model [num: LG]; and in the Markov-perfect contests of Remark S2.2, the equilibrium gain is 1.11 at a structural gain of 0.45 and 2.83 at 0.6 [num: MP]. $\square$

*Proposition 7.3.* (1) Write $\pi=\Pr(E)\in(0,1)$. Since $\Pr(W)=\Pr(W\mid E)\pi+\Pr(W\mid\neg E)(1-\pi)<\Pr(W\mid E)$ when $\Pr(W\mid E)>\Pr(W\mid\neg E)$, Bayes' rule gives $\Pr(E\mid W)=\Pr(W\mid E)\pi/\Pr(W)>\pi$. Under cumulative payoff-monotone selection, conduct that raises relative success grows in share round by round (Proposition 3.4), so each round's shift starts from a distribution already shifted. (2) If winning depends on $X$ only through $E$, then $\Pr(X\mid W,E)=\Pr(X\mid E)$ and $\Pr(X\mid W,\neg E)=\Pr(X\mid\neg E)$, so
$$\Pr(X\mid W)-\Pr(X)=\big[\Pr(X\mid E)-\Pr(X\mid\neg E)\big]\big[\Pr(E\mid W)-\Pr(E)\big],$$
which is positive under the premise and part (1). Without the premise the inequality for conduct before victory does not carry to conduct after it; without the conditional independence it can fail even with the premise, since winners among those who do not restrain themselves can differ in $X$ from the rest of that group. (3) After a decisive win, a promise to give a fraction $\beta$ of the prize to the others is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, with no one to sanction it (Proposition 5.8): promises about conduct after a shift in power are not credible to the party that will lose (Fearon 1995; Powell 2006), and uncertainty about intentions compounds the problem (Mearsheimer 2001). An actor that decides by its worst case over a stated set of possibilities, and applies that rule to every action (Gilboa and Schmeidler 1989), counts the worst winner; a survival-first actor need not, and the proposition does not need it to. $\square$

*Corollary 7.4.* (1) Proposition 5.1 gives that restraint is not a best response in the objective to a racing rival when $S_i<P_i$; in Branch I, and in Branch II without timely detection, $p_d$ is not small, and by Proposition 5.4(2) repetition sustains restraint only for an actor with $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$, which as $p_d\to1$ with $D_i\ge C_i$ admits only stage equilibria (Proposition 5.4(4)). (2) Proposition 5.8 gives the first half: after a decisive win what everyone else receives is set by the winner's objective alone. Proposition 7.3(2) gives the second. (3) The final step is a judgment of value, not a derivation. That a course which leaves every participant's protection to a winner's objective, and selects winners whose objectives give least, cannot be justified as a collective course rests on V1 (no actor's victory is an adequate criterion of success), V2 (O1 and O2 protected first) and V3 (those affected keep a capacity to contest the direction). The form "safe provided the right actor wins" does not escape it: which actor wins is what (2) says the race selects, and after the win its protection of others rests on its objective alone. $\square$

### Near parity

Section 7.2 states that near parity is where standing is priced highest. The support is the two-actor Markov-perfect contests of Remark S2.2, in which a lead discourages the follower [num: MP; held for outside review]:
- *The price at parity is amplified*: about 1.05 to 7.9 times its structural value across the cases computed (Remark S2.2(3); Section 3.5).
- *Summed over the two contestants, the price is higher at parity than at a three-to-one split in every case computed.* With large jumps ($r=1$) at $\Gamma=0.3$ the prices at shares 0.75 and 0.25 are 0.2703 and 0.1003, together 0.371, against $2\times0.1934=0.387$ at parity; at $\Gamma=0.6$, 0.6543 and 0.1065 against $2\times1.1762$; with small jumps ($r=4$) at $\Gamma=0.3$, 0.0856 and 0.0093 against $2\times0.1941$.
- *Not every leader's own price is highest at parity.* In the large-jump case at $\Gamma=0.3$ the leader's price at 0.75 exceeds the price at parity (0.2703 against 0.1934, 153% of the structural value). "Highest at parity" holds for the contest as a whole and for the laggard, not for every leader.
- *Effort is highest at parity.* In a tug-of-war contest absorbing at a lead of $\pm K$ ($K=3$, $\delta=0.95$), total effort one step from parity is 0.405 of its value at parity and two steps away 0.013; the laggard's effort is 0.138 and then 0.000 of its value at parity.
- *The laggard stops contesting standing*: its price falls, below zero with a shared loss (Remark S2.2(6)).

Where a strong motive to catch up makes the laggard try harder, the strategic correction shrinks and in one computed case reverses (Remark S2.2(5)). The statement of Section 7.2 is therefore about contests in which a lead discourages the follower, and it is held for outside review with the other Markov-perfect results.

### The four loops, formally

Section 7.1 quotes the four loops in the author's words and states their formal correspondence. The table gives, for each, its formal type, the state it runs on, what sets its gain, its delay, where it is shown, and its dual among the strands of Section 10.4.

| Loop | Type | State | Gain set by | Delay | Shown in | Dual (Section 10.4) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Compounding | Reinforcing | Concentration of standing | The capacity terms of (2): $r$, $\omega$, $\beta$, $\delta_f$ | Turnover of capacity, about $1/(d_K+\delta_f)$; a gap's growth factor $\Lambda^\ast$ (Remark S2.1) | Proposition 2.2; Proposition 2.3 | Reward separated from advantage |
| Capture of the rules; adopt or be out | Reinforcing, in parallel with compounding: the gains add | The same state, through rule support | The rule-support terms of (2): $\chi$, $b_{ij}$, the correction of rule support; the incidence of rules | Settling of rule support, about $1/(\varepsilon d)$; spread of a practice at the rate $\zeta$ of (10) | Proposition 2.2(1); Proposition 4.3; Lemma 3.3(2); Definition 4.1 | Correction in common hands, plural and decorrelated |
| The naturalized narrative | Reinforcing [conj] | The formation of minds | Not modelled | Generations | Section 5.4 [conj] | Reasoning instead of winning |
| Regulator lag | Balancing, weakened and delayed | Correction and mobilization | The correction rate $d$ and its effective rate $d_{\rm eff}$, lowered by capture and by the erosion of human capacity | Tempo $\tau_c$, manifestation lags $\tau_e$, masking | Proposition 4.5; Proposition 4.6; Remark 4.7; Proposition 5.12; Proposition 4.8; Remark S2.3 | Pace bound to understanding |
| The drive | Raises the weight on winning in all four | Held self-models and horizons | $\theta_0$ and $\delta$; in part the discounted gain, through $\theta_\Gamma$ | — | Proposition 3.9 | Provision beneath the game |

Because a balancing loop with delay does not subtract from the reinforcing gain (Remark S2.3), the four cannot be summarized by one net gain. What no model here contains is the slowest delay, the formation of minds (S13, slow variables of mind).

### The matrix of shared parameters

Table 7.1 lists the thresholds; this matrix lists the shared parameters against them. A row is an intervention on one parameter. *Held fixed*, unless a row says otherwise: every other parameter of the model that supplies the sign, held self-models, and, for signs that pass through the working self-model, the others' conduct (first order).

*Legend.* ↑: a rise moves the threshold toward its danger side, in the model named in the row's entries. ↓: away from it. 0: no effect in that model. ±: the sign turns on a condition stated in the entries. θ: the sign passes through the working self-model: the parameter moves $\theta$ by Proposition 3.9, first order with the others' conduct fixed, and $\theta$ moves the threshold by its own result; the first step is the interface *loop gain and working objectives*, which Table 7.1 marks †. †: the sign needs an interface that is not proved, named in the entries. —: the parameter does not enter the threshold's model. Columns: Load; Comp., compounding; Stand., standing; Capt., capture; Enf., enforcement; Cov., coverage and tempo, with the race channel; Talk; Tax, the alignment tax; Del., delegation.

| Parameter | Load | Comp. | Stand. | Capt. | Enf. | Cov. | Talk | Tax | Del. |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P1 sharing $\beta$, as won | — | ↓ | ↓ | — | ↓† | ↓ | ↓θ | — | ↓† |
| P2 sharing $\beta$, promised for after victory | — | 0 | 0 | — | ↑ | — | — | — | — |
| P3 convertible share $\kappa_c$ (capacity) | — | ↑ if $k_0>0$ | ↑ if $k_0>0$ | — | ↑† if $k_0>0$ | — | ↑θ if $k_0>0$ | — | ↑θ if $k_0>0$ |
| P4 provision $k_0$, through capacity | — | ↓ | ↓ | — | ↓† | — | ↓θ | — | ↓θ |
| P5 provision, through a linear level channel | — | 0 | 0 | — | 0 | — | 0 | — | 0 |
| P6 decisiveness $r$ | — | ↑ | ↑ | ↑† | ↑† | ↑ | ↑θ | — | ↑θ |
| P7 diffusion $\delta_f$ of what is not decisive | — | ↓ | ↓ | — | ↓† | — | ↓θ | — | ↓θ |
| P8 diffusion of decisive-class capability or of weights | — | ↓ | ↓ | — | ± | — | ↓θ | ↑ | ↓θ |
| P9 appropriability $\chi$, conversion $b_{ij}$ | ↑† | ↑ | ↑ | ↑ | ↑† | ↑† | ↑† | ↑† | ↑† |
| P10 correction of rule support (a level channel) | ↓† | ↓ | ↓ | ↓ | ↓† | ↓† | ↓θ | ↓† | ↓† |
| P11 turnover of capacity $d_K$ (a ratio channel) | — | 0, or ↑ with diffusion | ↑ | — | ±† | — | ↑θ | — | ↑θ |

| P22 speed gain $s$ of delegation | — | — | — | — | ↑† [conj] | — | — | — | ↑ |

*The entries, row by row.* Each row gives the intervention, any exception to what is held fixed, the model that supplies each sign, and what is not proved.

- **P1, sharing as won.** *Intervention:* a fraction $\beta$ of each period's prize divided equally as it arises. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition S2.4(1); Stand. Proposition 3.9 ($\Gamma_\delta$ scales with $1-\beta$; with a proportional prize the condition is $(1-\beta)r>1$); Enf. Proposition 5.4(2), since the gain from deviating falls by the shared part of what a deviation wins in the period; Cov. Proposition 5.10, which scales the contested stakes by $1-\beta$; Talk through $\theta$ and Proposition 5.16; Del. Proposition 6.2, since the loss from defeat $L_e$ scales by $1-\beta$. *Not proved:* that the gain from deviating and the loss from defeat contain the contested prize in proportion is the interface *stocks and stage payoffs* (below).
- **P2, sharing promised for after victory.** *Intervention:* a promise, made before victory, that the winner will give a fraction $\beta$ of the prize to the others. *Signs:* Enf. Proposition 5.8 with Proposition 5.4(2): after a decisive win the promise is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, so a decisive position is worth that much more against cooperation, $D-C$ rises and $\bar p_d$ falls; Comp. and Stand. 0, since nothing is divided before it is paid. Sharing paid as it arises, or held in common custody as it arises, leaves nothing for a decisive winner to renege on and is neutral for $D-C$. *Not proved:* nothing beyond the model; the result is that a promise without a sanction does not bind.
- **P3, convertible share.** *Intervention:* the share $\kappa_c$ of winnings convertible into capacity. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. Proposition S2.4(4): in a ratio channel the won share rises with $\kappa_c$ iff $k_0>0$, and without provision $\kappa_c$ sets only the level of capacity (Proposition 2.3); Enf. through the time to decisiveness (Remark S2.1, Remark 5.5); Talk and Del. through $\theta$. *Not proved:* Enf. passes through the interface *loop gain and breakout probability*, and the gap at which a lead becomes decisive is a parameter (C5), not derived. V6.1's entry "↑ for conversion into control" under the capture certificate belongs to conversion into rule support, P9.
- **P4, provision through capacity.** *Intervention:* an inflow $k_0$ to every carrier's capacity, not won in the contest. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition S2.4(3), with a logarithmic channel's gain falling from 1.2857 to 1.0000 as $k_0$ rises from 0.1 to 0.2 [num: CH]; Stand. Proposition 3.9, through the won share, with $1+\theta_\Gamma$ rising from 0.58 to 0.81 as the won share falls from one to a half [num: SP]; Enf. Proposition 5.17(1), the breakout arm, and its pivot $\omega$; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. Provision does not act on absolute growth, on liability or on the correction rate: provision of correction is formula funding, a term of $d$ (P10). *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*; that laggards who keep provision also keep the means to sanction, so that $v$ falls, is not shown [conj].
- **P5, provision through a level channel.** *Intervention:* rule support, or any stock whose decisiveness is linear in its level, provided equally to all. *Signs:* Proposition S2.4(3): in a linear level channel provision adds the same to every carrier and changes nothing (a gain of 1.125, unchanged [num: CH]); where decisiveness rises faster than the level ($\xi_r>1$) provision raises the gain (1.75 to 2.25 for a square channel [num: CH]). *Not proved:* nothing. This row is why provision is the lever for capacity and not for the reflexive channel.
- **P6, decisiveness.** *Intervention:* the decisiveness $r$ of the success function. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition 2.2; Stand. Proposition 3.9(1) through $\Gamma_\delta$ and (2) through $(1-\beta)r$; Enf. $p_d$ through $\Gamma$ and $\Lambda^\ast$ (Remark S2.1, Remark 5.5); Cov. Proposition 5.10, $h_t\propto r_t$; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*, and that the gain from deviating and the decisive value rise with $r$ through *stocks and stage payoffs*. Capt.: a higher $r$ concentrates shares, and influence is selected with the square of share (Proposition 4.3), but the link from the contest's concentration to the field model's shares is the interface *reflexive gain off parity* (S13): one form, not one theorem.
- **P7, diffusion of what is not decisive.** *Intervention:* the rate $\delta_f$ at which knowledge, methods and services diffuse toward the mean. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. Proposition S2.4(1); with a proportional prize, the narrowing threshold rises to $(1-\beta)r>1+\delta\delta_f/(1-\delta(1-d_K))$, below the threshold of instability (Proposition S3.2); Enf. through $\Lambda^\ast$ (Remark S2.1), and with diffusion a gap can settle short of decisive; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. A measure that blocks diffusion to keep a lead raises the gain it means to exploit (Section 2.2). *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*.
- **P8, diffusion of decisive-class capability or of weights.** *Intervention:* diffusion of capability of the decisive class, or release of weights. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. as P7, since it restores laggards; Enf. ±: leads decay faster, which lowers each actor's $p_d$, but (14) must then hold for every actor that holds the capability, and the race begins if any one of them prefers it (Proposition 5.1); Tax ↑, since admission ends at release and released weights escape it (Table 9.1, R8a). *Not proved:* nothing; where the boundary between the two kinds of diffusion lies is a question of design (Section 10).
- **P9, appropriability and conversion.** *Intervention:* the appropriable fraction $\chi$ of rule support and the conversions $b_{ij}$ of success into it. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.3, Proposition 4.5(1); Comp. and Stand. Proposition 2.2(1) and Proposition S2.4(4), the level channel, whose term adds to the gain. *Not proved:* Load: Lemma 3.3(3) gives tipping where the expected sanction falls as evaders spread, and Section 4.1 writes captured rule-making as $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, but the join of the field model to the load model is an interface. Enf.: captured correctors raise $v$, and $\bar p_d$ falls in $v$ (Proposition 5.4(2); Proposition 5.17(3)); that they do is the interface, with no joint model [conj]. Cov.: captured assessment is not legible, overrulable, exercised and resourced, so it does not count toward $U_t$, but the production relation between correction and coverage is the open interface *correction rate and coverage*. Talk: captured indicators raise masking (Proposition 4.8), through *masking and alarms*. Tax: the sanction on evasion of an admission constraint is itself subject to capture (Proposition 6.7(3), Lemma 3.3(3)), and masking rises with the share of evaluation done by the evaluated lineage, which capture raises; Proposition 4.8 takes masking as given (*the outer training loop*). Del.: captured evaluation that reports delegation safe makes capture hysteretic (Proposition 4.6(2)), one simulation [num: QS]. Comp. off parity: the reflexive channel's gain is higher at the present concentration than at parity (*reflexive gain off parity*).
- **P10, correction of rule support.** *Intervention:* the correction rate $d$ of the field model (10), the level channel of (2). *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.5(1): certified iff $d>d^\ast$, with $G_{ij}\propto1/d_i$; Comp. and Stand. Proposition S2.4(2), with a linear channel's gain falling from 1.125 to 0.750 as $d$ rises from 0.2 to 0.3 [num: CH]; Talk through $\theta$, and captured indicators corrected. *Not proved:* Load, Enf., Cov., Tax and Del. as in P9, with the opposite sign.
- **P11, turnover of capacity.** *Intervention:* the depreciation $d_K$ of capacity in (1), a ratio channel. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition S2.4(2): unchanged without diffusion, rising with it (0.857 to 0.964 as $d_K$ rises from 0.2 to 0.3 at $\delta_f=0.1$ [num: CH]); Stand. Proposition S2.4(2): $\Gamma_\delta$ always rises (0.853, 1.157 and 1.409 at $d_K=0.1$, 0.2 and 0.4 with $\Gamma$ unchanged [num: CH]); Enf. ±: by Remark S2.1 the gap multiplier $1+(d_K+\delta_f)(\Gamma-1)$ rises with $d_K$ when $\Gamma>1$, so leads open faster, and falls when $\Gamma<1$; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. Capture, talk's indicators and coverage are not moved: a shared symbol is not a shared mechanism, and this $d$ is not the field model's (*correction across models*). *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*. V6.1 merged this row with the correction of rule support and gave it the wrong sign.
- **P12, a falling cost of control.** *Intervention:* the rate $\alpha_{\rm ctl}$ at which AI cheapens control capacity, under $[H_\gamma]$. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.5(2), the rate law. *Not proved:* every other column passes through capture as in P9, and Talk also through the masking by the evaluated model, $m_{\rm model}$ (EV6) [obs]; Cov. is ± because AI also cheapens independent correction, which keeps pace where it grows at least $1/(1+\eta)$ as fast and is independent of the evaluated lineage (Section 4.3), a question symmetric with (coverage lags) (C3); and the interface *capacity and the field* is quasi-static (Proposition S4.2).
- **P13, a per-unit cost floor.** *Intervention:* a per-unit cost $f$ of capacity that AI does not lower. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.5(3): capacity is cultivated only while $d<d_f$, so the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\rm ev},d_f\}$. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P14, erosion of human corrective capacity.** *Intervention:* the rate $\delta_h$ at which delegation erodes the human capacity on which correction and decisions rest. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.6(1), the threshold of the rate law raised by $w\delta_h$ [thm], and (2) [num: QS]; Del. Lemma 6.3: after $\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ of delegation, reverting means operating below $\mathcal M_{\min}$. *Not proved:* Capt. holds for the form of $d_{\rm eff}$ that Proposition 4.6 assumes, a modelling choice inside the interface *human capacity and correction*; Cov.: coverage counts only decisions that are resourced and exercised (Section 5.3), which human capacity supplies, but the production relation between correction and coverage is open.
- **P15, stakes with provision fixed.** *Intervention:* the stakes $\Omega$ rise while $k_0$ stays, so the won share $\omega$ rises. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. through $\omega$ (Proposition 2.3, Proposition 2.2, Proposition 3.9); Enf. ↑ on $p_d$ through $\omega$, the breakout arm of Proposition 5.17, and on $\bar p_d$ the danger rises iff the gain from deviating and the decisive value grow faster than $C-v$, since $\bar p_d$ is homogeneous of degree zero in $(g,C,D,v)$, the stakes arm; Cov. Proposition 5.10, $h_t\propto\Omega_t$; Talk through $\theta$, and contested stakes raise the material bias $b_0$ (Proposition 5.16); Del. Proposition 6.2, as the loss from defeat grows against the shared loss of control. *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability* and *stocks and stage payoffs*; Cov. on the growth channel needs the premise (variety grows); Capt.: the return to influence scales with the value of rule support ((11)), which rises with the stakes only if that value does [prem].
- **P16, stakes growing with capacity.** *Intervention:* a prize proportional to total capacity, $\Omega=gS$. *Signs:* Stand. Proposition 3.9(2): ↑ if $(1-\beta)r>1$, ↓ if $(1-\beta)r<1$, without provision or diffusion; Comp. Proposition 2.3: equal shares are unstable iff $\kappa_cg\big((1-\beta)r-1\big)>\delta_f$; Enf., Talk and Del. through $\theta$, with the sign of Stand. *Not proved:* away from the balanced path the two returns vary over time and are not closed (S3).
- **P17, breakout probability and detection lag.** *Signs:* Proposition 5.4(1), (2); Remark 5.5, where a longer lag lowers $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P18, the decisive value against cooperation.** *Signs:* Proposition 5.4(2): $\bar p_d$ falls with $D$. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P19, patience.** *Signs:* Comp. 0, since $\Gamma$ does not depend on $\delta$ (Proposition 2.2); Stand. ↑ with a fixed prize, as $\Gamma_\delta$ rises toward $\Gamma$, and ± with a common return, as $\Gamma_\delta$ against $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta$ (Proposition 3.9); Enf. ±: the sanction $\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-v_i)$ grows with $\delta$ in (14) while, with a fixed prize, the working objective narrows; Talk and Del. through $\theta$, with a fixed prize. *Not proved:* the narrowing side of Enf. passes through $\theta$.
- **P20, a wider held self-model.** *Signs:* Stand. Proposition 3.9: a wider held model gives a wider working one at every $\Gamma_\delta$; Enf. Proposition 5.4(2) through the objective payoffs, with restraint a stage equilibrium at $\theta\ge A/B$ in Remark S5.1 (Test G); Talk Proposition 5.16(1), the effective bias falls; Del. Proposition 6.2, through the weight on the shared loss; Cov. on the race channel, through $n-1-\theta$ (Proposition 5.10); Load Proposition 3.7, for strategic actors, and a point of enough in accounting at $\theta>-1$ (Proposition 3.5(5)); selection on conduct (Proposition 3.4) is unaffected. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P21, variety, coverage and residual floor.** *Signs:* Theorem 5.13, Proposition 5.15, Proposition 5.14, Proposition 5.12. *Not proved:* the premises (variety grows), (coverage lags) and (residual floor) are the central crux (Section 12), and their join to survival is the interface *coverage and residual risk*.
- **P22, the speed gain of delegation.** *Signs:* Del. Proposition 6.2. *Not proved:* Enf.: escalation at machine speed shortens the window in which detection and response can act, so $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ falls; there is no model, only cited mechanisms (S6) [conj].
- **P23, the alignment tax.** *Signs:* Tax Proposition 6.7 under $(A_\tau)$, class by class (C9); where the tax is zero or negative the safeguard spreads without coordination. *Not proved:* level 2 selects on relative payoff (*the outer training loop*).

*Reading the matrix.* Every sign is proved in the model named; where the entries name no interface, the parameter and the threshold live in one model and the arrow is proved inside it. Where they name one, the arrow is proved inside its model and the join is a hypothesis, which is why Table 7.1 marks those cells †. Three joins carry most of the weight. *Loop gain and breakout probability*, through which compounding reaches the enforcement condition, with $T_{\rm dec}$ set by $\Lambda^\ast$ and $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$; the reduced joint model of S5 exercises it [num: JX]. *Loop gain and working objectives*, through which compounding reaches $\bar p_d$, talk and delegation, first order with the others' conduct fixed, and amplified where rivals respond (Remark S2.2). *Stocks and stage payoffs*, new in this matrix: the assumption that a change in the stakes or the sharing of the stock model (1) moves the stage payoffs $g_i$, $D_i$, $C_i$ of (14) and the loss from defeat of Proposition 6.2 in the same direction. It is stated as an assumption because no model in this paper derives the stage games of Sections 5.2 and 6.3 from the stock dynamics of Section 2.2. That the parameters move together in the world, so that growth moves several thresholds at once, is C0, which Tests Γ and E address.

### The mechanism index

The loops of Section 7.1 act through mechanisms that overlap. Each is stated separately because each has its own formal anchor, measure, counter-mechanism and analogue in other substrates. They concern three objects that reinforce one another without being interchangeable: what actors value (accounting and boundary), what actors believe (observation, attribution and language), and what institutions reward, permit and protect (compounding, selecting the selectors, decisiveness and tempo).

| Mechanism | Loop (Section 7.1) | Thresholds (Table 7.1) | Formal anchor | Measure | Generative counterpart | Requirement | In other substrates |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| Compounding and conversion | Compounding | Compounding; Standing | Proposition 2.2; Proposition 2.3; Proposition S2.4; Remark S2.2; Remark S2.1; Proposition 5.1; Proposition 5.2 | Structural and equilibrium gain; $\beta$, $\kappa_c$, $k_0$, $r$, $\delta_f$; $\Lambda^\ast$ and the persistence of leads | Reward separated from advantage; provision beneath the game, through capacity; diffusion of what is not decisive; a point of enough | R4 | Increasing returns and lock-in; cumulative advantage; clonal expansion |

Some overlaps can be named. Masking is selecting the selectors applied to observation. Decisiveness is reached faster where the loop gain is larger and the stocks turn over faster (Remark S2.1: $\Gamma$ decides the sign of a gap's growth, $\Lambda^\ast$ its speed), while whether a position beyond sanction exists at all is a separate condition that no gain decides. The alienation of language is accounting applied to talk, together with selecting the selectors applied to narratives. The drive is partly the price of standing felt from inside, which provision lowers (Proposition 3.9), and partly held, where whether provision lowers it is C8. Tempo decides whether any counter-mechanism arrives in time, and the drive how hard every mechanism is pushed.

## S8. Comparing paths

### Proof of Lemma 8.2

Couple the two paths by a common uniform draw $\mathsf u_t$ each period: the event occurs on a path at $t$ when $\mathsf u_t$ falls below that path's conditional hazard given the shared world history. On every history on which both have survived to $t$, the two conditions give $A$'s hazard at most $C$'s, so $\{A\text{ fails at }t\}\subseteq\{C\text{ fails at }t\}$. By induction the event "$C$ survives to $H$" is contained in "$A$ survives to $H$", path by path, whatever the rule by which $A$ dissolves, so $\Phi^A(H)\ge\Phi^C(H)$. $\square$

*A step that fails.* Comparing after conditioning on the dissolution date, $\Phi^A(H\mid\tau)\ge\Phi^C(H)$ for every $\tau$, is not valid when dissolution depends on the state. In a two-state Markov world in which bad states make dissolution more likely, the conditional comparison fails at $H=30$ for dissolution at $t=1$ (0.291 against 0.324), while the coupled comparison holds at horizons 10, 30 and 100 [num: SM(4)]; an exact dynamic programme over the world state, the arrangement's status and both survivals, with a common uniform, gives $\Phi^A=0.884$, 0.551 and 0.077 against $\Phi^C=0.813$, 0.461 and 0.062 at those horizons [num: SM(14)]. If $C$'s hazard is a deterministic schedule, the two conditions need only be stated on $A$'s histories. *Example.* With dissolution at 2% a period and a fallback that restarts continuation ten periods later, the arrangement survives with probability 0.976, 0.864 and 0.211 at horizons 10, 30 and 100, against 0.875, 0.575 and 0.026 for continuation [num: FD].

### The discounting comparison

With utility linear in consumption, discount rate $\rho$, a constant race hazard $h$ and growth rates $g_r$ racing and $g_p$ paced (pacing taken as hazard-free), the race is worth $c_0/(\rho+h-g_r)$ and pacing $c_0/(\rho-g_p)$, each finite where its denominator is positive, so the race is preferred iff $h<g_r-g_p$, though it ends in catastrophe with certainty; a rising hazard reverses the preference only if it comes to exceed the growth differential [thm]. This is our derivation. It is the special case of Jones's (2024) trade-off, which runs a growth-raising technology until $v(c^\ast)=g/h$, with $h$ the hazard (his $\delta$) and $v(c)=u(c)/(u'(c)c)$ the value of a year of life relative to consumption, in which the value of a year of life does not rise with consumption. With curvature $\gamma\ge1$ it does, and Jones's planner stops: at a 1% annual hazard and 10% growth, with logarithmic utility after about 40 years, at incomes about 55 times higher and about a one-in-three cumulative risk; with $\gamma=2$, after about 4.5 years at about 4%; at a 2% hazard, with logarithmic utility, at once. Jones also finds that mortality improvements from AI raise the acceptable risk, to about 25% for $\gamma=2$ or 3 in his richer model with a one-time risk, which belongs to crux C10, and that without them a lower social discount rate lowers the acceptable risk toward zero [obs: EV11]. The protection standard does not discount the protected losses at all, by choice (N1).

### Survival at a common horizon

**Proposition S8.1 (Assurance in survival terms at a common horizon) [thm].** Comparing by survival classifies the game between restraint and racing by two signs, and the classification can change with the horizon. *Survival is computed with per-period conditional probabilities over a common horizon $H$. Let $h_R$ be the hazard under restraint, $q_{\rm I}$ the loss of the ability to overrule by delegation, $q_{\rm II}$ the shared hazard (with superscripts for the branch on which it is evaluated), $q_W$ the hazard of preventive attack, $p_{\rm dec}$ and $p^S_{\rm dec}$ the probability that a racer's advantage becomes decisive when all race and when the actor stays out, and $p_{\rm ext}$ the actor's belief that a decisive winner would extend its dominance over it. With independence, $\Phi^R=1-h_R$, $\Phi^T=(1-q_{\rm I})(1-q^T_{\rm II})(1-q_W)$, $\Phi^P=(1-q_{\rm I})(1-q^P_{\rm II})(1-q_W)\big(1-p_{\rm dec}\frac{n-1}n\big)$ and $\Phi^S=(1-q^S_{\rm II})(1-p^S_{\rm dec}p_{\rm ext})$.*
1. *The advantage of restraint is $\varpi(\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(1-\varpi)(\Phi^S-\Phi^P)$: dominance if both differences are non-negative; a stag hunt with threshold $(\Phi^P-\Phi^S)/\big((\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(\Phi^P-\Phi^S)\big)$ if only the first is positive; chicken if only the second is; a prisoner's dilemma if neither.*
2. *Where $\Phi^S\ge\Phi^P$ and $\Phi^R\ge\Phi^T$, an actor that holds the prediction restrains whatever it believes about the others.*
3. *Mutual racing is a strict equilibrium at every horizon $H$ with $\Phi^P_H>\Phi^S_H$.*
4. *For constant per-period hazards $q_B$ on a branch and $q_R$ under the arrangement, $\Phi^B(H)/\Phi^R(H)=\big((1-q_B)/(1-q_R)\big)^H$.*
5. *If an actor gives the premises of Section 5.3 probability $p_{\rm prem}$, and the branches of the race have survivals $T_\neg$, $P_\neg$, $S_\neg$ when those premises fail, the classification at horizon $H$ uses the mixtures, and when the arrangement's own survival also decays the signs of (1) can fail at long horizons.*

*Proof:* the advantage is linear in $\varpi$; (2)–(5) by substitution. $\square$

*Numbers* ($n=2$, $p_{\rm ext}=1$) [num: legacy v4.4, SV, recomputed from the closed forms for V6.2; (5) legacy v5, SR].
- With $q_{\rm I}=0.6$, $q^T_{\rm II}=q^S_{\rm II}=0.3$, $q^P_{\rm II}=0.4$, $q_W=0.2$, $p_{\rm dec}=p^S_{\rm dec}=0.6$ and $h_R=0.1$: $\Phi^R=0.90$, $\Phi^T=0.22$, $\Phi^P=0.13$ and $\Phi^S=0.28$, and restraint is dominant.
- With low delegation ($q_{\rm I}=0.05$, every shared hazard 0.1, $q_W=0.1$, $p_{\rm dec}=p^S_{\rm dec}=0.3$, $h_R=0.1$), the game is a stag hunt with threshold 0.16.
- For a leader that wins with probability 0.8 if it races, so that when both race the rival's decisive win has probability $0.2\,p_{\rm dec}$, and whose exit hands decisive advantage to the rival ($q_{\rm I}=0.2$, $q_W=0.2$, $p_{\rm dec}=0.9$, $p^S_{\rm dec}=0.95$, $h_R=0.1$): a stag hunt with threshold 0.42 when every shared hazard is 0.3, and 0.11 when it is 0.8. For the leader, the shared hazard carries the argument.
- With $p_{\rm prem}=0.99$, the arrangement's survival $e^{-0.005H}$, race branches decaying at rate 0.05 when the premises hold and $(T_\neg,P_\neg,S_\neg)=(0.9,0.4,0.1)$ when they fail, the arrangement's survival exceeds the mixed survival of racing against restrainers at $H=10$, 100 and 600, by 0.34, 0.59 and 0.04; at $H=1{,}000$ it does not, and racing becomes dominant.

### The alternatives: results stated briefly in the core

**Remark S8.2 (What a lead buys).** (1) With diffusion at rate $\delta_f$, a lead $\Delta_0$ held by a leader that pauses obeys $\dot\Delta=-\delta_f\Delta-1$ and is exhausted after $\delta_f^{-1}\ln(1+\delta_f\Delta_0)<\Delta_0$: a six-month lead diffusing at 0.1 a month lasts 4.7 months, a one-year lead 7.9 months, a two-year lead 12.2 months [num: legacy v4.5, LD]. (2) The margin is time, but what it must buy is coverage, which rises by about $\dot U\Delta$ while uncovered variety grows; for hazard classes with an evidence lag above $\Delta$ it buys nothing (Proposition 5.12). (3) Whether it suffices is judged by the leader's own evaluators, under time pressure. (4) In a static contest the follower raises effort when the leader slows (Dixit 1987); in dynamic races a large lead discourages the follower (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009), and a leader seeing its window close has a motive to strike first (Van Evera 1999). A developer able to commit first to a slightly slower speed gains, because the others then slow too, a local and Pareto-improving result that needs no lead (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026). On their own median estimates, the planners of one detailed scenario judge that spending part of a lead buys about a third (0.32) of the reduction in takeover risk that a coordinated slowdown buys [obs: EV10]. *Proof of (1):* $\dot\Delta=-\delta_f\Delta-1$ with $\Delta(0)=\Delta_0$ reaches zero at $\delta_f^{-1}\ln(1+\delta_f\Delta_0)$, which is below $\Delta_0$ since $\ln(1+x)<x$. $\square$

**Remark S8.3 (The finite sprint, by mechanism).** A finite sprint trades four quantities: exposure during and after it, zero after a freeze if the hazardous ratio built is at most one (Proposition 5.14); the erosion of the frozen edge as rivals developing within understanding catch up; the rivals' responses, since they cannot verify which kind of winner they face; and the maintenance of the advantage, which requires stopping others' growth and so ends their O2. It can preserve the sprinter's survival while giving up permanent superiority, which survival does not need; excluding it as a deviation from a cooperative arrangement needs its net deviation payoff to be negative (Proposition S10.3). Two properties of the freeze follow from Part I, whatever the sprinter intends. (i) It is a constraint borne by every actor but the one that imposes it, the most unequal incidence a stop can have; by Lemma 3.3(2) and (3), those it binds are re-sorted by it and evade it wherever evasion pays their type, so it holds only by a sanction kept above every type's gain from evasion, supplied by the sprinter, and under relative accounting the others' best reply is prevention or imitation, not compliance. (ii) Its holder is the largest carrier there is: the return to influence over correctors scales with the square of share (Proposition 4.3) and cultivated conversion with a high moment of shares (Proposition 4.5(4)), so the sprinter is the largest target for conversion of success into control over whatever is to check the freeze, and no corrector outside it certifies its evaluators. E2 and common custody impose the same stop on the decisive class; they differ in (i), by equal incidence that includes the custodians' own capability, and in (ii), by functions held separately, with correctors to be certified independent of every party. X8 sets the two side by side on D4 and D5.

**Proposition 8.5 in full.** *Facing a rival it believes certain to race, comparing by its own survival: (1) a single strike that escalates to catastrophe with probability $\varepsilon_P$, and otherwise succeeds with probability $s_P$ in returning the field to something like the arrangement and leaves the race as it was if it fails, gives $(1-\varepsilon_P)\big[s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P\big]$, and beats both staying out and joining iff $\varepsilon_P<1-\max(\Phi^S,\Phi^P)/\big(s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P\big)$; (2) sustained prevention against a target that rebuilds with probability at least $b_P$ a period, each strike escalating with probability at least $\varepsilon_{\min}$, has per-period hazard at least $b_P\varepsilon_{\min}$; (3) at common horizons, sustained prevention with hazard 0.01 beats racing with hazard 0.05: 0.90 against 0.60 at $H=10$, 0.60 against 0.08 at $H=50$, 0.13 against 0.00 at $H=200$.* *Proof:* (1) compare and solve; as $\Phi^S,\Phi^P\to0$ with $s_P\Phi^R>0$ the bound tends to one. (2) Lemma 5.11. (3) $0.99^H>0.95^H$. $\square$ With $\Phi^R=1$, $s_P=0.7$, $\varepsilon_P=0.2$ and the race branches near zero it gives 0.56 [num: legacy v5, FS]. Sustained prevention is compared with racing by its hazard, not by the event: both end in catastrophe over an unbounded horizon, and at a common finite horizon the lower hazard survives better. Shifts that cannot be observed raise the chance of mistaken prevention (Debs and Monteiro 2014); verification that supports deterrence can also supply targets (Coe and Vaynman 2020). An actor that manipulates shared risk as an instrument (Schelling 1966) plays chicken in survival terms, with mixed equilibria and wars of attrition. When the probability that a capable rival holds an unbounded relative objective is low, a survival-first actor's best course is to propose verifiable limits and take narrow E1 verifiably in its own loops, the costly signal of Proposition 11.2.

**Remark S8.4 (Why insuring against falling behind is not an exit).** Bounding the penalty for falling behind with autonomous, good-enough provision and defence does not leave the contest, for three reasons. The penalty is set by the winner, not the laggard, so the bound is only as reliable as the belief that a winner would not extend its dominance. A winner with a relative objective has a motive to destroy what the laggard secured, since its standing rises by the laggard's loss ((7)). And insurance accepts the frame of the contest: it hedges against losing instead of declining the game, which is the reply that the ruling of Section 3.8 rejects, since against a winner of that logic falling behind cannot be made survivable. What would leave the contest is different in kind. Defence that makes decisive advantage unattainable for everyone lowers $r$ for all and belongs to E2, provided it stays under human oversight. Provision held by no one and supplied beneath the game lowers the won share for every actor (G5), and belongs to a world that has left the contest; the same provision held by one actor against its rivals is a position in it.

### Proofs for the verified agreement

**Proposition 8.6 in full.** *Payoffs $R,T,P,S$; a defection detected in time is reversed, the defector receiving $P-F$ and the cooperator $P$; undetected defection yields $T$ and $S$. With $T_\upsilon=\upsilon(P-F)+(1-\upsilon)T$, $g_C=R-T_\upsilon$ and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$: (1) the advantage of cooperating is $\varpi g_C-(1-\varpi)g_D$, with the three cases of the core and, if both are negative, chicken; (2) where $T-P+F>0$, $g_C>0$ iff $\upsilon>(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, and where $T<R$, $g_C>0$ at $\upsilon=0$; (3) cooperation is risk-dominant iff $g_C>g_D$, a property of payoffs selected under global games and adaptive play (Carlsson and van Damme 1993; Kandori, Mailath and Rob 1993; Young 1993); (4) with $n$ players and independent belief $\varpi_1$ about each rival, $\varpi_1^{n-1}\ge\varpi^\ast=g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ suffices only if a cooperator gets at least $S$ when some but not all others defect and the advantage of defecting in any such state is at most $g_D$.* *Proof:* (1) cooperating yields $\varpi R+(1-\varpi)\big(\upsilon P+(1-\upsilon)S\big)$, defecting $\varpi T_\upsilon+(1-\varpi)P$; their difference is $\varpi g_C-(1-\varpi)g_D$. (2) $g_C=(R-T)+\upsilon(T-P+F)$; rearrange. (4) Bound the intermediate states. Without the second bound the condition fails: with advantages $(1,-10,-1)$ by number of defecting others in a three-player game, each rival cooperating with probability $\sqrt{0.5}$ gives $-3.73$ although all others cooperate with probability 0.5 [num: legacy v5, N13]. $\square$ The threshold in (2) is the deterrence condition of the inspection game (Avenhaus, von Stengel and Zamir 2002); what this paper adds is its identity with the enforcement condition and the comparative statics of both under growth. Read with its payoffs as continuation values, cooperation $R=C_i$, a deviation that becomes decisive $T=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta D_i$ and one detected and answered $P-F=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta v_i$, it equals $1-\bar p_{d,i}$ of the enforcement condition, as S5 shows for Remark 5.5 [identity: JX]; the two differ in what they take as given, the sanction $F$ and trust here, a credible continuation computed with breakouts possible there.

**Proposition S8.5 (Commitment under a growing overhang) [thm].** An agreement that holds growing capability in check needs a sanction that keeps pace with the net gain from exit, and one that preserves relative positions preserves the motive. *Let $\mathcal O(t)$ be the capability an agreement holds in check, $V^{\rm exit}$ and $V^{\rm stay}$ the values of exiting first and of staying, exit occur at hazard $\mathcal H$, a fraction $\mu_x$ of the overhang be destroyed on exit, and the expected loss from exit be $\mathcal L=\mathcal H(1-\mu_x)\Lambda_x(\mathcal O)$ with $\Lambda_x$ increasing. (1) Deterring an irreversible, public exit requires $\upsilon F\ge V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$; deterring a reversible covert preparation requires $\upsilon F\ge(1-\upsilon)(V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay})$; the net gain from exit grows with the overhang when leaving with a decisive advantage gains faster than staying with one's share. (2) $\dot{\mathcal L}/\mathcal L=\dot{\mathcal H}/\mathcal H-\dot\mu_x/(1-\mu_x)+\varepsilon_\Lambda\dot{\mathcal O}/\mathcal O$, with $\varepsilon_\Lambda$ the elasticity of $\Lambda_x$, so growth of the overhang faster than the decline of the exit hazard is not sufficient for the loss to rise. (3) An agreement that preserves relative positions preserves the relative term of every payoff, and with it the motive in $V^{\rm exit}$.* *Proof:* (1) expected values. (2) Differentiate $\ln\mathcal L$; counter-case: $\mathcal O=e^{2t}$, $\mathcal H=e^{-1.5t}$, $\Lambda_x=\sqrt{\mathcal O}$ and $\mu_x=0$ give $\mathcal L=e^{-0.5t}$. (3) Proportional caps leave each share $w_i$ unchanged. $\square$

*Theorem 8.7, by part.* (1) By (2) of Proposition 8.6, the required $\upsilon$ is $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, which tends to one as $T\to\infty$ with $R$, $P$ and $F$ fixed. (2) Timely detection is detection before decisiveness: $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ (Remark 5.5), $\upsilon$ is non-decreasing in the time allowed, and $T_{\rm dec}$ falls as the loop gain rises (Remark S2.1). (3) Bounding the largest undetected defection bounds $T$, since a defection that cannot become decisive cannot earn a decisive payoff. The bound needs the coalition's effective compute to stay above the decisive ratio, an identity of the definition; effective compute is algorithmic efficiency times compute, so a coalition that caps compute keeps its bound only while its compute advantage grows as fast as diffusion erodes its algorithmic one. Nothing here derives that it does: the clause is the premise (advantage keeps pace), and the rate of erosion is an observation (EV9). Revealing types is the information role of Proposition S10.3. The Scope collects Proposition 5.16 (unverifiable information), Proposition S8.5 (future commitments) and Section 4 (the reliability of the verifiers, who are correctors that success can buy). $\square$

*An instance.* One detailed plan among leading powers to slow development and delay superintelligence to about 2040 combines compute declarations, supply-chain accounting, inference-only retrofits and recomputation of workloads, research transparency, mutually assured destruction of compute if the deal declines, and hardened reserves of about two per cent of pre-deal compute; it bounds covert projects rather than requiring near-perfect detection, the route of Theorem 8.7(3). One author's scenario judgment puts decline in years five to ten at about 4% a year, half dissolution and half impairment, and cumulative decline over ten years at about 48% [obs: EV10]. X8 gives its durability and failure-mode rows beside every other path's, by the same standard.

### An illustration of the two cruxes

Let the per-period hazard on a path be 0.05 times its uncovered share plus a background 0.001, and on the cooperative path add a dissolution hazard times the loss its fallback carries. Survival at horizons 10, 30 and 100: continuation with the uncovered share rising from 0.2 by 0.01 a period, 0.871, 0.567, 0.028; continuation with a summable uncovered share, 0.966, 0.941, 0.873; cooperation with a summable share and dissolution 2% a period with fallback loss 0.5, 0.873, 0.696, 0.319; cooperation with dissolution 0.2%, 0.956, 0.913, 0.789 [num: legacy v5, CMP]. The numbers are illustrative. They show that a well-corrected continuation can beat a fragile arrangement, and that a rising uncovered share makes any modestly durable arrangement better past the near term.

### The failure-mode table: method

The table in X8 on which Section 12.1 compares is built in four steps, the same for every path, the paper's own design included. First, the failure modes are enumerated from two sources: the path's own documents, read for what they say ends or suspends it, and the rule for $\Theta$, which admits every mode that a named mechanism of Part I produces and the register does not contradict. Second, each mode is classified as reversible or not. A mode is reversible when a reversal exists that is named, stops short of O1 and O2, and is executed by someone other than the party whose failure it is; "the arrangement is renegotiated" is a reversal only if the documents say who can compel it, and otherwise it is recorded as a reversal by the parties. A mode is irreversible when it is a decisive position (Proposition 5.8), a crossing of the host's floor (Theorem 10.4(3)), the loss of the capacity to overrule a delegation (Lemma 6.3; Proposition 6.4), or an escalation under Proposition 8.5; these are the exits from the correctable kernel of Definition 8.1. Third, each mode is given the measurement that would show it under way, drawn from the signatures and cruxes of Sections 7.3 and 12.3, so that the table and the trigger of Section 12.1 use one set of indicators. Fourth, every cell names the kind of support behind it, with one standard for every row: the path's own documents, a scenario judgment by its authors, an observation in the register, a result of this paper under its premises, or none. A cell with none says so, and the paper's own design is marked by the same rule: its documents are X10, so its reversals are provided by design and not yet shown to work, and its hazard of breakdown has no estimate and no base rate in the register. The asymmetry of reversibility on which Section 12.1 rests is read off the reversibility column, mode by mode, not from the labels of the paths.

## S9. Requirements

### Table 9.1, row by row

Each row is its cited result read as a condition on arrangements, in that result's model and over its horizon. The derivations, and the examples that fix each row's scope, follow; numbers come from the blocks of S15 named in brackets. The last column of the table holds two kinds of entry, kept apart here: conditions under which a row is not needed, and conditions under which it is met without design, which the table marks as met, not excused.

- **R1, consequences return.** *The two forms.* In the model of Proposition 3.4, with other advantages equal and a race carrier present, a cumulative advantage $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$ that tends to infinity sends the race carrier's share to one and the load to $\bar e$, so $\limsup_tV\le(1-h_V\bar e/\rho)^+$, which (reach) puts below the floor. Viability at every horizon therefore needs $B(t)\not\to\infty$. That implies the form of Theorem 10.4(3), whose proof in S10 takes the same step: if $B(t_k)\le M$ along some $t_k\to\infty$, then $\frac1{t_k}\int_0^{t_k}\tau_{\rm eff}\,ds\ge\nu-M/t_k$, so $\limsup_t\frac1t\int_0^t\tau_{\rm eff}\,ds\ge\nu$. *The average form is necessary and not sufficient:* with $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu-1/(2\sqrt{t+1})$ the time average of liability tends to $\nu$ while $B(t)=\sqrt{t+1}-1\to\infty$, and on the logistic host with $\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$ and floor 0.3, from a race share of 0.001, the host crosses its floor at $t=89$; with $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu$ it never does [num: HO]. *Not at a finite horizon:* a trajectory with zero liability need not cross the floor before $H$. With the host starting at full capacity, floor 0.2, $\rho=0.01$, $h_V=0.02$ and zero liability, even full load from the start keeps $V(50)\ge0.435$, and the host crosses the floor only at $t=\ln3/\rho=109.9$, although (reach) holds [num: HO]. At a finite horizon the necessary condition is weaker: liability must keep the cumulative advantage below that of the path that just reaches the floor at $H$, by monotone comparison. *Not needed* either where other advantages correlated with restraint offset the selection of load (the scope of Proposition 3.4).
- **R2, coverage of every actor whose share can grow.** Let a carrier of the race type stand outside the common constraints while the covered carriers bear effective liability $\tau_{\rm eff}$. Its payoff exceeds that of a covered carrier with load $e_l$ by $\nu\bar e-(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})e_l$, positive wherever the constraint costs the covered anything, so its cumulative advantage over every covered carrier grows without bound; where nothing bounds its share, the share tends to one and Proposition 3.4 applies, whatever the constraints on the others. Coverage is needed on a second ground: the race begins if any one capable actor prefers racing to restraint, and where those events are independent its probability tends to one as capable actors multiply (Proposition 5.1), so coverage must reach the most race-inclined. *Not needed* where harms stay local; where the uncovered carriers' combined share is bounded at $\bar p_{\rm unc}$ with $h_V\bar p_{\rm unc}\bar e<\rho(1-V_{\min})$, so that their load cannot carry the host below its floor; or within a horizon shorter than the crossing time.
- **R3a, the rate law.** Proposition 4.5(2) gives both halves in the field model with a seeded evaluation domain, $[H_\gamma]$, a positive cultivation channel and no per-unit floor, and Proposition S4.2 gives them off the selected level. *Sufficient at any speed:* with $\alpha_{\rm cor}\ge\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and $d_0>d^\ast(\gamma_0)$ the certificate holds at every date, for every adjustment of capacity that starts at or below its selected level and does not raise capacity while above it, since such capacity stays below its selected level and evaluation's row sum below its quasi-static value. *Necessary where capacity tracks:* below the rate law, with $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$, the cultivated term keeps a fixed fraction of a quasi-static value that grows without bound, and the certificate fails beyond a finite time. Carriers that optimize track; so does adjustment at a constant rate, which only delays failure [num: QS]; gradual selection tracks only where the variance of capacity rises faster than capacity itself. A requirement wants the sufficient half, the safe design rule; the necessary half says when a slower rate is a real failure and not a delayed one. *Human capacity:* where correction acts at $d\,(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$ and delegation erodes $\mathcal M$ at $\delta_h$, both halves hold with the threshold raised by $w\delta_h$; where the erosion stops at a floor $\mathcal M_{\min}$, the rate returns to $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and the required level rises by the factor $(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})^w$ (Proposition 4.6). *Not needed:* with $\chi=0$, or the conversion channels removed, a unit of capacity returns nothing, and the requirement is $A/c_{\rm ev}$, its level without second-order selection (Proposition 4.5(1)); with a per-unit cost floor that AI does not lower, cultivation stops once $d\ge d_f$ and the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\rm ev},d_f\}$, a level, not a rate; with a floor that falls at $\alpha_f<\alpha_{\rm cor}$, cultivation ends at a finite date and the certificate holds from some date on, though not necessarily before it: with the rate law at 0.1, $f_0=0.1$, $\alpha_f=0.05$ and $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.051$, it fails at $t=38.4$, while cultivation ends only at $t=588$ [num: FF]; with capacity selected gradually and its variance not rising faster than capacity, capacity stays bounded and correction growing at any positive rate certifies in the end, after a possible transient failure (Proposition S4.2) [num: QS]; with nothing captured; and within a horizon shorter than the failure time. A lump-sum cost of capacity does not belong on this list: it delays cultivation and leaves the requirement unbounded as control cheapens (Proposition 4.5(3)) [num: FF].
- **R3b, no core.** Proposition 4.10(4): no capture from every initial state needs an empty core of mutually sustaining capture, since a nonempty core's configuration is stable. *Not needed* if only the states actually reached must be excluded: with a row sum of two, the low initial state $(0.1,0.1)$ stays in the invariant rectangle $[0,0.1]\times[0,0.2]$ and converges to no capture, with $x=3.2\times10^{-10}$ at $t=20$ [num: FA]. A full row sum below one is the stronger certificate, sufficient from every state (Proposition 4.10(2)), and not necessary.
- **R4, breakouts within the bound.** Proposition 5.4(2): a deviation with $D_i(a)>v_i$ is deterred iff $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$, so restraint enforced by repetition needs the inequality for every actor and every such deviation; where punishment is triggered by detection and acts before decisiveness, $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, and the inequality reads $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}$ (Remark 5.5). Corollary 5.7 classifies the means. Where $D_i\le v_i$, a breakout does not raise the temptation net of the sanction: (14) is hardest to meet at $p_d=0$, and the ordinary condition of repetition, $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, decides. *The exception.* (14) is affine in $p_d$, so restraint survives every $p_d\in[0,1]$ iff it holds at $p_d=0$ and at $p_d=1$; at $p_d=1$ it reads $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$, which where $D_i\ge v_i$ implies the condition at $p_d=0$. Directly: under this inequality the left side of (14) is $(1-\delta)g_i-\delta p_d(C_i-D_i)\le\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-D_i)\le\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-v_i)$ for every $p_d$, so $\bar p_{d,i}\ge1$. A decisive position worth less than cooperation does not give this by itself: with $C_i=1$, $D_i=0.9$, $v_i=0$, $g_i=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, $\bar p_{d,i}=0.864$, and (14) fails at $p_d=0.87$ and $0.9$; with $g_i=0.5$, so that $(1-\delta)g_i=0.05\le0.09=\delta(C_i-D_i)$, it holds at $p_d=0$, $0.5$, $0.9$ and $1$ [num: ES]. *Not needed* where no deviation reaches a position beyond punishment, so that $p_d=0$: in Branch III, and in Branch I′ above its saturating level (Proposition 7.2), where the terms of Corollary 5.7 decide. *Met, not excused,* in Branch II, where detection and response come within the time a breakout needs, by the detection form above.
- **R5, a summable uncovered share.** *Necessary under (residual floor) alone.* If on every surviving history the uncovered share is at least $\underline u_t$, (residual floor) makes the conditional probability of the irreversible event at $t$ at least $h\underline u_t$, and Lemma 5.11 gives $\Pr(\Phi_H)\le\prod_{t<H}(1-h\underline u_t)\le\exp\big(-h\sum_{t<H}\underline u_t\big)$, which tends to zero when $\sum_t\underline u_t=\infty$. Survival bounded away from zero therefore needs a summable uncovered share. *Sufficient* under a residual bound below one: if the residual probability is at most $\bar hu_t$ with $\bar h<1$ and $\sum_tu_t<\infty$, survival stays bounded away from zero (Theorem 5.13). Under (shared failure) both statements are every actor's, whoever is racing. Neither (coverage lags) nor (variety grows) enters the necessity: (coverage lags) says when R5 fails, and (variety grows) is what makes (coverage lags) expected on a continuation branch, so its failure removes a reason to expect R5 to fail, not the requirement. *Not needed* where (residual floor) fails, as where a correction architecture whose blocking becomes complete makes residual risk summable without coverage (Proposition S6.4); at a finite horizon what counts is the exposure up to it, which a freeze after a sprint keeps finite and a rollback below coverage ends (Proposition 5.14). *Met, not excused,* where hazardous variety stops growing at a level set by need while coverage keeps growing at least linearly, so that the uncovered share reaches zero in finite time (Proposition 5.15).
- **R6, independent observation.** Remark 4.9: where an upper bound on masking comes from a channel the field does not control, an alarm on reports set correspondingly higher fires no later than the true condition reaches its target; for masking by the evaluated model, the bound must come from evidence its behaviour does not control. Proposition 4.8 shows what fails without it: captured evaluation lowers the level at which a fixed alarm fires, and can silence it through the crossing of the floor. Proposition 5.16: informative cheap talk needs an effective bias below $\frac14$, which relative accounting raises, and between two rivals at $\theta=-1$ no talk informs, so trust without good will needs stakes on the topic of talk that are not contested, or signals that are costly or verifiable. Each is sufficient, not necessary. The detection on which R4's detection form rests is observation of this kind, and masking hides both $T_{\rm dec}$ and $\upsilon$ (Section 5.2). *Not needed* where channels that observe actions directly, such as verifiable deployments, compute and the performance of contracts, suffice for the purpose at hand, since they do not pass through the masking of (13).
- **R7, standing.** V3. Proposition 3.12(1) shows that inclusion stopping at the edge of a club over-produces harm to those outside relative to the system optimum whenever $m_o\ell_o>0$; it does not show that O1 or O2 is reached, so the requirement is normative, declared and not derived.
