- **R8a, admission constraints that hold.** Proposition 6.7(3), with Lemma 3.3(3) type by type: under relative, cumulative selection among developers and $(A_\tau)$, with a matched variant admissible, a divergence whose costs fall on common terms persists unless a constraint enforced on every developer as a condition of admission covers it; the constraint keeps the divergence out if, for every type that can evade it, the expected sanction is at least that type's gain from evasion, and within a type only if. Across types the condition is sufficient, not necessary: in one field with equal baselines, compliance takes the field iff the best-paid variant complies, so a type whose evasion pays still loses its evaders to another type's compliers when those earn more. With compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2 and expected sanctions 1.0 and 0.15, the second type's evasion pays and still vanishes, while with 0.05 in place of 0.15 evasion takes the field [num: CI]. The matched variant is load-bearing, since Proposition 6.7(2) is about variants, not about every pair of developers: a safeguarded developer with baseline 2 and tax 0.1 outgrows an unsafe one with baseline 1, and falls to a share of $2\times10^{-9}$ by $t=200$ against its matched twin; a tax of 0.01 is paid out of a lead of 0.02 for as long as the lead lasts, and not out of a lead of 0.005 [num: CI]. *Not needed* where developers are selected by something other than relative payoff, such as a public mandate, though a national laboratory that only moves relative selection to the level of states leaves it in force there; where the tax is zero or negative for the class, so that the safeguard is neutral or spreads without coordination; and where decisions are not retained by contest performance (Lemma 6.5). *The liability route.* A liability on the developer for the harm of a divergence enters its payoff as a private cost of not countering it. Where the harm can be attributed and the liability collected, and it exceeds the tax, $(A_\tau)$ fails and the safeguard spreads without coordination (the scope of Proposition 6.7). It reaches only what can be attributed: delayed and masked divergences are where attribution fails, harms beyond the developer's assets call for regulation in advance (Section 10.3), and the liability is itself imposed by correctors (R3). The limits of admission, internal deployment, open weights and masked classes, are stated under the table, because none of them removes the requirement.
- **R8b, equal incidence.** Lemma 3.3(2): a constraint that costs every complier the same additive amount is a common term among them and leaves their relative growth unchanged, and one whose cost differs re-sorts them toward those it costs least. Equal incidence is therefore necessary and sufficient for neutrality among compliers, and not needed to keep a divergence out: with compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2 and a sanction of 1, the compliers re-sort to shares 0.88 and 0.12 by $t=20$ while the compliant share is 0.999999975, and with equal costs their relative shares stay at 1.000000 [num: CI]. What it protects against is the second direction of capture, a rule whose cost falls on challengers (Definition 4.1); that this protection is required rests on V5, since a rule that bars challengers gives the successful more advantage in the same game [val].
- **R9, human decision authority kept.** Lemma 6.3: with the capacity to take delegated decisions back decaying at $\delta_h$ under delegation and recovering at $\varrho_h$ without it, an actor that has delegated for longer than $T_{\rm lock}=\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ and reverts must operate below the floor of safe operation for a recovery time. Where the contest denies that time, the delegator can no longer overrule decisions taken in its name, and O2 is lost in that domain (Section 6.3); where a decision is irreversible within the time available for deliberation, the lock is immediate. Keeping the capacity in practice removes the lock, and where the contest is decided by strength it costs strength, so under relative selection it is not kept alone (Lemma 3.3(2)). Where delegation is dominant at the speed reached (Proposition 6.2(1)), or the lock is uncertain, so that delegation by all can be an equilibrium (Proposition 6.4(2)), the capacity survives only under a commitment binding every actor [thm∣prem]. *Not needed* where delegation leaves the capacity intact, or where automation is bounded and reversible within the time available, which the test of O2 in Section 6.6 decides. *Met without an arrangement* where every actor prices in a lock it knows to be certain: not delegating is then the unique equilibrium, strict and in need of no trust (Proposition 6.4(1)), the narrow E1 of Section 11.3.

**Sufficient safeguards.** Full row sums below one certify each corrector domain against every state of the others (Proposition 4.10(2)). The safeguards (I1)–(I4) give viability once in force, and over the transient when the liability margin is at least $\Delta_{\min}$: on the logistic host with the race strategy at 99% of activity, $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.108, 0.165 and 0.355 for floors 0.2, 0.3 and 0.5; on a host that degrades by itself below a threshold, the margin must also keep the transient above that threshold, 0.26 to 0.51 in the cases computed, and a shortfall there is permanent (Theorem 10.4) [num: HO]. Not building the decisive class, common custody, and detection and response faster than breakout lower $p_d$ by changing the premise of Proposition 5.4 (Corollary 5.7(a)). Verification with an expected sanction $\upsilon F$ at least the net gain from exit deters irreversible exit from an agreement whose overhang grows (Proposition S8.5).

### Proof of Proposition 9.2

(1) With other advantages equal, a carrier that loads less than another earns $\nu-\tau_{\rm eff}$ less per unit of the difference, so while $\tau_{\rm eff}<\nu$ its share falls relative to the other's (Proposition 3.4). Correction keyed to no one, and the cost of verification, are borne by the carrier that funds them and benefit every carrier, so they are selected against among otherwise identical carriers (Proposition 4.4). Each contribution is a cost borne by one carrier, which relative selection removes (Lemma 3.3(2)).

(2) A liability imposed by one rule-maker leaves the carriers it does not reach uncovered. By hypothesis their combined load satisfies (reach), and the race carriers among them earn the full advantage $\nu$ of load, so their cumulative advantage over every carrier that bears the liability grows without bound and Proposition 3.4 applies: the host crosses its floor whatever that rule-maker imposes on its own carriers, and it meets neither R1 nor R2 alone. Imposing the liability lowers the payoffs of the carriers it reaches against those it does not, by (1), and so lowers the rule-maker's standing among the $n_s$ rule-makers, while the benefit, a host kept above its floor, is common to all. Unilateral imposition is therefore restraint in the commons of Proposition 3.7, with $\Upsilon_s$ the value to the field of a unit of it over its private cost, and no rule-maker imposing is the equilibrium once $\lambda_s>(\Upsilon_s-n_s)/(n_s-1)$. Whether an agreement among the rule-makers is an equilibrium is governed by Proposition 8.6.

(3) Where captured rule-making leaves $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ (Section 4.1), the private payoff of a unit of load is $\nu-\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, negative iff $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})>\nu$, that is iff $x_{\rm rule}<1-\nu/\tau$, and only then does the selection of Proposition 3.4 reverse. That captured rule-making acts on liability in this form is the join of the field model to the load model, an interface of S13. $\square$

### Coverage can unravel

**Remark S9.1 (The participation threshold) [thm∣prem].** *Let $x$ be the share of capable activity outside an arrangement, weighted by share; let a participant of type $k$ bear a cost $c_k$ of its common constraints; and let the expected cost of standing outside, the sanction, the access forgone and the provision lost, be $s_k(x)$, continuous and strictly decreasing in $x$, because sanctions are funded by participants, shared provision is worth less with fewer inside, or rule-making is captured by those outside. Within a type, under cumulative payoff-monotone selection between staying and leaving, this is Lemma 3.3(3) with evasion read as exit: if $s_k(0)>c_k>s_k(1)$, staying and leaving are both stable, separated by the tipping share $x_k^\dagger$ at which $s_k(x_k^\dagger)=c_k$; if $s_k(1)\ge c_k$, staying is globally stable; and if $s_k(0)\le c_k$, leaving is, and the arrangement cannot hold the type on these terms. Participants who choose by best response, staying iff $s_k(x)\ge c_k$, have the same stable states and the same tipping share. Across types, where $s_k(0)>c_k$ for every type, every state whose outside share is below $\min_kx_k^\dagger$, the minimum taken over the types that tip, returns to full participation; past it, the type with the smallest tipping share leaves, raising the outside share toward the next. The arrangement's participation threshold is the coverage $1-\min_kx_k^\dagger$, set by the type for which leaving first pays.*

*Proof.* Within type $k$ the leaving variant grows relative to the staying one iff $c_k>s_k(x)$, so the type's outside share moves as $\dot x_k\propto x_k(1-x_k)\big(c_k-s_k(x)\big)$, and the claims for one type are those of Lemma 3.3(3). Across types, while $x<\min_kx_k^\dagger$ every bracket is negative, so every $x_k$, and with them $x$, falls. $\square$

An exit moves the outside share at once by the leaver's own share, so one large participant can carry it past a type's tipping share, and the exits of that type then follow without a further shock. With a cost of staying of 0.3 and a cost of standing outside of $0.48(1-x)$, the tipping share is 0.375; with $0.6-0.4x$ it is 0.75; with $0.5+0.5(1-x)$, which never falls below 0.5, staying is globally stable; with $0.24(1-x)$, leaving is [num: CI, evasion read as exit]. Conditions of access act on $s_k$: trade and access tied to participation, provision available only inside, and sanctions financed by formula rather than by the participants keep the cost of standing outside from falling as others leave, and once $s_k(1)\ge c_k$ for every type nothing can unravel. What raises the cost of staying moves the threshold the other way. A pause whose end is judged on several conjunctive indicators measured with noise errs toward "not ready", so an arrangement that holds participants longer than its evidence warrants spends its coverage (Section 11.3) [conj].

### The crosswalk

The paper states what an adequate arrangement must do in several places, each for its own purpose: the thresholds of Table 7.1, the generative marks G1–G7 (Section 8.2), the safeguards (I1)–(I4) (Section 10.3), the durability tests D1–D8 (Definition 8.4) and the readiness dimensions Q1–Q7 (Definition 11.4). The crosswalk maps each row of Table 9.1 onto the others, by label; each list is stated only in its home. A dash means that no entry of that list asks for the row; the readings after the table say why.

**Table S9.2 (Each requirement against the paper's other lists) [def].**

| Row of Table 9.1 | Threshold it guards (Table 7.1) | Mark | Safeguard | Durability test | Readiness |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 Consequences return | Load | G1 | (I1); (I2) in rule-making; (I4) | D5 | — |
| R2 Coverage of every actor whose share can grow | Load; Enforcement, since the race is set by the most race-inclined actor | every mark, held for every capable actor | the reach of (I1)–(I3) | D5; D8 | — |
| R3a Correction at the rate | Capture | G3 | (I2); (I4) | D4 | Q4 |
| R3b No core of capture | Capture | G3 | (I2) | D4; D7 | Q4 |
| R4 Breakouts within the bound | Enforcement; Compounding, through $T_{\rm dec}$; Standing, through $\bar p_d$ (Proposition 5.17) | G4; G2 and G5, through the won share | — | D5 | Q3; Q7 |
| R5 A summable uncovered share | Coverage and tempo | G7 | — | D7 | Q1; Q5 |
| R6 Independent observation | Talk; Capture, through masking; Enforcement, through detection | G6 | (I3) | D7 | Q1; Q7 |
| R7 Standing for those affected | — | — | — | D3; D8 | — |
| R8a Admission constraints that hold | Alignment tax | G3; G1, by the liability route; G7, for masked classes | (I1), by the liability route | D4; D7 | Q1; Q5 |
| R8b Equal incidence | Load; Capture, in its second direction | G2; G3 | — | D8 | — |
| R9 Human decision authority kept | Delegation | G7, applied to decisions | — | D3 | Q6 |
| *Sufficient:* row sums below one | Capture | G3 | (I2) | D4 | Q4 |
| *Sufficient:* (I1)–(I4) with margin | Load; Capture | G1; G3 | (I1)–(I4) | — | Q4 |
| *Sufficient:* not building, custody, detection faster than breakout | Enforcement | G4 | — | D5 | Q7 |
| *Sufficient:* verification against exit | Enforcement, under a growing overhang | G4; G6 | — | D5 | Q7 |

*Read by threshold.* Every threshold is guarded by some row. Compounding and Standing are guarded only through R4, by the time a lead needs to become decisive and by the bound the working objective sets; what reverses them directly is not a requirement but a lever, because the models give sharing, provision and non-convertible reward as alternatives, any one of which can carry the margin (Proposition 2.3).

*Read by mark.* G1, G3, G4, G6 and G7 each answer rows directly, G7 both the pace of R5 and, applied to decisions, the delegation of R9. G2 and G5 meet several rows at once rather than one: through the won share they move both sides of R4, and G2 is also what equal incidence protects (R8b), which is why Section 10 argues for them. No mark answers R7: the marks describe a change in what is selected, and standing (V3) is what any selection must leave in place.

*Read by safeguard.* (I1)–(I4) implement R1, R3 and R6 on the host of Section 3, reach R2 through their coverage and R8a through the liability route, and do not touch R4, R5, R8b or R9, which are not mechanisms of load on the host.

*Read by test.* D4, D5, D7 and D8 test rows directly, and D3 tests R7 and R9 after delegation. D1, D2 and D6 test no requirement: they test how an arrangement fails and what it keeps running, and their counterparts are the fallback (Lemma 8.2), the participation threshold (Remark S9.1) and the continuity of essential services, O3, which no row carries because the rows concern what the contest selects.

*Read by readiness.* Q1 and Q3–Q7 each ask a row. Q2 asks none: it measures the Standing threshold and G5 directly, through governing purposes, which requirements do not constrain, since selection acts whatever carriers value (Proposition 3.4). R1, R2, R7 and R8b have no readiness dimension. Readiness is asked of the frontier during a pause and presupposes them; a pause that ends with R1 or R2 unmet resumes a field in which load is selected.

*Other lists.* The four properties of provision in Section 10.1 are rows applied to provision itself: distributed is R3, many providers below the size at which influence over correctors pays (Proposition 4.3); good enough is R4 and R5, a point of enough that keeps provision within correction and confers no decisive advantage (Proposition 5.15); replaceable is R9, human capacity kept above its lock-in floor (Lemma 6.3); and for everyone is R2 and R7. The outer level of Section 6.6 is R8a, R3 and R6 for AI, and its inner level, correctability secured from outside, is R8a's capability threshold for masked classes together with R9. The function table of Section 10.5 gives the rows their forms. The requirements of Table 11.5 are of the path to an arrangement, not of the arrangement; persistence, the last of them, asks for the verification safeguard, row sums below one and (I4), with each step held for its dwell time.

## S10. The generative alternative

This chapter states in full the three results that Section 10 carries in a sentence each, gives Proposition 10.1 in full with its variations, proves Proposition 10.2 under both sharing conventions, and computes the margin of Theorem 10.4 on both hosts.

### A common frontier

**Proposition S10.1 (A common value: too little speed, not too much) [thm].** *If the value of capability is common, $\pi_i=\Omega(\mathcal E)/n-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$ with $\Omega$ increasing and strictly concave in total intensity $\mathcal E$, private incentives give $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=n(c+\ell)$ against the joint optimum's $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$, so total intensity is at or below the joint optimum: the residual failure is too little speed, not too much, and the joint optimum must also respect the host's floor (Definition 3.1).*

*Proof.* Each actor's first-order condition is $\Omega'(\mathcal E)/n=c+\ell$; the sum of payoffs, $\Omega(\mathcal E)-c\mathcal E-n\ell\mathcal E$, has the first-order condition $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$. Since $n(c+\ell)-(c+n\ell)=(n-1)c\ge0$, with equality iff $n=1$ or $c=0$, and $\Omega'$ is decreasing, private total intensity is at most the joint one (interior solutions). $\square$

**Proposition 10.1 in full.** *Of $n$ actors, cooperators hold effort $e_c$ and racers choose theirs; whoever wins the decisive contest gains $\Omega$, and every loser bears $L_b$. Under separate frontiers every effort enters the contest separately; under a common frontier the $n_c$ cooperators pool effort into one entity of strength $(n_ce_c)^r$, which yields a common value $\Omega_c$ split equally and confers no dominance among them. With $n=4$, $\Omega=\Omega_c=L_b=c=1$, $\ell=0.3$, $e_c=0.05$, $r=1.3$: (1) under separate frontiers the unique pure equilibrium is all racing; (2) under a common frontier all cooperating ($R=0.140>T=0.002$) and all racing are both strict equilibria, cooperating being a best response iff trust in each rival exceeds about 0.61, or 0.82 if a deviator best-responds to fixed intensities; (3) treating the others as a coalition maximizing their summed payoff, the coalition's cooperation is dominant; (4) a smaller pooled effort ($e_c=0.03$) leaves only all racing, and with $n=2$ all cooperating is an equilibrium for $r$ up to about 1.2; (5) holding $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ fixed, the pooled effort needed to keep $T\le R$ is 0.0054, 0.035, 0.141 and 0.49 for $\Omega=0.5,1,2,4$; scaling $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ with $\Omega$, it is 0.0175, 0.035, 0.070 and 0.140.* Racers' intensities are the symmetric fixed point of best responses, each verified as a global best response; cases without a symmetric pure equilibrium are excluded (`../v4/checks_v45.py`, N22, run in the legacy suite of `checks_v62.py`) [num: legacy v4.5, N22]. Variations in the same block keep both strict equilibria with $n=3$, with $L_b=0.5$, with $\ell=0$, with $\Omega_c=2$ and with $n=6$ at $r\in\{0.8,1\}$, the aggregate trust threshold ranging from 0.14 to 0.90; at $n=6$, $r=1.3$ some racer subgames have no symmetric pure equilibrium, and results there are not equilibria.

A common frontier turns a dilemma into a coordination problem while the value of winning is moderate; when it is large, keeping ahead of defectors is the race moved up a level. *Within the pool.* The model fixes $e_c$. Each cooperator's effort raises the pool's strength, and with it the chance that the pool and not a racer wins; the gain, $\Omega_c$ split equally and the loss $L_b$ avoided, goes to every cooperator alike, while the cost falls on the contributor. Contributions chosen one by one are therefore a good common to the cooperators, which selection among them does not favour (Lemma 3.11), and parts (4) and (5) put a floor under the pooled effort that rises steeply with $\Omega$: a pool whose contributions drift below the floor loses its cooperative equilibrium. That is a free-rider result, and it is why Discussion X10 collects frontier contributions by formula from the same base as the levy rather than by subscription. Their size in an equilibrium with chosen contributions is not computed here.

### Proof of Proposition 10.2

(1) Pooled payoffs $\pi'_j=(1-\beta)\pi_j+\beta\bar\pi$ give $J_i=\big[(1-\beta)+\beta(1+\theta_0)/n\big]\pi_i+\big[\theta_0(1-\beta)+\beta(1+\theta_0)(n-1)/n\big]\bar\pi_{-i}$; dividing by the first coefficient gives $\theta_{\rm eff}(\beta)$, strictly increasing in $\beta$ for $-1<\theta_0<n-1$, with value $n-1$ at $\beta=1$. At $\theta_0=-1$, $J_i=(1-\beta)(\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i})$, and under relative selection $\pi'_k-\pi'_l=(1-\beta)(\pi_k-\pi_l)$, so no choice and no ordering of carriers changes.

(2) Write $A_w=(1-\beta\varrho)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$ for what the winner keeps. *Shared among all $n$:* every actor receives $\varrho\beta\Omega_e/n$ whoever wins, so $\pi_i=w_iA_w+\varrho\beta\Omega_e/n-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$; the shared term is common to all and drops out of every objective $J_i=\pi_i+\theta\bar\pi_{-i}$ and of relative selection, leaving the contest with prize $\Omega_{\rm eff}=A_w$. *Shared among the $n-1$ others:* actor $j$ receives $\varrho\beta\Omega_e/(n-1)$ whenever another wins, so $\pi_j=w_jA_w+\varrho\beta\Omega_e(1-w_j)/(n-1)-ce_j-\ell\mathcal E=w_j\Omega_{\rm eff}+\varrho\beta\Omega_e/(n-1)-ce_j-\ell\mathcal E$ with $\Omega_{\rm eff}=A_w-\varrho\beta\Omega_e/(n-1)=\big(1-\beta\varrho\,n/(n-1)\big)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$; since $\sum_{j\ne i}w_j=1-w_i$, $\bar\pi_{-i}=-w_i\Omega_{\rm eff}/(n-1)$ plus terms independent of $w_i$, and $J_i=w_i\Omega_{\rm eff}(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)-ce_i-(1+\theta)\ell\mathcal E$ plus a constant: again the contest with prize $\Omega_{\rm eff}$. This is the convention under which Proposition 3.5(4) and Proposition 5.8 count what a winner gives. The symmetric equilibrium $e(\theta)$ of Proposition 3.6(1), the dissipation of Proposition 5.2 and the hazard of Proposition 5.10 are each proportional to the prize. Under relative selection a mutant at $y$ among residents at $x$ has relative payoff $\Omega_{\rm eff}(y^r-x^r)/(y^r+(n-1)x^r)-c(y-x)$ under either convention, with zero slope at $x^\ast=r\Omega_{\rm eff}/(nc)$, concave for $r=1$, and no profitable mutant on a grid for $r<1$ [num: SH].

*Where the uninvadable intensity is positive.* Under the first convention $\Omega_{\rm eff}>0$ for every $\beta\varrho<1$, and with $\Omega_s>0$, $\Omega_{\rm eff}\ge\Omega_s>0$ for every $\beta\le1$, so the uninvadable intensity is at least $r\Omega_s/(nc)$ however much of the divisible prize is shared. Under the second, $\Omega_{\rm eff}\ge\Omega_s$ while $\beta\varrho\le(n-1)/n$; beyond it a winner that has given the $n-1$ others more than $(n-1)/n$ of the divisible prize gains less from it than a loser, and $\Omega_{\rm eff}$ stays positive for every $\beta$ iff $\Omega_s>\Omega_e/(n-1)$, the value at which the positional prize outweighs the divisible loss of a winner who has given everything away. V6.1 stated "positive for every $\beta$ when $\Omega_s>0$" without the convention; it holds under the first.

(3) is Proposition 2.3(1) with Proposition S2.4(4): with $k_0=0$, $S^\ast=\kappa_c\Omega/d_K$, and the condition of Proposition 2.3(1) becomes $(1-\beta)r-1<\delta_f/d_K$, in which $\kappa_c$ does not appear, with or without diffusion. With $\chi=0$ the reflexive channels of Proposition 2.2 are removed; then the return to influence is zero (Proposition 4.3) and $d^\ast=A/c_{\rm ev}$ (Proposition 4.5(1)). $\square$

*Numerics* [num: SH]. With $n=4$, $r=1$, $c=1$, $\Omega_e=1$, the uninvadable intensity is 0.25 without a positional part and 0.125 with half the divisible prize shared among all; with $\Omega_s=1$, sharing all of $\Omega_e$ only halves it, from 0.5 to 0.25. In a common field of intensities $(0.2,0.05,0.1,0.1)$ the higher-intensity carrier out-earns the lower by 0.017 with half shared, and the difference turns negative only beyond 55%: sharing lowers the advantage of intensity at a given profile without moving the uninvadable intensity to zero. With $\Omega_e=1$ and $\Omega_s=0.5$, the uninvadable intensity is 0.300 (shared among all) and 0.275 (among the others) at $\beta=0.3$, and 0.225 and 0.175 at $\beta=0.6$, equal to $r\Omega_{\rm eff}/(nc)$ in each case with no mutant gaining on a grid. With $\Omega_s=0$, actors that value their absolute payoff and choose intensities as best responses follow the same two prizes: their Nash intensities, $r\Omega_{\rm eff}(n-1)/(n^2c)$, are 0.13125 and 0.11250 at $\beta=0.3$, and 0.07500 and 0.03750 at $\beta=0.6$. Earlier versions said that only changing what winning converts into reverses the direction of selection; in this model none of the three levers makes the selected intensity zero while a positional prize remains.

**Remark 10.3 in full.** At the first order, more contenders make the race fiercer (Proposition 5.1(4)), and in a model of developers racing under a shared existential hazard, fragmentation raises speed and conditional risk, while a developer able to commit first to a slightly slower speed gains because its rivals slow too (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026; EV11). At the second order, cultivated influence rises with $\sum_kp_k^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ (Proposition 4.5(4)), so merging the frontier into one carrier maximizes the return to capturing its correctors, and the gain of the reflexive channel is higher at the present concentration of standing than at parity (S2). A merged frontier also raises connectivity and with it common-mode failure, so it keeps modules of independent lineage inside it (Proposition 6.9), and its correction outside it.

### Proof of Theorem 10.4, and the margin on both hosts

(1) (I2) with a margin in rule-making gives a full inbound row sum below one, so $x_{\rm rule}\to0$ (Proposition 4.10(2)); once (I1) is enacted, $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ exceeds $\nu$ after a finite time, load has a negative private payoff, the load falls to $e_{\min}$, and $\liminf V\ge1-h_Ve_{\min}/\rho>V_{\min}$ on the host (3), the last inequality being the premise $h_Ve_{\min}<\rho(1-V_{\min})$, which V6.1 left implicit in "a carrier of low enough load". For unanticipated harms, (I2) in evaluation gives $x_{\rm ev}\to0$, so masking falls below its threshold, or the conservative rule of Remark 4.9 applies, and the alarm fires above the floor.

*The margin.* Fix the state, above the floor, at which the margin $\Delta=\tau_{\rm eff}-\nu>0$ takes force, and measure time from it. Relative fitness is then $-\Delta(e-E)$, so at selection speed $s$ the distribution of load at $t$ is the distribution at that state tilted by $e^{-s\Delta et}$ and normalized; its mean falls as the tilt $s\Delta t$ rises, its derivative in the tilt being minus the variance, so at every $t>0$ the load $E(t)$ is lower the larger $\Delta$. With two loads the race share solves $\dot x=-s\Delta(e_{\max}-e_{\min})\,x(1-x)$. Since $\dot V$ is decreasing in $E$ on the host (3) and on the threshold host below, the comparison theorem makes $V(t)$ nondecreasing in $\Delta$ at every $t$, so the minimum of $V$ along the path is nondecreasing in $\Delta$, and by continuity the margins that keep it at or above the floor form an interval $[\Delta_{\min},\infty)$. It is not empty on (3): as $\Delta\to\infty$ the load falls to $e_{\min}$ at once, and $V$ then moves monotonically toward $1-h_Ve_{\min}/\rho>V_{\min}$. On a host whose per-capita regeneration is lower at every level and negative below a threshold $V_u$, a path that falls below $V_u$ does not return; the interval is then not empty only if the state at which the margin takes force lies above $V_u$ at that load, and a normative floor below $V_u$ would make crossing the floor a loss that is not the first.

(2) Without (I1), Proposition 3.4 applies; without (I2) in rule-making, $x_{\rm rule}\to1$ and $\tau_{\rm eff}\to0$; without (I3), silent crossing follows (Proposition 4.13); with rule-making captured first, the floor can be crossed when rule-making adapts slowly.

(3) If the cumulative advantage $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$ tends to $+\infty$, the race share tends to one, the load to $\bar e$, and under (reach) Proposition 3.4 puts the host below its floor; so viability at every horizon keeps $B$ from tending to $+\infty$. If the lim sup of the time average of $\tau_{\rm eff}$ were below $\nu$, there would be $\epsilon>0$ and $T_0$ with average at most $\nu-\epsilon$ beyond $T_0$, so $B(t)\ge\epsilon t-C\to\infty$. The converse fails, as the numerics show. $\square$

*The margin, computed* [num: HO]. On the forgiving host ($\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$, $V_0=1$, the race strategy holding 99% of activity, $e_{\max}=1$): with $e_{\min}=0$, a margin of 0.08 from the start dips to 0.1397 before recovering to 1.000, and $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.108, 0.165 and 0.355 for floors 0.2, 0.3 and 0.5; with $e_{\min}=0.1$ the long-run level is 0.840, a margin of 0.08 dips to 0.1136, and $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.125, 0.192 and 0.414. Three independent integrations, a closed-form share with its own stopping rule, a replicator integrated jointly with the host, and the memo's own code, agree on these margins to 0.002. Below the margin this host recovers: with $\Delta=0.02$ it falls to 0.0025 and returns to 0.840, since on the logistic host any $\Delta>0$ recovers eventually. On the pointwise harsher threshold host $\rho_A(V/A-1)(1-V)$, which degrades by itself below its threshold $V_u(e_{\min})$, with $e_{\min}=0.1$ and the same initial composition, the margin that keeps the transient above $V_u$ is 0.260, 0.306, 0.359, 0.423 and 0.505 for $A=0.05,0.10,0.15,0.20,0.25$, with $V_u$ equal to 0.254, 0.308, 0.364, 0.421 and 0.481; below it the collapse is permanent. For $A\ge0.10$, $V_u$ lies above a normative floor of 0.3, so the floor must be set no lower than the host's threshold for crossing it to be the first loss. *Part (3) is not sufficient.* With $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu-1/(2\sqrt{t+1})$ the average tends to $\nu$, but $B(t)=\sqrt{t+1}-1$ grows without bound, and from a race share of 0.001 the host crosses its floor at $t=89$; with $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu$ it does not. The averaged condition of R1 is necessary and not sufficient; the cumulative one is the condition the proof uses.

*What the computation is.* Earlier versions called the threshold-host margin "the first computable instance of the correctable kernel", and stated viability under a clause, "viable if, in addition, the host stays above its floor while the load falls", that assumed what was to be shown. Both are withdrawn: the clause was circular, and the margin condition replaces it on both hosts. The computation is an instance of the boundary of viable parameters for one fixed strategy of correction; the correctable kernel of Definition 8.1 takes a union over admissible strategies and an intersection over disturbances in the justified range, and is not computed here.

### Rival verification

**Proposition S10.2 (Rival verification) [thm].** *A rival's verifier whose full inbound row sum is below one, counting conversion from the verified party, from its own side and from shared sources such as models, supply chains and professional paradigms, is captured by none of them. Rivalry keeps the verified party's contribution near zero; it does not by itself bound the rest of the row.*

*Proof.* Apply Proposition 4.10(2) to the verifier's full row. A verifier taking 0.10 from the verified party and 0.95 from shared sources is captured once those sources are, whatever the rivalry. $\square$

*Scope.* Rival verifiers raise false alarms, can collude against third parties, and check compliance rather than judgment. A verifier biased toward finding violations is, for claims the other side cannot check, a sender with a material bias, and talk survives only while the effective bias is below $\frac14$ (Proposition 5.16); so its findings must rest on evidence the other side can check. An accusation under rivalry can trigger prevention (Proposition 8.5), so joint resolution precedes any response.

### Participation

**Proposition S10.3 (Participation) [thm].** Under complete information an arrangement is efficient for a pair of types iff its survival-weighted joint value exceeds the sum of their survival-weighted race payoffs, and an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff. Under private types an arrangement without verification must give each type an interim share independent of its report, and is feasible iff the joint value covers the sum of the types' best race payoffs; verification that reveals types removes the information rents, and the constraint relaxes as survival in the race falls below survival under the arrangement.

*Two actors have private types, strong or weak, with costs $c_s<c_w$, each strong with probability $p_s$ independently. Outside the arrangement types are revealed in a complete-information all-pay contest for $\Omega+L_b$: a strong actor against a weak one gets $U^{\rm race}=(\Omega+L_b)(1-c_s/c_w)-L_b$, and otherwise $-L_b$; survival in the race is $\Phi^{\rm race}$. The arrangement yields a common value net of costs $S_a$, divided in shares $\mathsf s_i$, with survival $\Phi^R$; an actor's utility is $\Phi_i(\mathsf v_i+\bar{\mathsf v})$, with $\mathsf v_i$ its payoff and $\bar{\mathsf v}$ a value it keeps either way. (1) Under complete information and transferable utility, a pair gains iff $\Phi^R(S_a+2\bar{\mathsf v})\ge\sum_i\Phi^{\rm race}_i(U^{\rm race}_i+\bar{\mathsf v})$. (2) With private independent types and participation decided before types are revealed, Bayesian incentive compatibility makes each type's interim expected share independent of its report, budget balance makes the interim shares constants summing to one, and the arrangement is feasible without verification iff $\Phi^R(S_a+2\bar{\mathsf v})\ge\sum_i\sup_{c}E\big[\Phi^{\rm race}(c,t_j)(U^{\rm race}_i+\bar{\mathsf v})\mid c\big]$; ex-post shares may still vary with reports. (3) Verification revealing types removes the information rents; it is distinct from the timely detection of defection. (4) The information condition relaxes as $\Phi^{\rm race}/\Phi^R$ falls. (5) Under complete information an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff; raising the joint value, lowering the value of winning, compressing survival or changing the asymmetry of costs can still make equal shares feasible.*

*Scope.* Rare strong types expect much from the race: mutual optimism, a failure of bargaining under private information (Fearon 1995). Which mechanism is second-best depends on the mechanisms allowed and on commitment, and is not solved here. Participation benefits, voting rights and protection against domination are three different quantities in this model: (1) and (2) price the first, say nothing about the second, and the third is a constraint fixed before the shares are set (Section 10.3).

*Proof.* (1) is the comparison of joint survival-weighted values with transfers. (2) Interim incentive compatibility with independent types equalizes each type's interim expected share across reports; budget balance then fixes the interim shares as constants summing to one, and participation of every type needs the joint value to cover each type's best expected race payoff. (3) Revealed types allow shares conditioned on types, removing the rents. (4) and (5) follow from the form of the inequalities. $\square$

*Numerics* [num: legacy v4.5, K7; legacy v5, K7]. With $c_s/c_w=0.1$, $\Omega=1$, $L_b=0$, $S_a=1$ and every $\Phi=1$, the sum of suprema is 0.36, 0.90, 1.44 and 1.71 at $p_s=0.8,0.5,0.2,0.05$: feasible without verification in the first two cases and not in the last two, although cooperation is efficient for every pair; with $L_b=0.5$ the sums are $-0.46$, 0.35, 1.16 and 1.56. With $\Phi^R=0.95$, $\bar{\mathsf v}=1$ and $p_s=0.2$, equal shares become feasible once $\Phi^{\rm race}/\Phi^R\le0.872$. Under complete information with $L_b=0.5$ and $S_a=1$, the strong type's minimum share is 0.925 at $c_s/c_w=0.05$ and 0.700 at 0.2 (`../v4/checks_v45.py`, K7). The interim shares of (2) are checked in `../v5/checks_v5.py`, K7: ex-post shares that differ by report, with an interim expected share of 0.500 for each type. V6.1 attributed all of these numbers to the v5 block; the feasibility sums, the survival compression and the minimum shares are in the v4.5 block. Both are legacy blocks of `checks_v62.py --legacy`.

## S11. The transition

### Proof of Corollary 11.1

Write, as in Proposition 8.6, $g_C=R-\upsilon(P-F)-(1-\upsilon)T$ and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$. Where both are positive, the belief threshold $\varpi^\ast=g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ falls as $g_C$ rises and rises with $g_D$.

*(1)* $\partial g_C/\partial R=1$ and $\partial g_C/\partial T=-(1-\upsilon)\le0$, so raising $R$ or lowering $T$ lowers $\varpi^\ast$. The timely detection that cooperation needs, $u^\ast=(T-R)/(T-P+F)$ from Proposition 8.6(2), has $\partial u^\ast/\partial R=-1/(T-P+F)<0$ and $\partial u^\ast/\partial T=(R-P+F)/(T-P+F)^2$, positive iff $R>P-F$. The effective bias of Proposition 5.16(1), $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$, has derivative $-2(n-1)b_0/(n-1+\theta)^2<0$ in $\theta$. The net gain from exit is $V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$ (Proposition S8.5(1)), and an agreement that preserves relative positions preserves its relative term (Proposition S8.5(3)), so lowering that term lowers the gain.

*(2)* $\partial g_C/\partial\upsilon=T-P+F>0$ under the premise, and $\partial g_D/\partial\upsilon=-(P-S)<0$ where $P>S$. Verification reaches only what it observes (Theorem 8.7, Scope): messages about the rest remain cheap talk, credible only where the effective bias is below $\frac14$ (Proposition 5.16).

*(3)* $\partial g_C/\partial F=\upsilon>0$. An irreversible, public exit is deterred while $\upsilon F\ge V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$, and a reversible covert preparation while $\upsilon F\ge(1-\upsilon)(V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay})$ (Proposition S8.5(1)). A defector whose position has become decisive is beyond punishment, so against a deviation with $D_i>v_i$ the sanction acts only through $v_i$, and restraint holds only while $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$ (Proposition 5.4(2)).

*(4)* $\partial g_D/\partial(P-S)=1-\upsilon\ge0$. $\square$

*The link to the levers* [thm∣prem]. Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the contested stakes scales them by $1-\beta$ (Proposition 5.10), which lowers what racing against a restrainer gains over mutual restraint; provision beneath the game lowers the won share of capacity and with it $\Gamma_\delta$ (Proposition 5.17; Proposition 2.3); and a lower $\Gamma_\delta$ lowers the price of standing and widens the working objective (Proposition 3.9). A common frontier raises what restraint earns against a defector, $S$, by pooling the restrainers' effort, and so lowers $P-S$; in the contest of Proposition 10.1 it turns all cooperating into a strict equilibrium beside the race [num]. The stage payoffs $T,R,P,S$ of Proposition 8.6 are reduced forms of those contests, so the link holds where they are read from them.

### Proof of Proposition 11.2

The receiver restrains with probability $\rho_m$ after message $m\in\{\mathsf E,\mathsf N\}$, where $\mathsf N$ is not taking the step: $\rho_m=1$ if its posterior that the sender is $\mathsf S$ exceeds $\varpi^\ast$, $\rho_m=0$ if it falls short, and any $\rho_m$ at equality; after a message no type sends, any belief, and so any $\rho_m$, is admissible. Type $\mathsf t$ receives $\rho_{\mathsf E}\Pi_{\mathsf t}-c_{\mathsf t}$ if it takes the step and $\rho_{\mathsf N}\Pi_{\mathsf t}$ if not.

*(1)* In the candidate the posteriors are 1 after the step and 0 without it, so $\rho_{\mathsf E}=1$ and $\rho_{\mathsf N}=0$, since $\varpi^\ast\in(0,1)$. $\mathsf S$ takes the step iff $\Pi_{\mathsf S}-c_{\mathsf S}\ge0$, and $\mathsf R$ refrains iff $\Pi_{\mathsf R}-c_{\mathsf R}\le0$.

*(2)* If $c_{\mathsf R}<\Pi_{\mathsf R}$, $\mathsf R$ gains $\Pi_{\mathsf R}-c_{\mathsf R}>0$ by imitating the candidate of (1), which is therefore not an equilibrium. Let $0\le c_{\mathsf R}<\Pi_{\mathsf R}$ and $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, and take any equilibrium. The posteriors on the path average to the prior $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, so at most one message on the path has a posterior of $\varpi^\ast$ or more. If $\rho_{\mathsf N}>0$, then $\mathsf N$ is either off the path, so that $\mathsf E$ is the only message on it, its posterior is $1-\mu$ and $\rho_{\mathsf E}=0$; or on it with a posterior of at least $\varpi^\ast$, so that the posterior after $\mathsf E$, if $\mathsf E$ is sent, is below it and again $\rho_{\mathsf E}=0$, while if $\mathsf E$ is not sent the posterior after $\mathsf N$ is $1-\mu$, contradicting $\rho_{\mathsf N}>0$. If $\rho_{\mathsf N}=0$ and $\rho_{\mathsf E}>c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$, $\mathsf R$ takes the step with certainty, and whatever $\mathsf S$ does the posterior after the step is $(1-\mu)\sigma_{\mathsf S}/\big((1-\mu)\sigma_{\mathsf S}+\mu\big)\le1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, so $\rho_{\mathsf E}=0$, a contradiction. Hence $\rho_{\mathsf E}\le c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$ in every equilibrium, after the step whether or not it is on the path. The bound is attained in the equilibrium in which $\mathsf S$ takes the step, $\mathsf R$ takes it with probability $\sigma_{\mathsf R}=(1-\mu)(1-\varpi^\ast)/(\mu\varpi^\ast)\in(0,1)$, which holds the posterior after the step at $\varpi^\ast$, and the receiver restrains with probability $c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$ after the step and not otherwise; $\mathsf R$ is then indifferent, and $\mathsf S$ takes the step iff $c_{\mathsf S}\le\Pi_{\mathsf S}c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$.

*(3)* follows from (2) as $c_{\mathsf R}\to0$. $\square$

V6.1 stated (2) as "the receiver restrains after the step only if $1-\mu\ge\varpi^\ast$". That holds among pure-strategy equilibria, where $\mathsf R$ either imitates always or never; the equilibrium that attains the bound above has $\mathsf R$ imitating with a probability below one, and restraint after the step with positive probability even where prior trust falls short.

*Refinement.* Let $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, $c_{\mathsf S}<\Pi_{\mathsf S}$ and $c_{\mathsf R}>\Pi_{\mathsf R}$. The step is strictly dominated for $\mathsf R$, whose payoff from it is at most $\Pi_{\mathsf R}-c_{\mathsf R}<0$. In any equilibrium in which $\mathsf S$ does not take the step, the posterior without it is $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, so $\rho_{\mathsf N}=0$ and $\mathsf S$ receives 0, while the step could give it up to $\Pi_{\mathsf S}-c_{\mathsf S}>0$. The intuitive criterion (Cho and Kreps 1987) then puts all weight after the step on $\mathsf S$, the receiver restrains after it, and $\mathsf S$ deviates; and no partially separating equilibrium exists, since $\mathsf R$ never takes a dominated step and $\mathsf S$, once the step earns restraint, strictly prefers it. The separating equilibrium is the only one that survives. Where prior trust is already sufficient, $1-\mu\ge\varpi^\ast$, an equilibrium without the step and with restraint exists beside it, and the step is not needed.

*The signal and its imitation.* This is the costly signal of a security-seeking type (Kydd 2000). What the selection account adds is the reason the step is cheap to imitate unless it is inspected: a relative type that declares the removal and keeps the delegation it does not disclose pays nothing for the declaration, so $c_{\mathsf R}\to0$ and (3) applies. Inspection of doctrine and exercises, and durability that makes reversal costly, are what make $c_{\mathsf R}$ a cost at all.

*Numerics* [num: TR]. A search over strategy profiles, which does not use the conditions it tests, agrees with (1) in 300 random games, finds no equilibrium above the bound of (2) in 200, and finds the bounding equilibrium in every one of the 135 in which $c_{\mathsf S}\le\Pi_{\mathsf S}c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$. With $c_{\mathsf S}=-0.05$, $c_{\mathsf R}=0.30$, $\Pi_{\mathsf S}=0.40$ and $\Pi_{\mathsf R}=0.20$ the step separates. If it can be reversed cheaply, $c_{\mathsf R}=0.05$, it does not: with $\mu=0.5$ and $\varpi^\ast=0.6$, $\mathsf R$ imitates with probability 0.667, and the step buys restraint with probability 0.25 at most.

### Proof of Proposition 11.3

*(1)* Let $h_t$ be the conditional probability of the irreversible event in the $t$-th active round, given survival to it, on each surviving history. A pause that adds no hazard of its own changes the calendar date of each active round and nothing else, so survival over the first $N$ active rounds, $\prod_{t\le N}(1-h_t)$ on each history, is the same with or without it, and so is the limit. By Lemma 5.11, it is zero where the $h_t$ are at least a non-summable sequence. *(2)* Where after the pause $h_t\le\bar h_t<1$ with $\sum_t\bar h_t<\infty$, survival is at least the product of the rounds before the change and $\prod_t(1-\bar h_t)>0$. *(3)* is Proposition 4.12 with ordinary rule-making, $b_p=b$. $\square$

*Example* [num: TR]. With residual hazard 0.01 a round for 20 rounds and $0.01/t^{1.5}$ in the $t$-th round after a change, survival to round 10,000 is 0.80, and its limit is 0.80, since $\sum_t0.01\,t^{-1.5}=0.01\,\zeta(3/2)\approx0.026$; with the hazard left at 0.01 a round it is $2\times10^{-44}$.

### The decision rule of readiness

**Remark S11.1 (Readiness under noise and conjunction) [thm].** A reading fixed in advance biases a conjunction of seven dimensions toward "not ready", more so as noise grows; a sequential test turns noise into delay. *(1) If each of seven dimensions, when met, is declared met with probability $1-\mathsf b_q$, independently, a ready state is declared ready with probability $\prod_q(1-\mathsf b_q)$, which is $0.95^7\approx0.70$ at $\mathsf b_q=0.05$. (2) Let each dimension be decided by a sequential probability-ratio test between a registered level below its threshold and one above it, on independent readings whose likelihood ratio is computed from their distribution, with boundaries set for error rates $\mathsf a_q$ (declaring it met when it is not) and $\mathsf b_q$ (declaring it unmet when it is met). The test ends with probability one, and its error rates are at most $\mathsf a_q/(1-\mathsf b_q)$ and $\mathsf b_q/(1-\mathsf a_q)$ at every level of noise (Wald 1945). Over seven dimensions a ready state is held back with probability at most $\sum_q\mathsf b_q/(1-\mathsf a_q)$.*

*Proof.* (1) is a product of independent events. (2) is Wald's termination theorem and his bounds for the boundaries $\ln\big((1-\mathsf b_q)/\mathsf a_q\big)$ and $\ln\big(\mathsf b_q/(1-\mathsf a_q)\big)$ on the log-likelihood ratio, with a union bound over the seven. $\square$

*Numerics* [num: TR]. A fixed reading of 16 observations, designed for a 1% rate of false "met" and a 5% rate of missed "met" at unit noise, and holding its 1% rate at any noise, passes a met dimension with probability 0.95 at unit noise and 0.37 at twice it; seven met dimensions then pass together with probability 0.71 and 0.001. Sequential tests with $\mathsf a_q=0.01$ and $\mathsf b_q=0.05/7$, on Gaussian readings at levels 0 and 1, keep both observed error rates below their bounds at noise 1, 2 and 4, while the mean number of readings needed to pass a met dimension rises from 10.5 to 38.7 and 148.6, about as the variance. Over seven such dimensions a ready state is held back with probability at most $0.05/0.99\approx0.0505$.

The residence condition of Definition 11.4 sets the window over which the readings are taken: no test may end before the residence time has passed, so a dimension cannot be declared met before its slow variable could have crossed its threshold. The rates are registered asymmetrically because the errors are: declaring a dimension met too early is the error the protection standard weighs, since resuming cannot be undone; holding it back too long is bounded, since a pause held past its evidence spends coverage (Remark S9.1). A dimension once met is re-opened by a test of the same kind, so that a single low reading neither re-opens it nor is ignored.

### Dwell, depth and a recruitable lineage

Take one domain of the field model (10) with its own conversion $b$ and conversion gain $G=ab/(cd)>1$, and write it in the logit of the share, $u=\ln\big(x/(1-x)\big)$, and rule support in units of break-even, $\tilde z=az/c$: then $\dot u=\zeta c(\tilde z-1)$ and $\dot{\tilde z}=\varepsilon d(Gx-\tilde z)$. Capture is the state $x\to1$, $\tilde z=G$; absence of capture, $(0,0)$, is locally stable, the interior saddle is $(1/G,1)$, and the two basins are divided by the saddle's stable manifold, which meets $\tilde z=0$ at the share $x_\sigma$ of Proposition 4.12. A reform that insulates rule-making sets the conversion to zero for a time $T$ and then restores it; the *dwell* $T_{\min}$ is the least $T$ after which the state converges to absence of capture. "Rule-making ten times slower than adoption" is $\varepsilon d=0.1\,\zeta c$.

**Proposition S11.2 (Dwell, depth and a recruitable lineage) [thm; num: DW].** A reform that insulates rule-making has to be held past the return of shares to their former level, for longer the deeper the capture, and without bound as capture ages unless a lineage of the alternative persists that can be recruited when the rule changes. *Start an insulating reform from rule support at its captured level, $\tilde z=G$, with share $x_0$.*
1. *(Two phases.) Under insulation $\tilde z=Ge^{-\varepsilon dt}$; the share keeps rising for $\ln G/(\varepsilon d)$, and its logit is back at $u_0$ when $G(1-e^{-s})=s$, $s=\varepsilon dt$. After release the state converges to absence of capture iff it lies below the saddle's stable manifold. If $x_0>x_\sigma$, the dwell exceeds the time at which the share is back at its starting level* [thm].
2. *(Values.) From $x_0=0.99$, $\varepsilon dT_{\min}$ is 1.89, 5.32 and 10.45 for $G=2$, 5 and 10 when rule-making is ten times slower than adoption, and 5.86, 10.34 and 16.18 when the two are equally fast; the share is back at its starting level at $s=1.59$, 4.97 and 10.0* [num: DW].
3. *(Depth.) $T_{\min}$ is nondecreasing in the depth $u_0$ and at least $(u_0-\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma)/(\zeta c)$, so it has no bound as depth grows. At the captured state $\dot u=\zeta c(G-1)$, so the depth grows linearly with the age of capture* [thm]. *Each further unit of depth adds about $\varepsilon d/(\zeta c)$ to $\varepsilon dT_{\min}$* [num: DW].
4. *(A recruitable lineage.) If a lineage of the alternative persists inside selection at a share of at least $\phi_{\rm alt}$, so that the practice's share never exceeds $1-\phi_{\rm alt}$, and grows from it when the rule changes, the depth never exceeds $\ln\big((1-\phi_{\rm alt})/\phi_{\rm alt}\big)$, and $T_{\min}$ is at most its value from that depth, whatever the age of capture* [thm]. *With $G=10$ and rule-making ten times slower, a floor of 0.1% cuts $T_{\min}$ from 10.45 to 3.81; a share of the alternative protected outside selection, which does not recruit, leaves it at 10.44* [num: DW].

*Proof.* (1) Under insulation $\dot{\tilde z}=-\varepsilon d\tilde z$, so $\tilde z=Ge^{-\varepsilon dt}$ and $u(t)=u_0+\zeta c\big[G(1-e^{-\varepsilon dt})/(\varepsilon d)-t\big]$, which rises while $\tilde z>1$ and is back at $u_0$ when $G(1-e^{-s})=s$. After release the function of the proof of Proposition 4.12 is nondecreasing along solutions, so every solution converges to an equilibrium and the saddle's stable manifold separates the basins. The field is cooperative (Lemma S4.3), so the basin of absence is a down-set in $(u,\tilde z)$; it contains $(u,0)$ only for $u<\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma$, and so it contains no state with $u>\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma$. When the share is back at $x_0>x_\sigma$, and at every earlier time, the state is therefore outside the basin of absence. (3) The insulated equation for $u$ does not depend on $u$, and $\tilde z(t)$ does not depend on $u_0$, so a start deeper by $\Delta u$ stays deeper by $\Delta u$ at every $t$ with the same $\tilde z(t)$; since the basin of absence is a down-set, a $T$ that suffices from the deeper start suffices from the shallower one. The released state is in the basin only if $u(T)<\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma$, and $u(T)\ge u_0-\zeta cT$. At the captured state $\tilde z=G$, so $\dot u=\zeta c(G-1)$. (4) With the floor, $u\le\ln\big((1-\phi_{\rm alt})/\phi_{\rm alt}\big)$ throughout, and the insulated path from any start is bounded above, at every $t$, by the capped path from that depth; by the down-set property the bound on $T_{\min}$ follows. $\square$ The increments of (3) and the values of (2) and (4) are numerical: once rule support has decayed the logit falls at a rate approaching $\zeta c$, so each further unit of depth costs about $1/(\zeta c)$ of time.

*Numerics* (DW: forward integration in logit coordinates, and independently a backward-integrated separatrix with the analytic insulated path, agreeing to three decimals in six of six cases). Depth without a floor, with $G=2$ and equal speeds: from $x_0=1-10^{-1}$, $1-10^{-2}$, $1-10^{-3}$, $1-10^{-6}$ and $1-10^{-12}$ the dwell is 3.45, 5.86, 8.17, 15.08 and 28.90; with $G=10$ it runs from 13.78 to 39.22; with rule-making ten times slower the increments are a tenth as large (1.59 to 4.34 at $G=2$; 10.21 to 12.76 at $G=10$). A floor on the alternative, with $G=10$ and rule-making ten times slower: 3.52 at $\phi_{\rm alt}=0.01$, 3.81 at 0.001, 4.60 at $10^{-6}$ and 6.04 at $10^{-12}$, against 10.45 without; at $G=5$, 2.99 at 0.001; with equal speeds and $G=10$, 9.48 and 11.79 at the first two floors, against 16.18. Re-seeding the alternative at the rate $\phi_{\rm alt}\zeta(G-1)$ also bounds the dwell (3.28 at $G=10$, rule-making ten times slower, $\phi_{\rm alt}=0.001$; 4.12 at $10^{-6}$), but re-seeding is symmetric and re-seeds the practice wherever it has been removed. A share protected outside selection, $b\to b(1-\phi_{\rm alt})$ with $\phi_{\rm alt}=0.001$, leaves the dwell at 10.44.

*Scope.* One domain, quasi-static conversion and a fixed $G$. The dwell rises with $G$, so capacity that capture has built and that goes on converting, a sunk stock that decays slowly, keeps the effective gain, and the dwell, up. The starting share of 0.99 is a convention, which is why (3) matters. The field is cooperative, so these are lock-in times, not cycle times. With several domains the reform must also take down the core that sustains the domain (Proposition 4.11). Rule support is not observed directly, and near full share the depth cannot be read from the level. For real institutions the magnitudes are a conjecture (TESTS, Test D); the comparison the model supports, that a reform judged on share levels is withdrawn too early and that a floor under a recruitable alternative bounds the hold while a frozen order's hold grows with its age, is the claim of Section 11.4. The share-freezing pause has its own length, given with Proposition 4.12 in S4: a frozen share accumulates no lock-in.

### The ratchet

**Remark S11.3 (Graduated steps reach only as a ratchet) [thm].** *In the field model, a step that moves a captured domain's share, rule support or own conversion without bringing an inbound gain below one from the domains that remain captured is re-absorbed once the domain is re-seeded (Proposition 4.11), and a step that brings it below one must be held for the dwell of Proposition S11.2, or the state returns across the separatrix. A graduated sequence therefore crosses the separatrix only where some step crosses its own threshold and is held.* The trust the earlier steps build changes $\varpi$ in the game of Proposition 8.6; it changes the field only where it is spent on such a step. That is the part of graduated reciprocation (Osgood 1962) that survives capture: it answers a coordination failure, and it moves capture only where a step lands past a threshold and stays.

### Reachability as a capture basin

**Remark S11.4 (What a theorem of reachability would say) [def].** In the language of viability theory (Aubin 1991; Cardaliaguet, Quincampoix and Saint-Pierre 1999), a target is *reachable* from the present state if that state lies in the viable capture basin: the set from which some admissible control brings the system to the target while the state stays viable throughout; the states from which it is reached within a time $T$ form the capture basin of horizon $T$. For this paper the state is the field of Section 4 together with the host of Section 3, the admissible controls are the corrections the transition can apply (insulating rule-making, provision, sharing, custody), and the target is the configuration without capture, with the host above its floor. *Proved:* in the one-domain field model with insulation as the only control, the basin of absence is reached from the captured state iff insulation is held for the $T_{\min}$ of Proposition S11.2, which is therefore the minimum capture time; with a floor under a recruitable alternative as a second control, the minimum time is bounded whatever the age of capture (Proposition S11.2(4)). *Not proved:* the basin for several domains with all controls, with disturbances, and jointly with the host's floor. That is why Table 11.5 is a table of requirements with the support each has, and not a capture-basin proposition.

### The tracks and their time constants

Each track of Section 11.4 rests on a slow variable, and the order in which the tracks start follows their time constants.
- *Track 1, the shared account of hazards*, rests on shared understanding, which cannot run ahead of the evidence: for a class whose evidence arrives only after a manifestation window, no measurement shortens it (Proposition 5.12). Its instruments, evaluation suites and incident channels, are the fastest of the five to build, which is why agreement among independent measurements is the first indicator that can move.
- *Track 2, narrow commitments*, rests on human capacity $\mathcal M$, which decays at $\delta_h$ under delegation and recovers from it in $\varrho_h^{-1}\ln\big[(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)/(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M_{\min})\big]$ (Lemma 6.3); kept in parallel it removes the lock at a cost in strength, and where correction rests on it, it also sets how fast correction must grow (Proposition 4.6).
- *Track 3, correction and shared services*, rests on rule support in each corrector domain, with the dwell of Proposition S11.2. If $1/(\varepsilon d)$ is an institutional turnover of five to ten years, an assumption and not an estimate, the dwells computed there run from about a decade to more than a century [num: DW, with the assumed turnover]. That is why this track starts first and cannot wait for the trust of Track 4.
- *Track 4, delegation, access, custody and decision rights*, rests on the capability held in check, whose growth raises the net gain from exit (Proposition S8.5), against the clock $T_{\rm dec}$ to a decisive position (Remark S2.1); custody's installation and verification must take less than that time, so its preparation runs furthest ahead of need.
- *Track 5, broadening*, rests on the record of the arrangements under stress, which accrues only through cycles of stress and recovery, and on the self-models formed inside them, which change over generations, a time the field does not model [conj]. After a recovery, mobilization falls back and certificates can fail again (Remark 4.7), the regulatory cycle; openness of membership has to be built in from Track 1, since a bloc that closes early is hard to open later [conj].

### Table 11.5, row by row

*Existence* rests on Proposition 10.1, Proposition S10.3 and Theorem 10.4; *reachability* on Proposition 8.6 with Corollary 11.1, Proposition S8.1, Proposition 6.4 with $q_L=1$, Proposition 11.2 and Remark S11.3; *survival of the transient* on Theorem 10.4(1), Lemma 6.3, Theorem 5.13, Corollary 5.7 and the two clocks of Proposition 4.13; and *persistence* on Proposition S8.5, Proposition 4.10, Proposition S10.2, Proposition 4.11, Proposition S11.2 and Remark S9.1. The table claims no more than its rows. No result shows that the four can be met together, and graduated steps are shown to reach only as a ratchet (Remark S11.3); Remark S11.4 states what a proof of reachability would have to cover.

## S13. Interfaces between the models

The theory joins several models. Each join is an assumption, listed so that it can be checked separately.

| Interface | Between | Assumption | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Shared loss and irreversible hazard | The contest (Section 3.4) and survival (Section 5.3) | (hazard–damage link): hazard proportional to shared damage | Modelling premise; crux C1 |
| Coverage and residual risk | Hazardous variety and coverage, and survival | (residual floor) | AI-risk premise; crux C1 |
| Correction rate and coverage | $d$ in the field model and $U$ in Section 5.3 | A production relation $d\le F(U,\text{resources},\text{delay},\text{authority})$ | Open; neither bounds the other |
| Loop gain and breakout probability | $\Gamma$ (Section 2.2) and $p_d$ (Section 5.2) | $p_d=\Pr(T_{\rm dec}<\tau)$, with $T_{\rm dec}$ set by $\Gamma$ (Remark S2.1) | Linearized; the decisive level is crux C5, the detection lag C7 |
| Loop gain and working objectives | $\Gamma_\delta$ and $\theta$ | First order, others' strategies fixed (Proposition 3.9) | The full stochastic game is listed for outside review |
| Shared loss and coupling | $\ell$ and $\kappa$ | Distinct quantities, not counted twice | Maintained (Section 3.4) |
| Selection and strategy | Carrier selection and strategic actors | A selection result transfers only where the selected objective is shown to be the actors' | Rule of Section 3.1 |
| The outer training loop | Developers' relative payoff and trained systems' contribution to it | Level 2 selects on relative payoff, with $(A_\tau)$ | Where developers are selected otherwise, the result does not apply |
| Masking and alarms | Reports and decisions | Averaged, non-strategic reports (A5) | Remark 4.9 treats other rules |
| Capacity and the field | Selected capacity $q^\ast$ and the field model | Quasi-static capacity | Joint simulation not run (TESTS, Study 2) |
| Slow variables of mind | The selected self-model and the field model | A slow variable for the share of decision positions held by the winning model | Not modelled |
| The host | One host and many shared conditions | The forgiving logistic host | Threshold and vector hosts: S3, S10 |

## S14. Counterexamples that bound the claims

Each entry refutes a stronger version of a claim; the text states the weaker claim that survives.

| Counterexample | What it refutes | Where handled |
|---|---|---|
| Three of four actors widen their objective to $\theta=2.9$ and one keeps $\theta=-1$: total intensity falls from 1.0 to 0.015, but the holdout wins with probability 0.989 | That widening the self removes the problem; inclusion is not self-enforcing against one holdout | Coverage, not identity (Proposition 9.2); limits on what winning confers (Proposition 10.2) |
| With $D<C$, cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium survives $p_d=1$ | That decisiveness always shrinks enforcement to stage equilibria | Proposition 5.4(4) |
| The bound without the breakout term says "sustainable" when deviation pays (10,723 cases with $D>C$) | That the old bound is exact | Proposition 5.4(3) |
| Reversion to a race in which breakouts occur raises the punishment value above cooperation | That reverting to the race always punishes | Proposition 5.4(5) |
| Three players with role-differentiated punishment sustain cooperation among purely relative actors for $\delta\ge0.706$ | That relative actors can enforce nothing | Corollary 5.6 |
| Among three actors at $\theta=-1$, talk raises the receiver's value from 1/4 to 1/2 | That relative accounting makes all talk uninformative | Proposition 5.16(2) |
| Costs $(0.6,0.2,0)$ from one cap on loads $(1,0.6,0.2)$ | That a universal constraint is invisible to selection | Lemma 3.3(2) |
| Biased transmission sustains a costly trait at $\mu/c$ within one group | That nothing but anticipation can integrate one group | Lemma 3.11 |
| Zero liability keeps the host above its floor at $H=50$ although (reach) holds | That R1 is necessary at every horizon | Table 9.1, R1 |
| A row sum of two with a low initial state converges to no capture | That a full row sum below one is necessary | Proposition 4.10(2); R3b |
| A cap above equal-standing capacity, or below it without inflow not tied to rank, excludes laggards | That a point of enough always preserves standing | Proposition 2.3(2) |
| With per-decision costs the concentration measure is the Herfindahl index | That cultivated influence always tracks the third moment | Proposition 4.5(4) |
| A host normalized to the same peak regeneration regenerates faster above a third of capacity | That such a host is harsher | S3 |
| On a pointwise harsher threshold host the safeguards fail without a margin | That the sufficiency of the safeguards transfers to harsher hosts | Theorem 10.4(1) |
| Comparing after conditioning on the dissolution date fails when dissolution depends on the state | That the earlier proof of the fallback lemma was valid | Lemma 8.2 |
| A unilateral safeguard with negative net cost spreads without coordination | That every safeguard needs a common constraint | Proposition 6.7; crux C9 |
| Six checks with a common blind spot catch only $(1-\rho_{\rm cc})(1-0.1^6)$ | That certified row sums guarantee independent errors | Proposition 6.9 |
| In one common field the uninvadable intensity stays positive for every sharing fraction below one | That sharing reverses selection | Proposition 10.2(2) |
| In the continuous contest intensity is positive for every $\theta<n-1$ | That self-enforcing restraint exists below full inclusion in every game | Remark S5.1 |
| An industry institution after a serious nuclear accident; the Montreal Protocol | That restraint with unchanged motives is always fragile | Section 11.5 |
| Open models trailing closed ones by months; algorithmic efficiency diffusing | That decisive advantage is attainable now | Crux C5; enforced cooperation is at present available, and custody is a preparation |
| Verification faster than breakout | That enforcement fails whenever advantage is decisive | Crux C7 |
| A well-corrected continuation outperforming a fragile cooperative arrangement | That every cooperative arrangement beats regulated competition | Section 8.4 |
| A discounted criterion preferring a race that ends in catastrophe with certainty | That every criterion ranks the paths alike | Section 8.1; crux N1 |
| Mixed beliefs at a horizon of 1,000 periods making racing dominant | That the stag-hunt classification holds at every horizon | Proposition S8.1 |
| A corrector that cannot be understood lowering residual risk to a summable sequence | That correction beyond human understanding must fail | Proposition S6.4; crux N3 |
| After saturation, delegation with risk falling fast enough gives summable realized risk | That homeostasis is a permanent floor | Proposition S6.2(3) |
| Coverage growing in bursts with $\log U=o(t)$ | That sub-exponential coverage always makes a later sprint costlier | Proposition 5.14(4) |
| The host's minimum higher under relative accounting with war than under the isolated self in the frontier toy | That relative accounting is worse for the host at every moment | Remark 5.3 |
| The author's pilot experiment, in which one of four predictions failed | That the mechanism of Section 4 has been shown in experiment | Section 4.6; EV12 |

## S15. Numerics

`checks_v61.py` (pure Python, standard library only, about fifty seconds; log `checks_v61.log`) computes every number marked [num] in the core and this Supplement that V6.1 adds or restates. Each block names the stable keys it checks and its kind. *Independent* blocks decide the outcome by direct computation (value iteration, simulation, numerical best response, numerical Jacobian), never by the inequality under test; *identity* blocks evaluate an algebraic identity or a threshold formula against its own statement and guard only against slips; *derived* blocks compute numbers from formulas other blocks check.

| Block | Kind | Keys | What it computes |
|---|---|---|---|
| FB | independent | `bench` | spectral radius against $ab<d_ud_v$, 200,000 draws |
| LG | independent | `loopgain`, `compound`, `standprice` | numerical Jacobians against $\Gamma$ (4,000 systems); nonlinear deviations; present value of standing; two-period contest against $e(\theta_\Gamma)$ |
| CI | independent | `blind`, `nested` | unequal incidence; evasion; enforcement funded by compliers (tipping share); common liability; a safeguard with negative net cost |
| TC | identity | `twochan` | the accounting identity and its consequences |
| TH | identity | `twochan` | identifying $\theta$ with $\psi=1$ |
| SM | independent | `enough`, `wingives`, `club`, `twochan`, `fallback`, `faithful`, `delegatelock`, `topology`, `compound` | renege denominators; blocs; identification with $\psi<1$; coupled paths; cost exponents; one-or-both delegation; correlated errors; satiation; caps; growth variant |
| CB | independent | `club` | club against system intensity |
| PR | independent | `onegroup` | Price decomposition under five partitions; biased transmission; reversible and irreversible failure |
| CS | independent | `influence`, `corrrate` | size threshold, concentration, splitting factor, rate law for three elasticities, split delay, fixed cost |
| FA | independent | `require` | an invariant rectangle with row sum two |
| DW | independent | `pausefield` | dwell times, two phases, frozen-share thresholds |
| AC | independent | `alarm` | autocorrelation under static masking and under a filter with memory |
| RH | independent | `racehazard` | race hazard against best responses |
| FS | independent | `frontier` | the frontier toy |
| EC | independent | `enforce` | the enforcement condition against value iteration on 200,000 settings; the old bound by sign of $D-C$; breakouts during punishment |
| CR | independent | `relpunish` | mixed imitation ($n=2$); same-strategy punishers ($n\ge3$); the role-differentiated counterexample |
| BI | identity | `zonesillus` | the binary illustration at $D=C$ |
| WG | independent | `wingives` | what a decisive winner gives |
| TK | independent | `talk` | two-party zero-sum talk |
| T3 | independent | `talk` | talk among three or more |
| HO | independent | `safeguards`, `require` | the logistic transient; the margin on a threshold host; R1 at a finite horizon |
| SH | independent | `share` | pooling; sharing in one field; uninvadable intensity |
| PE | independent | `enough` | frozen capability against compounding |
| FD | independent | `fallback` | the fallback example |
| NB | derived | `standprice`, `enforce`, `zonesillus` | the $\theta_\Gamma$ table; the reduced-form scissors; $\bar p_d$ and its monotonicity; time to decisiveness |

Results carried unchanged from earlier versions keep their suites, which `python3 checks_v61.py --legacy` re-runs: `../v5/checks_v5.py` (blocks DL, OV, HO, SR, K7, N13, MM, CAP and others), `../v4/checks_v4.py`, `checks_v42.py` (derivative action), `checks_v44.py` (Branch II bargaining), `checks_v45.py` (common frontier, lead under diffusion), and `../v3/checks_v31.py`. Not yet computed: the joint simulation of capacity and the field (TESTS, Study 2), and the effect of an alternative lineage on dwell times. No suite certifies every proof, and none supplies evidence about the world.
